## "Prophecies of the Messiah" Miscellanies no. 1067.2 Misc. 1067.2 (ed.)

Jonathan Edwards61. ISAIAH 8:9-10, 14-17, AND CHAPTER 9:1-7. There are several passages in the 8th and 9th chapters of Isaiah, between those two great prophecies of the Messiah that have already been spoken of in Is. 7:14-15 and Is. 9:6-7, that are manifestly connected with those prophecies and relate to the times of the Messiah. I observed before, §19, that these two great prophecies seem to be connected one with the other, and that the discourse from one to the other seems to be continued. And here I would observe that nothing is more plain than that the 9th and 10th verses of ch. 8 are connected with the 14th verse of the foregoing chapter. The conclusion of the 10th verse shows [it]: "speak the word, and it shall not stand: *for God is with us*"; in the original it is *Ki Immanuel*, with a plain reference to, and connection with, ch. 7:14 foregoing. And the association of the enemies of the church in the 9th verse, is not chiefly that between Rezin and Pekah, but a much greater association in future ages, of which that was but a type. This is manifest, because here, those that are spoken of as joining together against the church of God are "*all those of far countries*." Such a combining and associating of the heathen and foreign enemies of the church against her in vast multitudes in the Messiah's days, and taking counsel and exerting themselves in vain, and being broken in pieces by the Messiah, is exactly agreeable to too many passages in formentioned prophecies to be now enumerated.

The 14th and 15th verses of this chapter are plainly connected with the 9th and 10th verses, as may be easily seen by the conjunctions that unite every sentence. So that he that it is said, v. 14, "shall be for a sanctuary," is the same "*God with us*," spoken of, v. 10; and those that he is a sanctuary to, are those that he is said to be with. 'Tis added, "but for a stone of stumbling and for a rock of offense to both the houses of Israel, for a gin and for a snare to the inhabitants of Jerusalem. And many among them shall stumble, and fall, and be broken, and be snared, and be taken." How agreeable is this to ch. 28:13 (These verses, as Stapferus, *Theolog. Polem*., Tom. III, p. 142, says, the Jews themselves interpret of the Messiah),[^001-note1] "the word of the Lord was unto them precept upon precept; . . . that they might go, and fall backward, and be broken, and snared, and taken," connected with vv. 16-17 following, "Therefore thus saith the Lord God, Behold, I lay in Zion for a foundation a stone, a tried stone, a precious corner stone, a sure foundation: he that believeth shall not make haste. . . . and the hail shall sweep away the refuge of lies," etc., with other prophecies of this book of the rejection of the greater part of the Jewish nation.

The [words of the] 17th verse, "I will wait upon the Lord, that hideth his face from the house of Jacob, and I will look for him," are very much like those words relating to the salvation of the Messiahbefore taken notice of,[^001-note2] Gen. 49:18, "I have waited for thy salvation, O Lord."

The beginning of the 9th chapter is plainly connected with the latter part of this 8th chapter, as appears by the conjunction, and is also most evidently connected with that great prophecy immediately following, 6th and 7th verses (before observed),[^001-note3] as a part of it.

The 2nd verse, "The people that walked in darkness have seen a great light: they that dwell in the land of the shadow of death, upon them hath the light shined," is agreeable to Is. 42:6-7, "I [. . .] will [. . .] give thee for a light of the Gentiles; to open the blind eyes, to bring out the prisoners from the prison, and them that sit in darkness out of the prison house"; Is. 49:9, "That thou mayest say [. . .] to them that are in darkness, Show yourselves"; ch. 42:16, "I will bring the blind by a way that they knew not; I will lead them in paths that they have not known: I will make darkness light before them"; Mic. 7:8, "when I fall, I shall arise; when I sit in darkness, the Lord shall be a light unto me"; Is. 50:10, "Who is among you that feareth the Lord, and obeyeth the voice of his servant, that walketh in darkness, and hath no light? let him trust in the name of the Lord, and stay upon his God"; Is. 60:1-2, "Arise, shine; for thy light is come, the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee. For, behold, the darkness shall cover the earth, and gross darkness the people: but the Lord shall arise upon thee, and his glory shall be seen upon thee."

What is said, v. 3, "Thou hast multiplied the nation, *to him* increased the joy" (as it is in the margin):[^001-note4] they joy before thee according to the joy in harvest, and as men rejoice when they divide the spoil," and also what is said of God's delivering his people from bondage to their enemies, v. 4, is agreeable to too many passages of forementioned prophecies to be enumerated.

*Corollary*. That there is so evident a connection of all the discourse from ch. 7:14-15 to ch. 9:6-7, with prophecies so much interspersed, so agreeable to other prophecies of the Messiah, is a great evidence that that prophecy, ch. 7:14, of a virgin's conceiving and bringing forth a son whose name shall be called Immanuel, has respect to the same child born and son given, whose name shall be called "Wonderful, . . . The mighty God, . . . the Prince of Peace," spoken of, ch. 9:6, as was before supposed, §19.

62. ISAIAH 19:18 TO THE END. In the 19th of Isaiah, from the 18th verse to the end, there seems to be a prophecy of the Messiahand his times. And that which is a sufficient evidence that the times of the Messiahare here spoken of, is that this is a prophecy of the calling of the Gentiles to "speak the language of Canaan, and swear to the Lord of hosts," and to offer sacrifice unto him, and pray to the Lord and know the Lord, and shall be acknowledged by God as his people and inheritance, and the work of his hands, and shall have the same privileges as the Jews. Nothing is more plain by preceding prophecies, than those are the great events of the Messiah's days, and peculiar to them.

'Tis here particularly foretold that Egypt and Assyria, two great nations that had been enemies and opponents of God's church, should be brought into his church, which is agreeable to many other prophecies of the Messiah's days, as Ps. 87:4, "I will make mention of Rahab and Babylon to them that know me: behold Philistia, and Tyre, and Ethiopia; this man was born there"; Ps. 68:31, "Princes shall come out of Egypt": Is. 45:14, "The labor of Egypt shall come over unto thee"; Is. 60:14, "The sons also of them that afflicted thee shall come bending unto thee; and all they that despised thee shall bow down themselves at the soles of thy feet; and they shall [call] thee, The city of the Lord, The Zion of the Holy One of Israel."

Here is foretold a change of the law, whereby other nations and lands shall have those privileges that by the law were peculiar to the land of Israel, so that there should be an altar in the land of Egypt, and they should offer sacrifices, and Egypt and Assyria should be holy lands as well as the land of Canaan. This is agreeable to other prophecies of the days of the Messiah, as Is. 66:21, "I will also take of them for priests and for Levites, saith the Lord." And God, in Is. 2 and Mic. 4, speaks of all nations going up to the house of the Lord, as the tribes of Israel were wont to go up.

That in the 23rd verse, "In that day shall there be an highway out of Egypt to Assyria," is agreeable to ch. 11:15-16, "And the Lord shall utterly destroy the tongue of the Egyptian sea; and with his mighty wind he shall shake his hand over the river, and shall smite it in the seven streams, and make men go over dryshod. And there shall be an highway for the remnant of his people, which shall be left, from Assyria; like as it was to Israel in the day that he came up out of the land of Egypt."

And there is all reason to think that the Savior and the great one spoken of, v. 20, whom God would send for the deliverance of the elect Egyptians from their oppressors, when they should cry to God, is the Messiah, who is everywhere spoken of as the great deliverer of his people in all nations from their oppressions. And it is very agreeable to the character everywhere given to him, that he is called a *great one*.

63. MALACHI 1:11. We have a plain and evident prophecy of the same times in Mal. 1:11, "For from the rising of the sun even unto the going down of the same my name shall be great among the Gentiles; and in every place incense shall be offered unto my name, and a pure offering: for my name shall be great among the heathen, saith the Lord of hosts." Here are the same distinguishing characters of a prophecy of the times of the Messiahthat were observed in the foregoing section. The calling of the Gentiles and changing of the law, bringing the Gentiles on a level with the Jews in privileges, their offering incense and a pure offering in other countries as well as Judea, is agreeable to the last-mentioned prophecy, that speaks of an altar to the Lord in the land of Egypt. That their offering should be pure is agreeable to Is. 60:7, "All the flocks of Kedar shall be gathered together unto thee, the rams of Nebaioth shall minister unto thee: they shall come up with acceptance on mine altar." And that God's name should be great among the Gentiles "from the rising of the sun even to the going down of the same," is agreeable to innumerable prophecies that speak of all nations being brought to know and serve God, and particularly Ps. 46:10, "I will [be] exalted among the heathen, I will be exalted through the earth"; and Ps. 113:3-4, "From the rising of the sun unto the going down of the same the Lord's name is to be praised. The Lord is high above all nations"; and Is. 59:19, "So shall they fear the name of the Lord from the west, and his glory from the rising of the sun."

64. ISAIAH 24, FROM VERSE 13, AND CHAPTERS 25, AND 26, AND 27, 28, 29, 30, 31, 32, 33, 34, 35. We have a continued prophecy of the times of the Messiah, from the 13th verse of the 24th chapter of Isaiah to the end of ch. 28, and from thence resumed from time to time with reproofs and threatenings interspersed to the enemies of the church and wicked professors, to the end of ch. 35. Here we have like prophecies of the great things that God in future ages would do for his church, introduced in like manner with the threatenings of approaching judgments on the land, and predictions of destruction to their oppressors, as other prophecies of the Messiahin this book that have been already taken notice of.

What is said, ch. 24:16, of God's people's rejoicing and singing praise in the utmost parts of the earth, and glorying in God, is agreeable to innumerable forecited prophecies.

Ch. 24:15, "glorify [. . .] the name of the Lord of God of Israel in the isles of the sea," don't agree to the time of the restoration of Israel from the Babylonish captivity. And the enlightening the isles and bringing them to give glory to God, is very often in other prophecies mentioned as a peculiar event of the times of the Messiah.

That terrible shaking of the earth and shaking of the nations, and punishing the kings and high ones of the earth, before the introduction of the glorious day of the church's peace and light, spoken of from v. 17, is agreeable to Hag. 2:6-7, 21-22, and Joel 3:16, and Ps. 46, and many others. Compare vv. 18-20, 23 with Is. 2:19, 21.

God's gloriously appearing in Mt. Zion and Jerusalem as the light of his church, far more glorious than the moon or sun [v. 23], is agreeable to Is. 2[:5], "O house of Israel, come, let us walk in the light of the Lord"; Is. 60:1-2, "Arise, shine," etc.; Mal. 4[:2], "to you that fear my name shall the Sun of righteousness"; and to that, Ps. 89:15,[^001-note5] "Blessed are the people that know the joyful sound: they shall walk, O Lord, in the light of thy countenance"; and Is. 60:19, "The sun shall no more be thy light by day; neither for brightness shall the moon give light unto thee: but the Lord shall be unto thee an everlasting light, and thy God thy glory."

Ch. 25:3, "Therefore shall the strong people glorify thee, the city of the terrible nations shall fear thee," is agreeable to ch. 19:23-25, and Ps. 87:4, and Ps. 22:29, and many other prophecies.

And this verse, together with the 6th and 7th verses, are agreeable to innumerable prophecies that speak of the calling of the Gentiles as a great event, peculiar to the Messiah's days.

I need say nothing of vv. 2 and 4 and 12, that speaks of the terrible destruction of the enemies and oppressors of the church.

And in v. 8, another great event often spoken of as peculiar to the times of the Messiahis spoken of, viz. the resurrection of the dead and the bestowment of eternal life: "He will swallow up death in victory." See Ps. 22:29, Job 19:25-27, Is. 66:14, Ezek. 37, and Hos. 13:14.

The latter part of v. 8, "and the Lord God will wipe away tears from all faces; and the rebuke of his people shall he take away from off all the earth: for the Lord hath spoken it," is agreeable to Is. 51:11, "Therefore the redeemed of the Lord shall return, and come to Zion with singing; and everlasting joy shall be upon their head: they shall obtain gladness and joy; and sorrow and mourning shall flee away"; and to Is. 40:1-2, "Comfort ye, comfort ye my people," etc.; and Is. 54:9, "this is as the waters of Noah unto me: . . . I have sworn I will not be wroth with thee, nor rebuke thee," and many others. V. 9, "Lo, this is our God; we have waited for him, and he will save us: this is the Lord; we have waited for him, we will be glad and rejoice in his salvation," is agreeable to Ps. 46:1, 5, 7, 11, and 48:14, and Is. 40:9. (See next page.)[^001-note6]

The treading down of Moab, v. 10, is agreeable to ch. 11:14 and Num. 24:17.

Ch. 26:1, "We have a strong city; salvation will God appoint for walls and for bulwarks," is agreeable to ch. 60:18, "Thou shalt call thy walls salvation"; and Ps. 48:12-14, "Walk about Zion, and go round about her: tell the towers thereof. Mark ye well her bulwarks, consider her palaces; that ye may tell it to the generation following. For this God is our God for ever and ever."

What is said of the bringing down of the high and lofty, ch. 26:5, is agreeable to Is. 2:11, etc.

What is said, v. 6, concerning God's people's treading down their proud enemies, is agreeable to Mic. 7:10, and Ps. 68:23, "That thy foot may be dipped in the blood of thine enemies"; Ps. 47:3.

Ch. 26, v. 2, "Open ye the gates, that the righteous nation which

keepeth the truth may enter in," is agreeable to Is. 4:3; Is. 52:1, "henceforth there shall no more come to thee the uncircumcised and the unclean"; and Is. 60:21, "Thy people also shall be all righteous: they shall inherit the land for ever, the branch of my planting, the work of my hands, that I may be glorified"; and Joel 3:17.

What is said, ch. 25:9, "this is our God; we have waited for him," and also ch. 26:8-9, "Yea, in the way of thy judgments have we waited for thee; the desire of our soul is to thy name, and to the remembrance of thee. With my soul have I desired thee in the night," is agreeable to Gen. 49:18, "I have waited for thy salvation, O Lord," and Is. 8:17. Ch. 26:4 is agreeable to Is. 45:17, 24-25.[^001-note7]

Ch. 26:17, etc., the deliverance of the church is described as brought forth by the church's being first as it were in travail, which is agreeable to Jer. 30:6, 9, and Mic. 5:3.

V. 19, the resurrection of the dead is again foretold, as in ch. 25:8. (See observation on this verse in the last page.)[^001-note8] And here God speaks of his dead body's rising, and his church reviving with him, which can be no other than the Messiah, and is agreeable to other prophecies of the Messiahalready mention[ed], as Is. 53[:10], "He shall see his seed, he shall prolong his days," with the context, that speaks of his death; and Ps. 16, latter end; Ps. 22:15 with the context.

Ch. 27:1, "In that day the Lord with his great and sore and strong sword shall punish leviathan the piercing serpent, even leviathan that crooked serpent; and he shall slay the dragon that is in the sea," is agreeable to the first prophecy of the Messiah, "*it shall bruise thy head*" [Gen. 3:15].

Vv. 2-3, "In that day sing ye unto her, A vineyard of red wine" (i. e. of the choicest wine; Prov. [23:31], "Look not on the wine when it is red"). "I the Lord do keep it; I will water it every moment," is agreeable to Gen 49:11, "Binding his foal to the vine, and his ass's colt unto the choice vine." And this verse, together with v. 6, "He shall cause them that come of Jacob to take root: Israel shall blossom and bud, and fill the face of the world with fruit," are agreeable to Hos. 14:5-7, "I will be as the dew unto Israel: he shall grow as the lily, and cast forth his roots as Lebanon. His branches shall spread, and his beauty shall be as the olive tree, and his smell as Lebanon. They that dwell under his shadow shall return; they shall revive as the corn, and grow as the vine: the scent thereof shall be as the wine of Lebanon"; and Ps 72:7, "In his days shall the righteous flourish," or "bud" or "branch forth," as the original word signifies; together with v. 16, "There shall be an handful of corn in the earth upon the top of the mountains; the fruit thereof shall shake like Lebanon: and they of the city shall flourish like grass of the earth."

V. 12, "And it shall come to pass in that day, that the Lord shall beat off from the channel of the river," etc., is agreeable to ch. 19:23 and ch. 11:15-16.

V. 13, "And it shall come in that day, that the great trumpet shall be blown, and they shall come which were ready to perish in the land of Assyria, and the outcasts in the land of Egypt, and shall worship the Lord in the holy mount at Jerusalem," is agreeable to Ps. 89[:15], "Blessed is the people that know the joyful sound."

Ch. 28:5-6, the words "*In that day*," with which these verses are introduced, refer to the same time spoken of in the same manner, ch. 27:13, "And it shall come to pass in that day, that the great trumpet shall be blown," etc.; and v. 14 and v. 1; and ch. 26:1; and ch. 25:9, and 24:21.

What is said, v. 5, "In that day shall the Lord of hosts be for a crown of glory, and for a diadem of beauty unto the residue of his people," is agreeable to Is. 60:19, "thy God thy glory."

And that, v. 6, "and for a spirit of judgment to him that sitteth in judgment, and for strength to them that turn the battle to the gate," is agreeable to ch. 11:2, "and the spirit of the Lord shall rest upon him, the spirit of wisdom and understanding, the spirit of counsel and of might."

In v. 9 and following verses, we have an account still of what shall be done on that day.

And[^001-note9] so still, v. 16. And here it is to be noted, that what we have now observed of the connection of this 16th verse with the preceding part of the prophecy contained in these chapters, as a part thereof, and there being in them so many things that make it evident that these are predictions of the times of the Messiah, is a great confirmation of what has before been supposed, §20, that the precious tried stone there spoken of is the Messiah; and the connection being considered, those other arguments there mentioned that that was the Messiah, is a mutual evidence that these chapters are a prophecy of the times of the Messiah.

V. 21, it is said, "the Lord shall rise up as in mount Perazim, he shall be wroth as in the valley of Gibeon." This refers to that in II Sam. 5:20, "And David came to Baal-perazim, and David smote them there, and said, The Lord hath broken forth upon mine enemies before me, as the breach of waters. Therefore he called the name of that place Baal-perazim"; and to the destruction of the Amorites by great hailstones, with the sun standing still in the valley of Gibeon, in Joshua's time. This therefore is agreeable to v. 2, "Behold, the Lord hath a mighty and strong one, which as a tempest of hail and a destroying storm, as a flood of mighty waters overflowing, shall cast down to the earth with the hand."

Ch. 29:6-7, etc., "Thou shalt be visited of the Lord of hosts with thunder, and with earthquake, and with great noise, with storm and tempest, and the flame of devouring fire," etc. See also Is. 30:27-28, 30, and 32:19. Those things that are said of the manner of the destruction of the enemies of the church, is an argument that what is ultimately and chiefly aimed at is not the destruction of Sennacherib's army: for that was not in such a manner, but was by a secret plague inflicted by an angel in the night, whereby they were struck dead at once, so that when they arose in the morning, behold, they were all dead corpses. But 'tis most manifest that punishment and destruction that is threatened to be brought upon the host of the high ones, the lofty city, etc., is not chiefly intended [of] the Assyrians that warred against Jerusalem in Hezekiah's days, by those events that are spoken of as being consequent on that destruction. Compare ch. 24:21 with v. 23; and ch. 25:2, 5 with vv. 6-8; and ch. 25:12 and 26:5-6, 14 with v. 19; and ch. 27:1, 10-11 with vv. 12-13.

The marvelous work and wonder spoken of, ch. 29:14, seems to be the work of redemption by the Messias, which is often in the prophecies of the Messiahspoken of as the greatest of all wonders, as Num. 23:23 and Mic. 7:15-16, II Sam. 7:19, Ps. 118:23, Joel 2:26; and is often spoken of in this book of Isaiah as an exceeding great wonder, ch. 64:4, and 52:15, and 7:14, and 9:6 and other places. It seems here to be foretold that work, being so wonderful, hence it should not be understood nor acknowledged by many of that people, vv. 9-14; which is agreeable to other passages in this book, as particularly ch. 52:14 and 53:1-2.

Ch. 29:17, "Is it not yet a very little while, and Lebanon shall be turned into a fruitful field" (see also ch. 32:15), "and the fruitful field shall be esteemed as a forest." There seems to be a prophecy of the rejection of the Jews, for their not knowing and acknowledging the marvelous work and wonder that God would do; which is agreeable to other prophecies in this book, as ch. 49:4-8, ch. 53 at the beginning with [ch.] 54 at the beginning, and 65:1-15 and 66:1-5.

V. 18, "the eyes of the blind shall see out of obscurity, and out of darkness" (see also ch. 32:3), is agreeable to ch. 42:7, "To open the blind eyes, to bring out the prisoners from the prison, and them that dwell in darkness out of the prison house."

V. 19, "The meek shall increase their joy in the Lord, and the poor among men shall rejoice in the Holy One of Israel," is agreeable to ch. 61:1, "the Lord hath anointed me to preach good tidings unto the meek"; Ps. 22:26, "The meek shall eat and be satisfied"; Ps. 68:10, "thou hast prepared of thy goodness for the poor"; Ps. 72:4, "He shall judge the poor of the people, he shall save the children of the needy"; and v. 13, "He shall spare the poor and needy," etc.; and Ps. 45:4, "ride prosperously because of truth, meekness and righteousness"; Is. 11:4, "with Righteousness shall he judge the poor," and other places.

V. 22, "Jacob shall not be ashamed, neither shall his face now wax pale," is agreeable to ch. 45:17, "But Israel shall be saved in the Lord with an everlasting salvation: ye shall not be ashamed nor confounded world without end"; and ch. 49:23, "kings shall be thy nursing fathers," etc., "for they shall not be ashamed that wait for me"; and 50:7, "I know that I shall not be ashamed"; and ch. 54:4, "Fear not; for thou shalt not be ashamed: neither be thou confounded; for thou shall not be put to shame."

Ch. 30:19, "thou shalt weep no more," is agreeable to ch. 25:8, "and the Lord will wipe away tears from off all faces"; and also to Is. 51:11, and 40:1-2 and 53:9.

What is said in the 22nd verse, of the people's forsaking idolatry, is agreeable to many other prophecies of the times of the Messiah.

What is said in the 23rd and 24th verses, of the great plenty that shall be, is agreeable also to many of the forementioned prophecies. See especially Ezek. 34:26-27.

V. 25, "And there shall be upon every high mountain, and every high hill, rivers and streams of waters," is agreeable to Is. 41:18, "I will open rivers in high places, and fountains in the midst of the valleys: I will make the wilderness a pool of water, and the dry land springs of water."

V. 26, "Moreover the light of the moon shall be as the light of the sun, and the light of the sun shall be sevenfold as the light of seven days, in the day that the Lord bindeth up the breach of his people, and healeth the stroke of their wound," is agreeable to Is. 60:1-2, 19-20 compared with ch. 24:23, and among other prophecies that speak of a great increase of light to the church in the days of the Messiah.

[That in] vv. 27-28 is agreeable to ch. 29:6-7. And God's tongue being as a devouring fire, and his breath as an overflowing stream to destroy his enemies, and v. 31, "through the voice of the Lord shall the Assyrian be beaten down, which smote with the rod," is agreeable to ch. 11:4, "and he shall smite the earth with the rod of his mouth, and with the breath of his lips shall he slay the wicked."

That in the 29th verse, "Ye shall have a song, as in the night when a holy solemnity is kept; and gladness of heart, as when one goeth with a pipe to come into the mountain of the Lord, to the mighty one of Israel," is agreeable to ch. 51:11, "Therefore the redeemed of the Lord shall return, and come with singing unto Zion; and everlasting joy shall be upon their head: they shall obtain gladness and joy; and sorrow and mourning shall flee away"; and ch. 52:8-9, Thy watchman shall lift up the voice; with the voice together shall they sing: for they shall see eye to eye, when the Lord shall bring again Zion. Break forth into joy, sing together, ye waste places of Jerusalem: for the Lord hath comforted his people, he hath redeemed Jerusalem," and many other parallel places.

V. 30, "And the Lord shall cause his glorious voice to be heard, and shall show the lighting down of his arm, with the indignation of his anger," is agreeable (see ch. 31:4) to ch. 42:13-14, "The Lord shall go forth as a mighty man, he shall stir up jealousy as a man of war: he shall cry, yea, roar; he shall prevail against his enemies. I have long time holden my peace; I have been still, and restrained myself: now will I cry like a travailing woman; I will destroy and devour at once"; and Joel 3:16, "The Lord also shall roar out of Zion, and utter his voice from Jerusalem; and the heavens and the earth shall shake: but the Lord will be the hope of his people, and the strength of the children of Israel," and many other places.

That expression in v. 30, "the Lord [. . .] shall show the lighting down of his arm," is agreeable to ch. 52:10, "The Lord hath made bare his holy arm in the eyes of the nations."

And that in the latter part of the verse, "with the flame of devouring fire, with scattering, and tempest and hailstones" (see also ch. 32:19), this is agreeable to vv. 27-28, and ch. 29:5-6, and ch. 28:2, 21. That, "with flame of devouring fire," is agreeable to Is. 66:15 and ch. 9:5.

That in v. 32, "and in battles of shaking will he fight with it" (see ch. 24:18-20), taken with that, v. 30, "the Lord shall cause his glorious voice to be heard," is agreeable to Joel 3:16, "The Lord shall roar out of Zion, and utter his voice from Jerusalem; and the heavens and the earth shall shake: but the Lord will be the hope of his people," etc.; and Hag. 2:21-22, "I will shake the heavens and the earth; and I will overthrow the throne of kingdoms, and I will destroy the strength of the kingdoms of the heathen; and I will overthrow the chariots, and those that ride in them," and many other places.

CHAPTER 31:4, "Like as a lion," etc., is agreeable to ch. 30:30, and ch. 42:13-14, and Joel 3:16.

That of the people's casting away their idols, v. 7, is agreeable to ch. 30:22, and many other prophecies of the times of the Messiah.

I have already shown that CHAPTER 32:1-2, etc., is a prophecy of the Messiah, §24;[^001-note10] and what now appears of its connection with the foregoing chapters, wherein are so many evident prophecies of the times of the Messiah, is a great confirmation of it. And this prophecy being so connected, is also a confirmation that those other things now observed in those chapters are predictions relating to the times of the Messiah.

Ch. 32:3, "And the eyes of them that see shall not be dim, and the ears of [them] that hear shall hearken," is agreeable to 29:18 and ch. 42:7.

V. 4, "The heart of the rash shall understand knowledge," is agreeable to ch. 29:24 (see ch. 33:6), "They also that erred in spirit shall come to understanding, and they that murmured shall learn doctrine."

And those things in these two verses, being evidently prophecies of what shall be in the days of the Messiah, by their connection with vv. 1-2 foregoing, and being plainly the same things that were spoken of before, ch. 29:18, 24, show that there also and consequently in that context, the Prophet is speaking of the times of the Messiah.

V. 15, "Until the spirit be poured from on high." This pouring out the spirit, is doubtless the same with that which we read of in the 2nd chapter of Joel, and [Is.] 44:3 and 59:21.

That in the latter part of v. 15, "and the wilderness be a fruitful field, and the fruitful field be counted for a forest," is the same with ch. 29:17. And this being so evidently what is to be in the time of the outpouring of the Spirit in the days of the Messiah, is an argument that those things that were observed in that chapter are also prophecies of the times of the Messiah.

Vv. 17-18 (see ch. 33:20) are agreeable to Hos. 2:17-18, Is. 26:1 and Is. 4:5-6 and ch. 11:5-9, Zech. 9:10, Ezek. 34:23-25, 28, and Joel 3:20, Jer. 30:9-10, "And they shall serve the Lord their God, and David their king, whom I will raise up unto them. Therefore fear thou not, O my servant Jacob, saith the Lord; neither be dismayed, O Israel: for, lo, I will save thee from afar, and thy seed from the land of their captivity; and Jacob shall return, and shall be in rest, and quiet, and none shall make him afraid," and innumerable other places.

V. 19, "When it shall hail, coming down on the forest; and the city shall be *utterly abased*" (as it is in the margin),[^001-note11] is agreeable to Joel [3:]16, "the heavens and the earth shall shake: but the Lord will be the hope of his people, and the strength of the children of Israel"; and Ps. 48. And whereas it is here represented that God's enemies shall be destroyed with hail, this is agreeable to ch. 24:21, and ch. 30:25, ch. 30:30 and ch. 29:6-7.

The latter part of the verse, "the city shall be utterly abased," is agreeable to ch. 25:12 and ch. 26:5.

CHAPTER 33:2, "O Lord, be gracious to us; we have waited for thee," is agreeable to ch. 25:9 and 26:8, Gen. 49:18.

V. 3, "At the noise of the tumult the people fled; at the lifting up of thyself the nations were scattered," is agreeable to Ps. 48:4-6, "For, lo, the kings were assembled, they passed by together. They saw it, and so they marveled; they were troubled, and hasted away. Fear took hold upon them, and pain, as of a woman in travail." "The nations were scattered": this is agreeable to ch. 30:28, "to sift the nations with a sieve of vanity."

V. 5, "The Lord is exalted; for he dwelleth on high: he hath filled Zion with judgment and righteousness," is agreeable to Ps. 97:8-9, "Zion heard, and was glad; and the daughters of Judah rejoiced because of thy judgments, O Lord. For thou, Lord, art high above all the earth: thou art exalted far above all gods," and many other passages in the 96th, 97th, 98th, 99th, and 100th Psalms, and many other places.

V. 6, "And wisdom and knowledge shall be the stability of thy times," i. e. of his times who is spoken of in the foregoing verse, that the Lord that should be exalted and should fill Zion with judgment and righteousness, i. e. of the Messiah's times. This is agreeable to ch. 32:3-4 and 29:24.

V. 17, "Thine eyes shall see the king in his beauty." The king here spoken of, is doubtless the same with that, ch. 32:1, that is the Messiah. This is agreeable to Ps. 45[:2], "Thou art fairer than the sons of men"; and Zech. 9:9, "Rejoice greatly, [. . .] behold, thy [King] cometh . . . having salvation," with vv. 16-17, "And the Lord their God shall save them in that day as the flock of his people: . . . For how great is his goodness, how great is his beauty"; and Is. 52:8, "for they shall see eye to eye, when the Lord shall bring again Zion"; and Ps. 89[:15], "Blessed are the people that know the joyful sound: they shall walk, O Lord, in the light of thy countenance."

V. 20, "Look upon Zion, the city of our solemnities: thine eyes shall see Jerusalem a quiet habitation, a tabernacle that shall not be taken down; not one of the stakes thereof shall ever be removed, neither shall any of the cords thereof be broken." This is agreeable to ch. 4:5-6, ch. 32:17-18 and ch. 26:1; and Hos. 2:17-18, Ps. 48:12-14 and ch. 11:5-8; and Zech. 9:10, Ezek. 34:23-25, 28; and Jer. 30:9-10, Amos 9:15, and many other places.

We have already observed[^001-note12] that there is great reason to suppose the king, judge and savior that shall [be] to his people as a place of "broad rivers and streams," spoken of, vv. 21-22, is doubtless the same with the king that shall rule in judgment, that should be as rivers of water in a dry place, spoken of, ch. 32:1-2; which king is spoken of again, v. 17.

That in v. 21, "wherein shall go no galley with oars, neither shall gallant ships pass thereby," and [v.] 23, "Thy tacklings are loosed," etc., is agreeable to Is. 2:12, 16, "The day of the Lord [. . .] shall be upon all the ships of Tarshish."

V. 22, we before observed,[^001-note13] is agreeable to many other prophecies.

V. 24, "the people that dwell therein shall be forgiven their iniquity," is agreeable to very many passages in forementioned prophecies, as Joel 3:21, Mic. 7:18.

CHAPTER 34. The great slaughter of God's enemies and the enemies of his church, spoken of in this chapter, is undoubtedly the same with that spoken of, ch. 30:25, "in the day of the great slaughter, when the towers fall"; and in ch. 26:21, "behold, the Lord cometh forth out of his place to punish the inhabitants of the earth for their iniquity: the earth also shall disclose her blood, and shall no more cover her slain"; and ch. 28:18-19, "the overflowing scourge shall pass through, then ye shall be trodden down by it. From the time that it goeth forth it shall take you: for morning by morning it shall pass over, by day and by night: and it shall be a vexation only to understand the report"; v. 21, "For the Lord shall rise up as in mount Perazim," etc. 'Tis manifestly the same that is spoken in the latter part of ch. 24. Here, vv. 1-2, the indignation of God is represented as being "upon all nations"; and there, vv. 17-18, it is represented as being upon all the inhabitants of the earth. Here, vv. 3-5, it is said, "the mountains shall be melted into their blood. And all the host of heaven shall be dissolved, and the heavens shall be rolled together as a scroll: and all their hosts shall fall down, as the leaf falleth off from the vine, and as a falling fig from the fig tree"; there, vv. 18-21, it is said, "the windows from on high are open, and the foundations of the earth do shake. The earth is utterly broken down, the earth is clean dissolved, the earth is moved exceedingly. The earth shall reel to and fro like a drunkard, and shall be removed like a cottage; and the transgression thereof shall be heavy upon it; and it shall fall, and not rise again. And it shall come to pass in that day, that the Lord shall punish the host of the high ones that are on high, and the kings of the earth that are upon the earth." And the destruction spoken of, 24th chapter, that is represented as being of "the treacherous dealers that have dealt very treacherously," v. 16, is doubtless the same with the destruction of those treacherous dealers that dealt treacherously, spoken of, ch. 33:1, 3-4, 7-13; and therefore the description of the destruction that we have of the enemies of God's people in this 34th chapter, being the same with that, ch. 24, is also the same with that, ch. 33. And what is said, ch. 34:9-10, of the everlasting, dreadful burning by which the enemies of the church shall be destroyed, is agreeable to what is said of the devouring, everlasting burnings threatened to the same, and to the sinner in Zion as having a share with them, ch. 33:10-14. And what is said here, vv. 9-10, is agreeable to ch. 30:33, "Tophet is ordained of old; for the king is prepared; he hath made it deep and large: the pile thereof is fire and much wood; the breath of the Lord, like a stream of brimstone, doth kindle it." See also v. 30 of the same chapter. And the sword spoken of, ch. 34:5-6, with which this execution should be done, is doubtless the same with that spoken of, ch. 31:8.

Therefore the description that we have in this 34th chapter of that terrible destruction of God's enemies, that is so often spoken of [in] those chapters, is a plain demonstration that the destruction of Sennacherib's army is not the thing mainly intended; but that greater destruction of the enemies of the church, that is so often in other prophecies spoken of as what should be in the days of the Messiah, to which the description here made is agreeable. Compare v. 4 with Hag. 2:6-7, 21-22, and Ps. 46, and this whole description, with Joel 3:9 to the end. Vv. 5-8 is agreeable to ch. 63:1-6. And vv. 9-10 is agreeable [to] Mal. 4:1, Ps. 21:8-10, and Is. 9:5. And the everlasting duration and unquenchableness of those burnings, is agreeable to Is. 66:24.

CHAPTER 35. The wilderness is represented as flourishing, and watered with springs and streams, as a thing that attends the great slaughter spoken of in the foregoing chapter; which is agreeable to ch. 30:25, "And there shall be upon every high mountain, and upon every high hill, rivers and streams of waters in the day of the great slaughter, when the towers fall." And this is another confirmation that the great slaughter spoken of, ch. 34, is the same with that, ch. 30:25.

Vv. 1 and 2, "The wilderness and the solitary place shall be glad for them; and the desert shall rejoice, and blossom as the rose. It shall blossom abundantly, and rejoice even with joy and singing: the glory of Lebanon shall be given unto it, the excellency of Carmel and Sharon," which is agreeable to ch. 32:15, "Until the spirit be poured upon us from on high, and the wilderness be a fruitful field, and the fruitful field be counted for a forest"; and ch. 29:17. To the same purpose, see also ch. 55:12-13 and 41:19.

V. 4, "Say to them that are of a fearful heart, Be strong, fear not: behold, your God will come with vengeance, even God with a recompense; he will come and save you," is agreeable to many prophecies of the Messiahin this book, and in the book of Psalms and other places.

Vv. 5[:6], "Then the eyes of the blind shall be opened, and the ears of the deaf shall be unstopped. Then shall the lame man leap as an hart, and the tongue of the dumb sing," is agreeable to ch. 32:3-4, and 29:18, and 42:7, 16, 18.

Vv. 6-7, "for in the wilderness shall waters break out, and streams in the desert. And the parched ground shall become a pool, and the thirsty land springs of water: in the habitation of dragons, where each lay, shall be grass with reeds and rushes." This is agreeable to ch. 32:2, and 41:18, and 43:18-19 and 44:3-4.

V. 8, "the unclean shall not pass over it." This is agreeable to Is. 52:1 and 60:21, and Joel 3:17.

V. 9, "No lion shall be there, neither shall ravenous beast go up thereon," is agreeable to ch. 11:9 and 65:25, and Ezek. 35:25 and Hos. 2:18.

V. 10, "And the ransomed of the Lord shall return, and come to Zion with songs and everlasting joy upon their heads: they shall obtain joy and gladness, and sorrow and sighing shall flee away," is almost the same words with ch. 51:11. And those same words, "sorrow and sighing shall flee away," is agreeable to ch. 25:8 and 30:19.

65. JEREMIAH 3:14 TO THE END. We have a prophecy of the times of the Messiahin Jer. 3:14 to the end. And there are these things contained in this prophecy that are sure notes of it. One is that in the 16th and 17th verses: it is supposed that the Law of Moses, it is set aside, and the ceremonial worship that that appointed in the tabernacle and temple is abolished. This, as I observed before, §62.

The second is the calling of the Gentiles. V. 17, "At that time they shall call Jerusalem the throne of the Lord; and all nations shall be gathered together unto it, to the name of the Lord, to Jerusalem: neither shall they walk any more after the imagination of their evil heart." It were needless here particularly to observe how exactly this is agreeable to many other prophecies of the Messiah.

The third note is the union of the house of Judah with the house of Israel, which is from time to time spoken of as an event to be brought to pass by the Messiah, as Is. 11:12-13 and Ezek. 37:15, etc., and Hos. 1:11, Ezek. 34:23.

V. 14, "I am married unto you." This is agreeable to Is. 54:5 and ch. 62:4-5, Zeph. 3:17, Jer. [2:]30-33, Hos. 2:14 to the end and Ps. 45, and other places.

This prophecy, vv. 14, 18, speaks of bringing back the children of Israel from[^001-note14] captivity. This is agreeable to too many other prophecies to be now enumerated.

V. 15, "I will give you pastors according to mine own heart, that shall feed you with knowledge and understanding." This is agreeable to ch. 23:4 and Is. 30:20.

V. 23, that speaks of God's evidently appearing as the only savior, is agreeable to many forementioned prophecies.

66. JEREMIAH 16:14 TO THE END. That Jer. 16:14 to the end is a prophecy of the salvation of the Messiah, is also evident by several sure notes.

First, here is a prophecy of the Gentiles rejecting the idolatry which they had continued in from generation to generation, and turning to the true God, vv. 19-21. This has been often observed to be a distinguishing note of the days of the Messiahin other prophecies.

Secondly, 'tis here foretold that God would do a far more wonderful work, bring to pass a much more glorious salvation for his people, than that of bringing of them out of the land of Egypt, and so much exceeding of it that the other shall be forgotten; which is a distinguishing note of the Messiah's days. See Is. 64:3-4 and Is. 43:16-18.

Thirdly, here is implied that the ceremonial law, appointing the Passover and other rites that were monuments of the great works God did in bringing the children of Israel from Egypt into[^001-note15] Canaan, should be abrogated; which is another sure note of the Messiah's times. See Jer. 3:16-17, Is. 19:19, 21, 23-25 and Is. 66:21.

This prophecy speaks of redeeming the children of Israel out of captivity, v. 15, which is abundantly spoken of in forementioned prophecies of the Messiah.

V. 15, "But, The Lord liveth, that brought up the children of Israel from the land of the north, [. . .] and I will bring them again into the land that I gave unto their fathers." This is agreeable to Jer. 3:18, "In those days the house of Judah shall walk with the house of Israel, and they shall come together out of the land of the north unto the land that I have given for an inheritance unto your fathers."

67. JEREMIAH 31 AND 32, VERSE 36 TO THE END. That the 31st chapter of Jeremiah is a prophecy of the times of the Messiah, is sufficiently evident by its connection with the preceding chapter, wherein is, as has been before observed, a noted plain prophecy of the Messiahunder the name of *David*, v. 9. This chapter is plainly a continuation of the prophecy, as appears by the first words in the chapter, "At the same [time], saith the Lord God," etc.

And the chapter itself contains many plain evidences of its being a prophecy of the times of the Messiah, in the things foretold in it, as particularly the uniting of all the families of Israel, v. 1. See §65 on Jer. 3:18. See Is. 11:12-13, Ezek. 37:15, etc., Hos. 1:11, Ezek. 34:24.

V. 7, "Sing with gladness for Jacob, and shout among the chief of the nations: publish ye, praise ye," is agreeable to numberless passages before observed.

V. 8, "Behold, I will bring them from the north country." See ch. 16:15 and 3:18.

V. 8, "and gather them from the coasts of the earth, and with them the blind and the lame, the woman with child and her that travaileth with child," together with v. 10, "will gather him, and keep him as a shepherd doth his flock." See Is. 35:5, and 32:3-4, and 29:18, and 42:7, 16, 18, and 40:11, and Ezek. 34:16.

V. 9, "They shall come with weeping, and with supplications," together with vv. 18-20, etc. See Jer 3:21-22.

V. 11 is agreeable to very many forementioned passages.

And so are vv. 12-14 (see Zech. 9:10, 16-17). The parallel places have been so often mentioned that 'tis needless to enumerate them again. V. 12, see Is. 27:13 and 58:11.

Vv. 18-20, see Jer. 3:21-25.

V. 27 is agreeable to Hos. 2:23.

What is said, vv. 31-32, of the new covenant that God would make with his people, diverse with that which he made with them when he brought 'em out of the land [of Egypt], implying that the church should receive a new constitution from God, the old being abolished, is by many forementioned prophecies a sure note of the Messiah's times, as Jer. 16:14-15, ch. 3:16-17, Is. 19:19-21, 23-25, and Is. 66:21.

V. 34, "And they shall teach no more every man his neighbor, and every man his brother, saying, Know the Lord: for they shall all know me, from the least of them even to the greatest of them, saith the Lord." This is agreeable to Is. 54:13, "And all thy children shall be taught of the Lord; and great shall be the peace of thy children"; and Is. 11:9, "for the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the Lord, even as the waters cover the seas," and many other places.

V. 34, "I will forgive their iniquity, and I will remember their sin no more," is agreeable to Is. 33:24, "the people that dwell therein shall be forgiven their iniquity"; and Jer. 33:8, "And I will cleanse them from all their iniquity, whereby they have sinned against me; and I will pardon all their iniquities, whereby they have sinned, and whereby they have transgressed against me." See also Mic. 7:18-19, Is. 43:25 and 44:22, Is. 53:6, 11, Is. 55:7-9.

Compare vv. 35-36 with Ps. 72:5, 7, and 89:2, 36, Is. 51:15, Is. 54:9 and Jer. 33:20-21; and v. 37 with ch. 33:20-21.

What is said, v. 40, of "the valley of dead bodies, and of the ashes" being "holy to the Lord," signifies the resurrection of the dead and the remarkable and wonderful cleansing of Jerusalem from all her filthiness, agreeable to many other prophecies of the Messiah.

The same times and the same things are manifestly spoken of again, CHAPTER 32, 36th verse to the end. The things are so exactly the same with the things spoken of in this chapter, and in many other prophecies, that a particular observation of them is needless.

68. EZEKIEL 16:53 TO THE END. In Ezek. 16:53 to the end is evidently a prophecy of things to be brought to pass in the times of the Messiah. Here are several sure notes of it, as particularly, first, a prophecy of the return of the Jews, which [is] in most of the prophecies of the Messiah.

And secondly, this return that is promised, is after a very long captivity and dispersion, even when Sodom and her daughters, whose desolation is spoken of as "a perpetual desolation," Zeph. 2:9, [are returned].[^001-note16] So the desolations of Israel that the Messiahshall restore, are spoken of as perpetual desolations, Is. 32:14-15, and "the desolations of many generations," Is. 61:4 and 58:12.

Thirdly, here is foretold the conversion of the Gentiles, and such as were the people of Sodom, and the neighbor cities thereof, vv. 55, 61.

And not only that they should be converted, but admitted to the same privileges with the Jews, and that, even the worst of the heathen, v. 61. This is agreeable to what is elsewhere said of Egypt and Assyria being put on a level with Israel as to spiritual privileges, Is. 19, and three last verses; and of their being an altar to the Lord in the land of Egypt, and the Egyptians offering sacrifices and oblations; and of God's making "mention of Rahab and Babylon to them that know him: behold Philistia, Tyre, and Ethiopia," etc. [Ps. 87:4]; and of God's taking of the Gentile nations to be priests and Levites, and of the flocks of Kedar and rams of Nebaioth coming up with acceptance on God's altar; and particularly what is said in the 56th of Isaiah, v. 3, "Neither let the son of the stranger, that hath joined himself to the Lord, speak, saying, The Lord hath utterly separated me from his people"; and vv. 6-8, "Also the sons of the stranger, that join themselves to the Lord, to serve him, and to love the name of the Lord, every one that keepeth the sabbath from polluting it, and taketh hold of my covenant; even them will I bring to my holy mountain, and make them joyful in my house of prayer: their burnt offerings and their sacrifices shall be accepted upon mine altar; for mine house shall be called an house of prayer for all people. The Lord God which gathereth the outcasts of Israel saith, Yet will I gather others to him, besides those that are gathered unto him." Agreeable to this is what is said of all nations going up to the house of the Lord, Is. 2[:3] and Mic. 4[:2].

The manner of expression, v. 61, "I will give them unto thee for daughters," implies what is agreeable to Is. 49:21-22, "Then thou shalt say in thine heart, Who hath begotten me these," etc. "Thus saith the Lord God, Behold, I will lift up my hand to the Gentiles, and set up my standard to the people: and they shall bring thy sons in their arms, and thy daughters shall be carried upon their shoulders"; and Is. 60:9, "Surely the isles shall wait for me, and the ships of Tarshish first, to bring thy sons from far"; and Ps. 87:4, "I will make mention of Rahab and Babylon to them that know me: behold Philistia, and Tyre, with Ethiopia; this man was born there."

V. 61, "I will give them unto thee for daughters, *but not by thy covenant*," is agreeable to Jer. 31:31-32, etc., "Behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that I will make a new covenant," etc.

V. 60, "and I will establish unto thee an everlasting covenant," and v. 62, "And I will establish my covenant with thee; and thou shalt know that I am the Lord," is agreeable to Jer. 31:31-34, "I will make a new covenant . . . not according [to] the covenant I made with their fathers . . . which my covenant they brake, . . . But this shall be the covenant that I will make with the house of Israel; After those days, I will put my law in their inward parts, and write it in their hearts; and will be their God, and they shall be my people. . . . and . . . they shall all know me, from the least of them unto the greatest of them"; and Jer. 32:40, "And I will make an everlasting covenant with them."

V. 61, "Then thou shalt remember thy ways, and be ashamed," and v. 63, "That thou mayest remember, and be confounded, and never open thy mouth any more because of thy shame, when I am pacified toward thee for all that thou hast done, saith the Lord God," is agreeable to Jer. 3:21, "A voice was heard upon the high places, weeping and supplications of the children of Israel: for they have perverted their way, and they have forgotten the Lord their God"; and vv. 24-25, "shame hath devoured the labor of our fathers from our youth; . . . We lie down in our shame, and our confusion covereth us: for we have sinned against the Lord our God," etc.; and Jer. 31:18-20, "I have surely heard Ephraim bemoaning himself . . . Surely after that I was turned, I repented; and after that I was instructed, I smote upon my thigh: I was ashamed, yea, even confounded. . . . Is Ephraim my dear son? is he a pleasant child?"

69. EZEKIEL 20:33-44. The evidence is exceeding clear that Ezek. 20:33-44, is a prophecy of the same times. For here, as commonly in forementioned prophecies of the Messiah, there is a prediction of God's gathering his people out of the countries wherein they had been scattered, vv. 34 and 41. And this work of God in thus gathering them, is compared to his bringing them up out of the land of Egypt, v. 36, agreeably to Hos. 2:15, Mic. 7:15, Num. 23:23, Is. 43:16-19, Jer. 16:14-15, Jer. 23:7-8, Is. 64:3-4, Is. 63:11-13.

Vv. 35-37, "And I will bring you into the wilderness of the people, and there will I plead with you face to face. Like as I pleaded with your fathers in the wilderness of the land of Egypt, so will I plead with you, saith the Lord God. And I will cause you to pass under the rod, and I will bring you into the bond of the covenant." This is very agreeable to Hos. 2:14-16, "Therefore, behold, I will allure her, and bring her into the wilderness, and speak comfortably unto her. And I will give her her vineyards from thence, and the valley of Achor for a door of hope: and she shall sing there as in the days of her youth, and as in the day when she came up out of the land of Egypt. And it shall be on that day, saith the Lord, that thou shalt call me *ISHI*."

That, v. 38, "And I will purge out from among you the rebels, and them that transgress against me: I will bring them forth out of the country where they sojourn, and they shall not enter into the land of Israel," is agreeable to Is. 4:3, Ezek. 34:17, Is. 26:2, Is. 52:1, Is. 60:21, Joel 3:17, Mal. 3:17-18 and 4:1, and many other places.

Vv. 38, 42 and 44, "and ye shall know that I am the Lord," is agreeable to ch. 16:62.

Vv. 40-41, "For in mine holy mountain, in the mountain of the height of Israel, saith the Lord God, there shall all the house of Israel, all of them in the land, serve me: . . . when I bring you out from the people, and gather you out of the countries wherein ye have been scattered." This is agreeable to Is. 27:13, "And it shall come to pass in that day, that the great trumpet shall be blown, and they shall come which were ready to perish in the land of Assyria, and the outcasts in the land of Egypt, and shall worship the Lord on the holy mount at Jerusalem"; and Jer. 31:11-12, "For the Lord hath redeemed Jacob, and ransomed him from the hand of him that was stronger than he. Therefore they shall come and sing in the height of Zion, and shall flow together to the goodness of the Lord." See also Is. 2:23 and Mic. 4:1-2, Is. 30:29 and Is. 25:6, Is. 56:7 and 57:13.

V. 43, "And there shall ye remember your ways, and all your doings wherein ye have been defiled; and ye shall loathe yourselves for all your evils that ye have committed." This is agreeable to Jer. 3:21, 24-25, and Jer. 31:18-20, and Ezek. 16:61, 63.

V. 44, "when I have wrought for my name's sake, not according to your wicked ways, nor according to your corrupt doings, O ye house of Israel, saith the Lord God." This is agreeable to Is. 48:9-11.

70. EZEKIEL, CHAPTERS 36 AND 37.[^001-note17] The 36th chapter of Ezekiel is a prophecy of the times of the Messiah; and so the following chapter, which we have shown before was a prophecy of those times, is only a continuation of it, or at least God, in giving Ezekiel the vision of the dry bones and making those revelations upon it, has reference to things he had revealed to him that are spoken of in this chapter.

Vv. 4 and 33-36, etc., is agreeable to what is often said in prophecies of the Messiah, concerning building the old wastes and restoring the desolations of many generations.

What is said, vv. 20-22, 32, of God's restoring the children of Israel from their captivity and desolation, not for the sake of their righteousness but only for his own "holy name's sake," is agreeable to ch. 20:44 and Is. 48:9-11.

And none can doubt, that compares this chapter with the following, but that the same returning the people into the land of Israel is spoken of in this chapter, that continues to be spoken of in the next. Compare vv. 12-14 with ch. 37:25. Compare v. 24 with ch. 37:12; and vv. 1, etc., with ch. 37:22; and v. 25 with ch. 37:23; vv. 10-11, 37-38 with ch. 37:26. Compare ch. 36:25 and ch. 37:23 with Is. 4:4 and Joel 3:21.

And none that compares vv. 24-28 with Jer. 32:37-41, can doubt but they are prophecies of the same things.

V. 27, "I will put my spirit within you," is agreeable to ch. 37:14, "And shall put my spirit within you, and ye shall live."

Vv. 29-30, "and I will call for the corn, and I will increase it, and lay no famine upon you. And I will multiply the fruit of the tree, and the increase of the field, that ye shall receive no more reproach of famine among the heathen," is agreeable to Jer. 31:12 and Amos 9:13-14, and many other prophecies, and ch. 34:27, 29, and Is. 30:23-24.

V. 31, "Then shalt ye remember your own evil ways, and your doings that were not good, and shall loathe yourselves in your own sight for your iniquities and for your abominations," is the same with ch. 16:61, 63, and 20:43, and Jer. 3:21, 24-25, and 31:18-20.

Vv. 12-14, "thou shalt no more henceforth bereave them of men," etc., is agreeable to the next chapter, vv. 25, etc., and Jer. 31:28, 40, Amos 9:15, and many other prophecies.

And if we compare vv. 12-14 with ch. 34:28; and vv. 8-9, 29-30 with[^001-note18] ch. 34:26-27, 29; and v. 15 with v. 29 of that chapter; and vv. 37-38 with vv. 11-12, etc., and v. 31 of the same, it will appear very manifest that this is a prophecy of the same times and things that we have in that 34th [chapter], that before has been observed[^001-note19] to contain so plain a prophecy of the Messiah, in vv. 23-24.

V. 15, "neither will I cause men to bear in thee the shame of the heathen any more, neither shalt thou bear the reproach of the people any more," is agreeable to Is. 52:1, "henceforth there shalt no more come unto thee the uncircumcised and the unclean"; and ch. 35:8, and Joel 3:17 and Ezek. 20:38.

It appears also by the manifest connection and identity of the things foretold in ch. 36, with those, ch. 34, that the intermediate ch. 35, concerning[^001-note20] the destruction of Idumea, is also to be referred to the same times; the destruction of the Edomites being here, as in many other prophecies of the Messiah's days, put for the destruction of the bitterest, most envious and cruel enemies of the church that were formerly brethren but are apostates. See observations on the prophecy of Obadiah.[^001-note21] This also will further appear by v. 14, that speaks of this being at a time when "the whole earth rejoiceth," that shows it plainly to be the times of the Messiah. This is also manifest by comparing ch. 36:2 with ch. 35:10, and ch. 36:3 with ch. 35, vv. 12-13. But especially is it evident by ch. 36, v. 5. See Is. 34:6 and Is. 63 at the beginning.

71. EZEKIEL, CHAPTERS 38 AND 39. Nothing can be more plain than that in the 38th and 39th chapters of Ezekiel, concerning Gog and Magog, God is showing Ezekiel what should happen when that return of the captivity of Israel should be brought to pass, that he had foreshown to him in the two foregoing chapters, and also in ch. 34; and consequently what should happen in the days of the Messiah. In vv. 8, 12, the restoration of Israel "to the mountains of Israel," that have long lain waste, are spoken of. See also ch. 39:28, which is evidently the same that had been spoken of, ch. 36:4, 19, 24, 28, 34-35, and ch. 37:12, 14, 21, 25.

Vv. 8, 11, "they shall dwell safely all of them." See ch. 34:25, 27-28.

V. 12, in those words, "to turn thine hand upon the desolate places that are now inhabited, and upon the people that are gathered out of the nations, which have gotten cattle and goods," there seems to be a reference to what had been said before, ch. 34:27 and ch. 36:11, 29-30.[^001-note22]

In these chapters, the kings and nations of the world are represented as gathered together against God's Israel from the four quarters of the earth, or the four winds of heaven: Magog and Meshech and Tubal, Gomer and "Togarmah of the north quarters," ch. 38, vv. 2, 6; Persia from the east, v. 5; Ethiopia or Cush, and Lybia or Phut, Sheba and Dedan from the southeast, south and southwest, vv. 5, 13; the merchants and "young lions" (i. e. princes) of Tarshish from the west, vv. 13 and ch. 39:6. And these are spoken of as having God's terrible judgments and wrath in a very sudden and awful manner executed upon them, to their great destruction, with very great manifestations of God's power and wrath, to the entirely defeating their design against God's people. And in ch. 38, v. 17, it is signified that the same thing had been spoken of before, from time to time, in their prophecies. And therefore this we may certainly infer, that in many of those places in the former prophets, where we have prophecies of a great combination and gathering of many nations to invade the church of God, and their being defeated of their design by God's suddenly and with great manifestations of his power and wrath confounding and destroying, and working glorious deliverance for his church: I say, we may infer that many such prophecies, in the former prophets, are the same thing that is the subject of these two chapters. But we have few or[^001-note23] no such prophecies in the former prophets, but what have been shown already to relate to the times of the Messiah, or others that might have been mentioned that are parallel to them.

And since we are told that the former prophets did actually often prophesy of the same thing, we may also judge, with good reason, when we find things foretold in other prophets agreeing in many things with what is here foretold, and in nothing inconsistent, that those are things that here the prophet Ezekiel (or the Spirit of God in him) has respect to here, ch. 38:17.

And there are some predictions in the former prophets, in their forementioned prophecies of the times of the Messiah, that remarkably agree with the prophecy in these two chapters, as particularly that in Is. 30:25, "in the day of the great slaughter, when the towers fall." This that is here described in these chapters of Ezekiel, may[^001-note24] undoubtedly be eminently called "the great slaughter" of the enemies of the church, there being no such a remarkable slaughter anywhere described in all the Old Testament; and the day of it spoken of as eminently the day of slaughter, that had been before spoken of by other prophets. Ezek. 39:8, "Behold it is come, it is done, saith the Lord God; this is the day whereof I have spoken."

And the description of that great slaughter in Isaiah agrees remarkably with this in Ezekiel. In [Is. 30,] vv. 27, 30, 33, and also ch. 29:6 and 33:10-12, it is represented as being with fire and brimstone, and therein agreeing with Ezek. 38:22, and 39:6 and v. 28. 'Tis represented as being an overflowing of waters, as also is the same slaughter and destruction, Is. 28:21 and ch. 24:18. And in Is. 30:28, it is said concerning that "great slaughter, when the towers fall," "there shall be a bridle in the chaws[^001-note25] of the people, causing them to err"; which is agreeable to Ezek. 38:4, "And I will turn thee back, and put hooks into thy chaws." And it is represented concerning that great slaughter in Isaiah, ch. 30:30,[^001-note26] that it should be "with the indignation of his anger," which is agreeable to Ezek. 38:18-19. 'Tis further represented in Isaiah that this great slaughter should be with scattering, and tempest and hailstones. So [in] ch. 28:21, 'tis represented as though it should be with great hailstones, such as was in the valley of Gibeon in Joshua's time; so Is. 28:2, Is. 32:19. This is agreeable to Ezek. 38:22. Again, it is represented of that great slaughter in Isaiah, that it should be with a great earthquake, and mighty and universal shaking. Ch. 30:32, "and in battles of shaking will he fight with it"; and, more fully and largely, ch. 24:18-21, which compares with Ezek. 38:19-21. And see the remarkable agreement, and also that description of the great slaughter, Is. 29:5-8, with the description in Ezekiel. God represents himself as greatly glorifying and magnifying himself by the great slaughter, in Ezekiel, in the sight of many nations, both God's own people and strangers that are far off, Ezek. 38:16, 23 and 39:21-22; which is very agreeable with the description of the great slaughter in Isaiah, ch. 33:10-13. By that great slaughter in Isaiah, it is represented as being upon a multitude of nations, yea, on the greater part of the nations of the world, as this is in Ezekiel, as Is. 24:17, 21, ch. 26:21, and 28:22, and 29:7-8, and 30:28 and 33:3-4. So that slaughter is represented as being on the kings and great ones of the earth, Is. 24:21; so here, ch. 38:18.[^001-note27] By that great slaughter in Isaiah, 'tis represented as though the enemies of the church were greatly disappointed, when they promised themselves success against them, and thought themselves sure of them, Is. 29:5-8. Compare this with Ezek. 38:11-16, etc. That great slaughter in Isaiah is represented as attended with the most remarkable pouring out of God's Spirit, Is. 32:15, 19; so this, Ezek. 39:29.

But especially is the description we have of that great slaughter in Isaiah, in the 34th chapter of his prophecy, agreeable to this in Ezekiel. There, as here, all nations are represented as gathered together. There, as here, God's great indignation and fury is represented as being upon all nations, and all their armies. There, as here, they are represented as being utterly destroyed and delivered to the slaughter. There, their dead carcasses are represented as lying in the open field, v. 3; as here, ch. 39:4-5 and vv. 17, etc. There, that great slaughter is represented as attended with a great shaking and commotion, v. 4; there, the land is represented as covered with their dead carcasses and with their blood, v. 3; so here, ch. 39. There, their stink is represented as coming out of their carcasses, vv. 3, 7; so here, Ezek. 39:11. There, the slaughter is represented as by the sword of the Lord; this is agreeable to Ezek. 38:21. There it is said, vv. 6-7, "The sword of the Lord is filled with blood, it is made fat with fatness, and with the blood of lambs and goats, and with the fat of the kidneys of rams: for the Lord hath a great sacrifice in Bozrah, and a great slaughter in the land of Idumea. And the unicorns shall come down with them, and the bullocks with the bulls; and their land shall be soaked with blood, and their dust made fat with fatness." How agreeable is this with Ezek. 39:17-20, "Assemble yourselves, and come; gather yourselves on every side to my sacrifice that I do sacrifice for you, even a great sacrifice upon the mountains of Israel, that ye may eat flesh and drink blood. Ye shall eat the flesh of the mighty, and drink the blood of the princes of the earth, of rams, and of lambs, and of goats, all of them fatlings of Bashan. And ye shall eat fat till ye be full, and drink blood till ye be drunken, of my sacrifice which I have sacrificed for you. Thus shall ye be filled at my table with horses and chariots, with mighty men, and with all men of war, saith the Lord God."

'Tis undoubtedly the same great sacrifice in Bozrah, and great slaughter, that in Is. 34:6-7 is represented as being in the land of Idumea and in Bozrah, and attended with such a mighty shedding of blood, that is spoken of again in the 63rd chapter of Isaiah. That is represented as attended with great and extraordinary manifestations of God's anger and fury, like this in Ezekiel, and that in Is. 34; and also in like manner a destruction in which God greatly manifies[^001-note28] himself and wonderfully shows his glory, and a destruction of the enemies of the church. There [Is. 63], v. 4, it is said, "For the day of vengeance is in mine heart, and the year of my redeemed is come," as it is also said, Is. 34:8, "For it is the day of the Lord's vengeance, and the year of recompenses for the controversy of Zion"; which is agreeable to Ezek. 39:8, "this is the day whereof I have spoken" (and see Obadiah, v. 15, §73), and 25, "Therefore [. . .] now will I bring again the captivity of Jacob, and have mercy upon the whole house of Israel, and will be jealous for my holy name."

And the same great vintage[^001-note29] of God's wrath, and treading of the enemies of the church in the winepress, with an abundant shedding of blood, so as to cause the fats to overflow, is spoken of, Joel 3:13 with the context. There, as here in Ezekiel, all nations that are without the church are represented as being gathered together at the time of the bringing again the captivity of God's people, and preparing war in vast multitudes to go up to invade Israel, vv. 1-2, 9-12, 14 (compare vv. 9-11 with Ezek. 38:7). There, as here in Ezekiel, the heathen nations are represented as being assembled and brought into the land of Israel, to be judged and dreadfully overthrown of God with remarkable manifestations of God's power, majesty and wrath, and with a great and mighty shaking of the world, and all for the defense of his people Israel, v. 16; and attended with a desolation of the land of Edom, v. 19, as in the 63rd of Isaiah; and attended with God's people's thenceforward dwelling safely forevermore, vv. 16, 20, agreeable to Ezek. 39:26, 29; and with a great plenty of all manner of blessings in the land of Israel, v. 18; and with the cleansing of God's people from all their filthiness, agreeable to those preceding chapters in Ezekiel, viz. chs. 36 and 37, which we have shown this prophecy is a continuation of.

The same great slaughter seems to be spoken of, Is. 66:14-16, "and the hand of the Lord shall be known towards his servants, and his indignation towards his enemies. For, behold, the Lord will come with fire, and with his chariots like a whirlwind, to render his anger with fury, and his rebuke with flames of fire. For by fire and by his sword will the Lord plead with all flesh: and the slain of the Lord shall be many."

And "the acceptable year of the Lord, and the day of vengeance of our God," spoken of, Is. 61:2, seems to be the same great day with that spoken of here, Ezek. 39:8, 25, and in Is. 63:4 and 34:8. The same day of God's anger seems to be spoken of, Ps. 21:9 with the context.

And the great gathering together of the nations to be judged and destroyed of God before his people, that is spoken of here in Ezekiel and in Joel 3, seems to be spoken of also in Is. 41:1-16, who are there represented as coming from the most distant parts of the earth, and renewing their strength and encouraging and strengthening one another, but being terribly destroyed before God's people, which destruction is followed with a glorious triumph and rejoicing of God's people. See especially vv. 15-16, "Behold I will make thee a new sharp threshing instrument having teeth: thou shalt thresh the mountains and beat them small, and shalt make the hills as chaff. Thou shalt fan them and the wind shall carry them away, and the whirlwind shall scatter them: and thou shalt rejoice in the Lord, and shalt glory in the Holy One of Israel." Which is very parallel with that in Mic. 4:11-13, which seems to be a prophecy of the same thing: "Now also many nations are gathered against thee, that say, Let her be defiled, and let our eye look upon Zion. But they know not the thoughts of the Lord, neither understand they his counsel: for he shall gather them as sheaves into the floor. Arise and thresh, O daughter of Zion: for I will make thine horn iron, and I will make thine hoofs brass: and thou shalt beat in pieces many people: and I will consecrate their gain unto the Lord, and their substance unto the Lord of the whole earth." See Ezek. 39:10.

And it seems still to be the same gathering together of the heathen and kings of the earth against the Messiah, and raging, and making great attempts, in vain and to no purpose but to be broken as with a rod of iron and dashed in pieces as a potter's vessel, spoken of, Ps. 2.

And whereas here in these chapters in Ezekiel there is a mighty commotion, gathering and raging of the heathen and kingdoms of the world against God's people, followed with their utter and terrible destruction, attended with a great and universal shaking of the world, and destroying and burning of the weapons of war, ch. 39:9-10, attended with the sure defense and safety and establishment of God's people; so the same thing seems to be foretold, Ps. 46, where all these things are expressed. Ps. 46:10 is agreeable to Ezek. 39:21.

The same assembling and discomfiture of the kings of the earth is spoken of again, Ps. 48:4-8.

And that terrible shaking of the earth that shall attend God's judging and rebuking the many nations that are enemies of Zion, spoken of in this prophecy of Ezekiel and the many parallel prophecies that have been spoken, seems [to] be that which is spoken of, Is. 2:4, 19, 21.

These seem to be some of those prophecies of the former prophets referred to, Ezek. 38:17 and 39:8, and all in forementioned prophecies of the Messiah's days.

I would now take notice of a notable prophecy of a prophet that succeeded Ezekiel, evidently parallel with this. Hag. 2:21-22, "I will shake the heavens and the earth; and I will overthrow the throne of kingdoms, and I will destroy the strength of the kingdoms of the heathen; and I will overthrow the chariots, and them that ride in them; and the horses and their riders shall come down, every one by the sword of his brother." See how exactly parallel this is with Ezek. 38:19-21, where is expressed first the universal shaking and then the overthrow of the strength of the kingdoms of the heathen, the mountains and every wall; and then it is added, v. 21, "every man's sword shall be against his brother."

72. DEUTERONOMY 32:18-43. The prophetical song of Moses, Deut. 32, from the 18th verse to the end, seems to relate chiefly to the times of the Messiah.

Vv. 18, 20, "Of the Rock that begat thee thou art unmindful. . . . they are a froward generation, children in whom there is no faith," is agreeable to many other prophecies that speak of the bigger part of the nation of the Jews as not acknowledging or believing in the Messiah, but very frowardly and perversely rejecting him; as that, Is. 53[:1], "Who hath believed our report? to whom hath the arm of the Lord been revealed?" There, as here, it is declared that they have no faith. The same Rock that we read of here, is that which we read of in Is. 28:16, that some should believe in: "Behold, I lay in Zion for a foundation a stone, a tried stone, a precious corner stone, a sure foundation: he that believeth shall not make haste." And, as has been observed before, it is the same stone that we read of, Is. 8:14-16, "And he shall be for a sanctuary but for a stone of stumbling and rock of offense to both the houses of Israel, for a gin and for a snare to the inhabitants of Jerusalem. And many among them shall stumble, and fall, and be broken, and snared, and taken. Bind up the testimony, seal the law among my disciples." And 'tis the same Rock that is spoken of, Ps. 118[:22], that is called "the stone which the builders refused." And the great perverseness of the people in rejecting the Messiah, is livelily set forth in Is. 65, from the beginning.

V. 21, "They have moved me to jealousy with that which is not a God; they have provoked me to anger with their vanities: I will move them to jealousy with those which are not a people; I will provoke them to anger with a foolish nation." Where it is foretold that the bigger part of the Jews should be rejected, and the Gentiles, a foolish nation, received in their room, as they had rejected God and had received a false god in his room. This is a plain prophecy of the calling of the Gentiles, and a sure note of a prophecy of the times of the Messiah. 'Tis also a plain prophecy of the rejecting of the Jews. [Compare] to Is. 65 and other places. 'Tis also here prophesied that this should stir up the jealousy and anger of the unbelieving Jews against believers that are received of God upon that account, which is agreeable to Is. 66:5, taken with the two foregoing verses and the foregoing chapter.

What is said of the manner of their punishment—"a fire is kindled in mine anger, it shall burn to the lowest hell," etc. [v. 22]--is agreeable to Is. 33:14, "The sinners in Zion are afraid; . . . Who among us can dwell with devouring fire," and Is. 66:24 taken with the 3rd, 4th, 5th, [and] 6th verses, and with the foregoing chapter, and Is. 26:11.

V. 23, "I will heap mischief upon them; I will spend mine arrows upon them," is agreeable to Is. 28:18-20.

Vv. 24-25, "They shall be burnt with hunger, and devoured with burning heat, and with bitter destruction: I will also send the teeth of beasts upon them, with the poison of serpents of the dust. The sword without, and terror within, shall destroy both the young man and the virgin, the suckling also with the man of gray hairs. I said, I would scatter them into corners, I would make the remembrance of them to cease from among men," is agreeable to Is. 65:12, etc., and Is. 33:17-19.

What is spoken of in the latter part of the song, is concerning the terrible vengeance of God upon the enemies of his people, when the time of their deliverance comes after their long-continued captivity and desolation, and which is succeeded by the rejoicing of the Gentiles with his people, and a time of universal peace and joy through the whole earth; and therefore relates to the same terrible destruction of the enemies of the church that is spoken of, Ezek. 38 and 39, and in other parallel places taken notice of, §71.

V. 39, "See now that I, even I, am he, and there is no god with me: I kill and I make alive; I wound, and I heal: neither is there any that can deliver out of my hand." This is very agreeable to the declarations God makes concerning himself in Isaiah, with which he introduces predictions of the great deliverance of his church and overthrow of his and their enemies in the days of the Messiah, chs. 40-48, especially ch. 40:12, etc., and vv. 21-22, etc.; and ch. 41:1, 4, 20-21, etc.; and 43:3, 11-15, 25-26; and 44:6-8, etc.; and 45:5-7, 12-13, 18, 20 to the end; and 46:4-5, etc.; and 48:1-8, 12-13, 15-17.

V. 41, "If I whet my glittering sword," is agreeable to Is. 27:1, "In that day the Lord with his sore and great and strong sword shall punish leviathan," etc.

V. 41, "and mine hand take hold on judgment; I will render vengeance to mine enemies, and will reward them that hate me"; and v. 42 from the beginning, "of revenges upon the enemy"; and v. 43, "will render vengeance to his adversaries, and will be merciful to his land, and to his people," is agreeable to Is. 34:8, "For it is the day of the Lord's vengeance, and the year of recompenses for the controversy of Zion"; and Is. 63:4, "For the day of vengeance is in mine heart, and the year of my redeemed is come"; and Is. 61:2, "To proclaim the acceptable year of the Lord, and the day of vengeance of our God"; and Ezek. 39:8, "Behold, it is come, it is done, saith the Lord God; this is the day whereof I have spoken," with v. 25, "Now will I bring again the captivity of Jacob, and have mercy upon the whole house of Israel, and will be jealous for my holy name."
