V. 42, "I will make mine arrows drunk with blood, and my sword shall devour flesh; and that with the blood of the slain and of the captives." This is agreeable to Is. 34:5-6, "For my sword shall be bathed in heaven: behold, it shall come down upon Idumea, and upon the people of my curse, to judgment. The sword of the Lord is filled with blood, it is made fat with fatness, and with the blood of lambs and goats, with the fat of the kidneys of rams: for the Lord hath a sacrifice in Bozrah, and a great slaughter in the land of Idumea"; v. 7, "their land shall be soaked with blood"; v. 3, "the mountains shall be melted with their blood." See also ch. 63 and 66:16, and Ezek. 38:21-22 and [ch.] 39 throughout, and Joel 3:19.

V. 43, "for he will avenge the blood of his servants," is agreeable to Is. 26:21, "For, behold, the Lord cometh out of his place to punish the inhabitants of the earth for their iniquity: the earth also shall disclose her blood, and shall no more cover her slain."

V. 43, "Rejoice, O ye nations, with his people: he [. . .] will be merciful to his land, and to his people," is agreeable to what follows that description of the great slaughter, Is. 34, [and] in ch. 35. See also Is. 24 at the latter end; and Is. 25:5-6, and v. 12, with the beginning of the following chapter; and Is. 30:25 to the end.

73. THE PROPHECY OF OBADIAH. 'Tis apparent and obvious that the short prophecy of Obadiah does, part of [it], respect times of the Messiah, because it speaks of the time wherein the captivity of God's people should be returned, v. 20; and the day wherein God would revenge the quarrel of his church upon all the heathen, vv. 15-16; and the time wherein God would set up his kingdom upon earth, v. 21; and there should be deliverance and salvation in Zion, v. 17; and God's Israel should dreadfully consume their enemies, v. 18; and should possess the heathen lands, vv. 19-20.

And [if] we carefully consider this prophecy, it will be found that the whole of it chiefly respects the times of the Messiah. Though it seems to be mainly against Edom, yet it seems evident that by Edom here is mainly intended the same thing as in many forementioned prophecies of the church, viz. the chief of the enemies of the church that formerly had been brethren, with whom other enemies of the church shall be in combination, and in whose destruction they shall be involved, as in Is. 34 and 63, and Ezek. 35. The same envy and hatred against Israel is here spoken of, vv. 10, etc., that is spoken of, Ezek. 35:5, 11, 15; and the same thing is here threatened to Edom, v. 15, "as thou hast done, so shall it be done unto thee," [that] is threatened, Ezek. 35:15, "As thou didst rejoice at the inheritance of the house of Israel, because it was desolate, so will I do unto thee: thou shalt be desolate, O mount Seir, and all Idumea."

Here the destruction of Edom is spoken of as attended with the destruction of all the heathen that were enemies of Israel, vv. 15-16. So it is, Ezek. 36:5, "Surely in the fire of my jealousy have I spoken against the residue [of the heathen], and against all Idumea." So Is. 34, vv. 1-2, "Come near, ye nations, . . . For the indignation of the Lord is upon all nations, and his fury upon all their armies: he hath utterly destroyed them, he hath delivered them to the slaughter"; vv. 5-6, "For my sword shall be bathed in heaven: behold, it shall come down upon Idumea, . . . the Lord hath a sacrifice in Bozrah, and a great slaughter in the land of Idumea." So Is. 63:1, "Who is this that cometh from Edom," etc.; v. 6, "I will tread down the people in mine anger."

V. 15, "the day of the Lord is upon all the heathen." This is agreeable to Ezek. 39:8, "this is the day whereof I have spoken," and Is. 34:8 and 63:4 and 61:2.

V. 17, "But upon mount Zion shall be deliverance." This is agreeable to Is. 63:4, "the year of my redeemed is come." This deliverance is that great salvation that the Psalmist wishes were come out of Zion, and that is so often mentioned in other prophecies. See §51.

V. 17, "and the house of Jacob shall possess their possessions." This is agreeable to what follows the prophecy of the destruction of Idumea in Ezek. 36.

V. 18, "And the house of Jacob shall be a fire, and the house of Joseph a flame, and the house of Esau for stubble, and they shall kindle in them, and devour them; and there shall not be any remaining of the house of Esau." This is agreeable to Is. 41:15-16, "I will make thee a new sharp threshing instrument," etc., and Mic. 4:11-13, "Arise, thresh, O daughter of Zion: I will make thine horn iron, and thy hoofs brass," etc.

V. 19, "they of the south shall possess the mount of Esau," is agreeable to Num. 24:18, "Edom shall be a possession, Seir also shall be a possession." See also Amos 9:12, "that they may possess the remnant of Edom."

V. 21, "And saviors shall come to judge . . . and the kingdom shall be the Lord's," is agreeable to Hos. 13:10 and the many other prophecies already mentioned that speak of God's being king, and his reigning, etc., in the time of the Messiah.

74. EZEKIEL, CHAPTERS 40, AND 41, AND 42, AND 43, AND 44, AND 45, AND 46, AND 47, AND 48, ESPECIALLY 43:1-4, AND 44:1-3, AND 45:13 TO THE END, AND 46:1-11. The nine last chapters of Ezekiel are evidently a description of the state of God's Israel after that restoration from captivity, that resurrection as it were from the dead, and that re-establishment of them in their own land, and that great destruction of their enemies that had been spoken of in the six preceding chapters: and consequently is a prophecy of the times of the Messiah. Here we have a particular account of the land resettled, and how it should be settled and divided, and of the city rebuilt, and of the ordinances and worship of God restored. Here is a particular account of God's return to the people, and to the sanctuary, to dwell amongst them, ch. 43:1-7, of whose departure we have an account, ch. 9:3, and 10:18 to the end, and 11:22-23. And here we have a particular account of that which had been promised before, ch. 37:26-28, "I [. . .] will set my sanctuary in the midst of them . . . My tabernacle also shall be with them: . . . And the heathen shall know that I the Lord do sanctify Israel, when my sanctuary shall be in the midst of them." Here we have a particular description of that sanctuary of God.

Ch. 43:7, "And he said unto me, Son of man, the place of my throne, and the place of the soles of my feet, where I will dwell in the midst of the children of Israel for ever": and v. 9, "and I will dwell in the midst of them for ever"; and ch. 48:35, "and the name of the city from that day shall be, The Lord is there." These are evidently predictions of the same things that had been foretold, ch. 34:30, "Thus shall they know that I the Lord their God am with them, and that they, even the house of Israel, are my people, saith the Lord God"; and ch. 37:26-28, "and I [. . .] will set my sanctuary in the midst of them for evermore. My tabernacle also shall be with them: yea, I will be their God, and they shall be my people. And the heathen shall know that I the Lord do sanctify Israel, when my sanctuary shall be in the midst of them for evermore"; and ch. 39:29, "Neither will I hide my face any more from them: for I have poured out my spirit upon the house of Israel, saith the Lord God."

And that, ch. 43:7, "and my holy name shall the house of Israel no more defile, neither they nor their kings, by their whoredom, nor by the carcasses of their kings in their high places," is plainly a prophecy of the same thing with that foretold, ch. 37[:23], "Neither shall they defile themselves any more with their idols, nor with their detestable things, nor with any of their transgressions: but I will save them out of all their dwelling places, wherein they have sinned, and will cleanse them"; and ch. 36:25, "Then will I sprinkle clean water upon you, and ye shall be clean from all your filthiness, and from all your idols will I cleanse you."

That which is prophesied of, ch. 37:15, etc., and 34:23, viz. that the whole house of Israel should be one nation and should be no more two kingdoms, and should be united under one prince, we have here in these chapters a particular account, ch. 48.

And this prophecy in these nine last chapters of Ezekiel, is agreeable to other prophecies of the times of the Messiah, in those particulars that have been already mentioned, wherein it agrees with passages in the six preceding chapters (which may be seen in places referred to in past observations on those passages), and also in other things. As particularly here, it is foretold that there should be a new measuring out and dividing of the land, and a new laying out of the plan of the city, which is agreeable to Jer. 31:38-40, and Obadiah, vv. 18-20. And who can doubt whether God's return to his temple, here spoken of, ch. 43:1-7, be the same that is spoken of, Mal. 3:1, "the Lord whom ye seek, shall suddenly come to his temple."

And what is said, ch. 47, of the living waters that proceed from the house of the Lord and go forth eastward towards the east country, is agreeable to Joel 3:18, "and a fountain shall come forth of the house of the Lord, and shall water the valley of Shittim," which Shittim was eastward from Jerusalem, on the other side Jordan. Ch. 47:8, 'tis said those waters "go down into the desert," which is agreeable to those many passages that have been already mentioned in other prophecies of the Messiah's days, of fountains, streams and rivers in the desert, wilderness and dry places. And what is said, v. 5, "Afterward he measured a thousand, and it was a river that I could not pass over: for the waters were risen, waters to swim in, a river that could not be passed over." This is agreeable to Is. 33:21, "there the glorious Lord will be to us a place of broad rivers and streams." What is said of the healing virtue of those waters, and of the leaves of the tree that grow on the banks of the river, they should be for medicine [v. 12], is agreeable to Jer. 33:6, "Behold, I will bring it health and cure, and I will cure them, and will reveal unto them the abundance of peace and truth." So what is said, vv. 8-9, of the waters going into the [sea] and healing of the waters of the great ocean, and healing and giving life to everything, is very agreeable to what is elsewhere said of turning the barren land in[to] a fruitful field, and making the wilderness to bud and blossom as the rose; and the Messiah's giving light and life and salvation to all nations; and changing the nature even of those things that are poisonous, that they shall become harmless; and restoring all things, making a new heavens and new earth, and the like.

Here in these chapters it is foretold that the Law of Moses, as to what concerns the Mosaic constitution of worship, shall be set aside and give place to a new constitution (see "Miscellanies," no. 1034);[^001-note30] which has often been observed to be a sure note of a prophecy of the times of the Messiah.

Again, it is here foretold that the Gentiles that shall turn to God shall be admitted to equal privileges with the Jews, insomuch that they shall have a lot with them in the division of the land of Israel. Ezek. 47:21-23, "So shall ye divide this land unto you according to the tribes of Israel. And it shall come to pass, that ye shall divide it by lot for an inheritance unto you, and to the strangers that sojourn among you, which shall beget children among you: and they shall be unto you as born in the country among the children of Israel; they shall have inheritance with you among the tribes of Israel. And it shall come to pass, that in what tribe the stranger sojourneth, there shall ye give him his inheritance, saith the Lord God." This has often been observed to be a plain and certain note of a prophecy of the times of the Messiah. See Ezek. 16:61, and see many other places mentioned, §68.

Ch. 44:9, "Thus saith the Lord God; No stranger, uncircumcised in heart, nor uncircumcised in flesh, shall enter into my sanctuary, of any stranger that is among the children of Israel," is agreeable to Is. 52:1, "henceforth there shall no more come into thee the uncircumcised and the unclean"; and Joel 3:17, "then shall Jerusalem be holy, and there shall no stranger pass through her any more"; also Is. 26:2, and 34:8, and 60:21 and 4:3.

Ch. 48:35, "and the name of the city from that day shall be, The Lord is there." This is agreeable to Is. 7[:14], his name shall be called Immanuel; and Is. 8:10, "God is with us"; and Ps. 46:4-5, "the city of God, the holy place of the tabernacle of the most High. God is in the midst of her"; and so vv. 7 and 11, "The Lord of hosts is with us"; and [Ps.] 48:2-3, "Beautiful for situation, the joy of the whole earth, is mount Zion on the sides of the north, the city of the great King. God is known in her palaces for a refuge"; v. 14, "For this God is our God for ever and ever"; Is. 25:9, "And it shall be said in that day, Lo, this is our God; we have waited for him, and he will save us: this is the Lord; we have waited for him, we will be glad and rejoice in his salvation"; and Jer. 33:16, "in those days shall Judah be saved, and Jerusalem shall dwell safely: and this is the name wherewith she shall be called, The Lord our righteousness"; and many other places.

Ch. 43:5, "So the spirit took me up, and brought me into the inner court; and, behold, the glory of the Lord filled the house." This is agreeable to Hag. 2:7, "the desire of all nations shall come: and I will fill this house with glory, saith the Lord of hosts."

"Á Lapide tells us, that many Rabbins and Jews refer this temple and city to the Messiah, expecting that he should build them; and, because this third temple and new city are not yet built, they think the Messiah is not yet come. This argument was objected by the Jews to Galatinus." Greenhill on Ezekiel, last vol., p. 279.[^001-note31]

As to the prince spoken of in the 44th and following chapters, although the whole of that authority and rule or power of dominion that God should set in his Israel, may be included in what is designed to be here represented by the prince, yet there are several things that make it manifest that the Messiah, the head and source of all that authority and power, is especially intended:

1. The prophecies in the preceding chapters, of the same times and this same state of God's people, which the Spirit of God continues to describe in those last nine chapters, do in effect declare plainly that this prince over God's people is the Messiah; as ch. 37:24-25, "And David my servant shall be king over them; and they all shall have one shepherd: they shall also walk in my statutes, and observe my judgments, and do them. And they shall dwell in the land that I have given unto Jacob my servant, wherein your fathers have dwelt; and they shall dwell therein, even they, and their children, [. . .] for ever: and my servant David shall be their prince for ever." Here we are expressly told who their prince should be, and we are told they should have but one, v. 24, "*and they all shall have one shepherd*." And we are not only told that there should be no other that should be contemporary with him, or that he only should be their prince for a time; but that they should never have any other prince, or that he only should be their prince forever, v. 25, "and my servant David shall be their prince for ever." This, as the context shows, shall be when God has brought 'em out of captivity and resettled 'em in their own land, and united the two kingdoms of Judah and Israel, and God has purged them from their idols and detestable things, and set his sanctuary in the midst of them; which is the time and state described in those last chapters. Therefore it follows that this prince here spoken of, is that *David* there spoken of, who is also spoken of under the same appellation of THEIR PRINCE, ch. 34:23-24. "And I will set up one shepherd over them" (one and no more), "even my servant David; he shall feed them, and he shall be their shepherd. And I the Lord will be their God, and my servant David A PRINCE among them; I the Lord have spoken it."

2. This prince was to do the part of an high priest among the people. He was to offer all the sacrifices of the nation. They were to bring their sacrifices to him, ch. 45:13-16. They were to go through his hands, and by him to be offered to God: their daily and their weekly and their monthly sacrifices, and their sacrifices on all their feasts and special solemnities, ch. 45:17. "And it shall be the prince's part to give burnt offerings, and meat offerings, and drink offerings in the new moons, in the sabbaths, in all solemnities of the house of Israel. He shall prepare the sin offering and the meat offering, and the burnt offering, and the peace[^001-note32] offering, to make reconciliation for the house of Israel." See also v. 21 of the same chapter to the end, and ch. 46:4-7. These offerings of the sabbaths and new moons that the prince was to offer, were the weekly and monthly sacrifices of the nation, instead of those appointed, Num. 28:9, etc. This shows plainly who this prince was, viz. the same with that David spoken of, chs. 34 and 37: the antitype of David the son of Jesse that was girded with a linen ephod, the king over Israel; and of him spoken of in the 110th Psalm [v. 4], to whom the Lord swore, "Thou art a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek," i. e. both a king and a priest; and the same with the man whose name is the Branch, spoken of, Zech. 6[:12-13], who should "sit and rule upon his throne, and be a priest upon his throne."

3. It is said, ch. 44:2, of the eastern gate of the temple, "This gate shall be shut, it shall not be opened, and no man shall enter in by it; because the Lord, the God of Israel, hath entered by it, therefore it shall be shut." And yet it is said in the next verse, it shall be for "the prince, he shall sit in it to eat bread before the Lord; he shall enter by the way of the porch of that gate, and shall go out by the way of the same." And so afterwards we have accounts of the prince's entering on the sabbaths and new moons by that gate, ch. 46. This argues that this prince was more than a mere man, one on whom divine honor was conferred; as 'tis said, Zech. 6[:13], "he shall bear the glory." He was to enter into God's temple at that gate at which Jehovah had entered, and which none other man entered in at but he, and which it was not lawful for any man to enter in by, because Jehovah the God of Israel had entered by it. This shows that he was not a mere man, infinitely distant from God, a worm or grasshopper, as all the kings of the earth are before God, who "accepteth not the person of princes, nor regardeth the rich more than the poor, for they are all the work of his hands" [Job 34:19].

It is no solid objection against the Messiah's being chiefly intended by this prince, because princes are spoken of in the plural number, ch. 45:8-9. For this is evidently paralleled in other prophecies of the Messiah, as Is. 32:1, "Behold, a king shall reign in righteousness, and princes shall rule in judgment." So in Jer. 23:4 'tis said, "I will set up shepherds" (in the plural number) "over them which shall feed them: and they shall fear no more, nor be dismayed, neither shall they be lacking, saith the Lord"; and then in the next verse it follows, "Behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that I will raise up unto David a righteous Branch," etc.; and of this Branch of David it is said, Ezek. 34, and [vv.] 23-24, and 37:24-25, that he only shall be their shepherd. So in Obadiah's prophecy of the Messiah, v. 21, it is said, "saviors shall come upon mount Zion to judge the mount of Esau." In all these, there may either be a kind of *Enaltage* *numeri*,[^001-note33] prophetically (and so very figuratively) representing the Messiah by such a succession of princes or judges or captains as were a type of him in Israel. Or, as I said before, this prince in the prophecy may represent the rule, authority and dominion that God will set in his Israel, which, though it chiefly respects the Messiah, the head and source of it all, yet may include subordinate rulers, or all such as do partake of any part of his dominion.

"Rabbi David, in his commentary, saith that the Hebrews interpret this prince to be the Messiah; so doth Jerome." Greenhill on Ezekiel, last vol., p. 407.[^001-note34]

75. PSALMS 60 AND 108. The 60th and 108th Psalms, which are in great part the same, seem to be prophecies of the times of the Messiah. The first verse of Ps. 60 speaks of a time of great displeasure against Israel, and casting them off and dispersing them in the world, preceding the glorious things that are spoken of in the Psalm. "O God thou hast cast us off, thou hast scattered us, thou hast been displeased; O turn thyself to us again." So v. 10; and Ps. 108, v. 11, "O God, which hadst cast us off, and thou, O God, which didst not go out with our armies"; and [Ps. 60,] v. 3, "Thou hast showed thy people hard things: thou hast made us to drink the wine of astonishment." This don't agree with the time of that victory of David over his enemies, mentioned in the title of the Psalm, but it is agreeable to the greater part of the forementioned prophecies of the Messiah.

V. 2, "Thou hast made the earth to tremble; thou hast broken it," is agreeable to Haggai's prophecies of shaking the heavens and the earth, and shaking all nations, Hag. 2:6, and also vv. 21-22; and to Ezek. 38:19-21; and Is. 24:18-20, "the foundations of the earth do shake. The earth is utterly broken down, the earth is clean dissolved, the earth is moved exceedingly. The earth shall move to and fro like a drunkard, and shall be removed like a cottage; and the transgression thereof shall be heavy upon it"; and Joel 3:16, and many others.

V. 4, "Thou hast given a banner to them that fear thee, that it may be displayed because of the truth," is agreeable to Is. 59:19, "When the enemy shall come in like a flood, the Spirit of the Lord shall lift up a standard against him"; and Ps. 20:5, "in the name of our God we will set up our banners."

Ps. 108:3, "I will praise thee, O Lord, among the people: and I will sing praises unto thee among the nations," supposing David here speaks in the name of the Messiah, agrees well with other prophecies that foretell the Messiah's being the light of Gentiles, and his making known the true God to them.

Ps. 108:4, "For thy mercy is great above the heavens: and thy truth reacheth unto the clouds," is agreeable to what is said in other prophecies of the Messiah, of God's great mercy and faithfulness manifested to his people in the time of the Messiah.

Ps. 60:5 and 108:6, "That thy beloved may be delivered; save with thy right hand, and answer me." Here the deliverance of God's beloved or spouse, is spoken of as a thing dependent on his being saved, and his prayers answered, that speaks; which is agreeable to Ps. 22:19-21, "be not thou far from me, O Lord: O my strength, haste thee to help me. Deliver my soul from the sword; my darling from the power of the dog. Save me from the lion's mouth: for thou hast heard me from the horns of the unicorn."

Ps. 60:6-7 and 108:7-8, "God hath spoken in his holiness; I will rejoice, I will divide Shechem, and mete out the valley of Succoth. Gilead is mine, and Manasseh is mine; Ephraim also is the strength of my head; Judah is my lawgiver." Here is a prediction of God's acknowledging Israel anew as his people, after he had cast 'em off (as Ps. 60:1, 10 and Ps. 108:11), and his settling of them anew in their own land, after he had scattered 'em, as v. 1; agreeable to most of the foregoing prophecies of the Messiah. And here is also a prophecy of a new laying out and division of the land, agreeable to Ezekiel, the nine last chapters, and Jer. 31:38-40, and Obadiah, vv. 18-20. These things are plain notes of a prophecy of the Messiah's times, and was no consequence of that victory of David, spoken of in the title of the 60th Psalm, nor did they agree to David's times. Particularly, here is a prediction of the restoration and resettlement of that part of the land that was beyond Jordan, as the land of Gilead and Manasseh, that were subject to captivity and desolation before other parts of the land, and had lain desolate much longer. This is agreeable to Is. 9, at the beginning.

Ps. 60:8 and 108:9, "over Edom will I cast out my shoe," and Ps. 60:12 and 108:13, "he it is that shall tread down our enemies." This is agreeable to Is. 63[:1-3], "Who is this that cometh from Edom, with dyed garments [. . .], with thy garments like him that treadeth in the winefat? I have trodden the winepress alone; [. . .] I will tread them in mine anger, and trample them in my fury."

Ps. 108:9 and 60:8, "Moab is my washpot; over Edom will I cast out my shoe; over Philistia will I triumph," is agreeable to Is. 11:14, "But they shall fly upon the shoulders of the Philistines towards the west; they shall spoil them of the east together: they shall lay their hand upon Edom and Moab"; and Obadiah, vv. 18-19, "And the house of Jacob shall be for a fire, . . . and the house of Esau for stubble, and they shall kindle in them, and devour them; . . . And they of the south shall possess the mount of Esau; and they of the plain the Philistines." See also Is. 25:10 with 34:5-6, and Num. 24:17-18, "a Star shall arise out of Jacob, and a Scepter shall rise out of Israel, and shall smite the four corners of Moab, and destroy all the children of Sheth. And Edom shall be a possession, Seir also shall be a possession for his enemies; and Israel shall do valiantly." These last words are agreeable to Ps. 60:12 and 108:13, "Through God we shall do valiantly."

Ps. 60:8, "Philistia, triumph thou because of me." Here is a prediction of the calling of the Gentiles, even of some of the worst of them, that had been great enemies to the church, and bringing them into a covenant relation to the God of Israel as their God. This is agreeable to what is said of Egypt and Assyria in the three last verses of Is. 19; and especially is this agreeable to Ps. 87:4, "I will make mention of Rahab and Babylon to them that know me: behold Philistia, and Tyre, with Ethiopia; this man was born there."

Ps. 60:9 and 108:10, "Who will bring me into the strong city? who will lead me into Edom?" Here, the conquest and destruction of the enemies of Israel is represented as chiefly consisting in subduing Edom and the strong city. This is agreeable to Is. 25:2-3, "For thou hast made of a city an heap; of a defensed city a ruin: a palace of strangers to be no city; it shall never be built. Therefore shall the strong people glorify thee, the city of the terrible nations shall fear thee"; and v. 12, "the fortress of the high fort of thy walls shall he bring down, lay low, and bring to the ground, even to the dust"; and ch. 26:5-6, "For he bringeth down them that dwell on high; the lofty city, he layeth it low; he layeth it low, even to the ground; he bringeth it even to the dust. The foot shall tread it down, even the feet of the poor, and the steps of the needy"; and ch. 30:25,[^001-note35] "in the day of the great slaughter, when the towers fall," together with ch. 34:5-6, "For my sword is bathed in heaven: it shall come down upon Idumea, . . . for the Lord hath a sacrifice in Bozrah" (which was the strongest city in Idumea), "and a great slaughter in the land of Idumea."

76. PSALM 67. The 67th Psalm is very evidently a prophecy of the times of the Messiah. For though what is expressed in it is chiefly by way of pe[ti]tion, yet those petitions seem to have the force of predictions: for it appears to have been of old the manner to prophesy of future events in the form of petitions, or language of prayer. See Gen. 27:28-29, and Deut. 33:6-8, 24, and Judg. 9:20.

What is said, v. 1, of God's causing his face to shine to shine upon his people, is agreeable to Ps. 89:15, "Blessed are the people that know the joyful sound: they shall walk, O Lord, in the light of thy countenance"; and what is said, Is. 6,[^001-note36] of God's people's walking in the light of the Lord; and in Is. 60[:2, 19], of God's glory's being risen on his church, and the sun's being no more her light by day, but God's being her everlasting light; and in Mal. 4[:2], of the Sun of righteousness rising on his people; and many other such things in forementioned prophecies.

What is said, vv. 2nd, 3rd, 4th, 5th, [and] 7th verses, of the calling of the Gentiles, and God's appearing to judge the people and to govern the nations upon earth, and all nations being taught God's ways and having God's saving health shown to them, and all nations rejoicing and singing praises to God on this account, and all the ends of the earth fearing God, are certain and clear notes of a prophecy of the days of the Messiah, and are all things agreeable to so many passages everywhere in other prophecies, that it would be both endless and needless particularly to show them.

V. 6, "Then shall the earth yield her increase; and God, even our own God, shall bless us," is agreeable to Is. 30:23-24, and Jer. 31:12, and Ezek. 34:27, 29 and 36:29-30, and Amos 9:13-14 and many other prophecies.

77. PSALM 138. The 138th Psalm has that clear evidence of being a prophecy of the Messiah. There, in vv. 4-5, the calling of the Gentiles is foretold with like circumstances as in other prophecies of the Messiah. "All the kings of the earth shall praise thee, O Lord, when they hear the words of thy mouth. Yea, they shall sing in the ways of the Lord: for great is the glory of the Lord." And this is here spoken of as a consequence of a great deliverance, granted to him that is represented speaking in this Psalm, in answer to his prayer. See vv. 3-5, which is agreeable to the manner in which the same thing is foretold, Ps. 22:19-21 with vv. 27-29, and Ps. 60:5-9 and 108:6-10.

Vv. 1-3, the Psalmist declares that in consequence of his great deliverance in answer to prayer, that he would praise God with his whole heart and sing praise to him before the gods, and worship towards his holy temple and praise his name; [which] is agreeable to Ps. 22:20-22 with v. 25, and 108:3 with v. 6.

That, v. 2, "for thy lovingkindness and for thy truth: for thou hast magnified thy word above all thy name," is agreeable to, and is brought in in like manner with, Ps. 108:3-4, "I will sing praises unto thee among the nations. For thy mercy is great above the heavens: and thy truth reacheth unto the clouds."

V. 2, "thou hast magnified thy word above all thy name," i. e. "In what thou hast done for thy people in fulfillment of thy promises, thou hast exceeded all the fame that we have heard of thy great works of old." This don't well agree with anything that came to pass in David's time, but well agrees with the great works which it is often foretold God would do for his people by the Messiah, and well agrees with what is expressly said of those works in many forementioned prophecies, as Is. 43:16-18 and 64:3-4, and Jer. 16:14-15, and Is. 65:17-18. This is a clear evidence of this Psalm's being a prophecy of the times of the Messiah.

V. 6, "Though the Lord be high, yet hath he respect unto the lowly," is very agreeable to Is. 57:15, "For thus saith the high and lofty One," etc., and Is. 66:1-2, "The heaven is my throne," etc.

V. 8, "The Lord will perfect that which concerneth me: thy mercy, O Lord, endureth forever: forsake not the work of thine own hands." This is agreeable to Is. 66:9, "Shall I bring to the birth, and not cause to bring forth? saith the Lord: shall I cause to bring forth, and shut the womb? saith thy God."

78. PSALM 85. That the 85th Psalm is a prophecy of the redemption of the Messiah, there are many evidences.

The grand subject is the bringing back the captivity of Jacob, and forgiving the iniquity of his people and covering all their sin, and taking away all his wrath and turning the people again to God; which is the description everywhere given of the Messiah's salvation.

The redemption and captivity here spoken of, is after a long-continued manifestation of God's anger, so as to make their case appear to an human eye almost desperate, vv. 5-6, agreeable to many forementioned prophecies that speak of perpetual wastes and the desolations of many generations, when their bones were dried, their hope lost, etc.

V. 6, "Wilt thou not revive us again." The Hebrew signifies "restoring life." This is agreeable to Ezek. 37, and many other prophecies.

This Psalm speaks of that which is eminently *God's salvation*, vv. 7 and 9, agreeable to the places taken notice, §51, and other places.

Vv. 10-11, "Mercy and truth are met together; righteousness and peace have kissed each other. Truth shall spring out of the earth; and righteousness shall look down from heaven." This is agreeable to Ps. 138:2, and Ps. 108:3-4, and Ps. 72:3, and Is. 45:8, "Drop down, ye heavens, from above, and let the skies pour down righteousness: let the earth open, and let them bring forth salvation, and let righteousness spring up together; I the Lord have created it."

V. 12, "Yea, the Lord will give that which is good." This is agreeable to Jer. 33:14, "Behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that I will perform that good thing which I have promised unto the house of Israel, and to the house of Judah."

V. 12, "and our land shall yield her increase." This is agreeable to Ps. 67:6, "Then shall the earth yield her increase; and God, even our own God, shall bless us"; and Is. 30:23-24 and Jer. 31:12, Ezek. 34:27 29 and 36:29-30, and Amos 9:13-14, and many other places.

79. PSALM 69. The 69th Psalm is very parallel with the 22nd Psalm, and there is much evidence that the same things are the subject of it, that it has already been shown are spoken of in that Psalm. Here is described a like suffering state of the person that is represented speaking, and a like deliverance, and like consequences of the deliverance.

What is said here of the multitude of the Psalmist's enemies, v. 4, is parallel with Ps. 22:7, 12, 16.

What is said here of the great reproach that he suffered, vv. 7, 9-12, 19-20, is parallel with Ps. 22:7.

V. 20, "Reproach hath broken my heart," is agreeable to Ps. 22:14, "my heart is like wax; it is melted in the midst of my bowels."

V. 3, "I am weary of my crying: my throat is dried: mine eyes fail while I wait for God," is agreeable to Ps. 22:15, "My strength is dried up like a potsherd; and my tongue cleaveth to my chaws."

V. 20, "I looked for some to take pity, but there was none; and for comforters, but I found none," is agreeable to Ps. 22:11, "there is none to help."

Vv. 13, 16[^001-note37] are parallel with Ps. 22:24.

Vv. 30, 32, "I will praise the name of God with a song, and will magnify him with thanksgiving. . . . The humble shall see this, and be glad; and your heart shall live that seek God," is almost the same with Ps. 22:25-26, "My praise shall be of thee in the great congregation: I will pay my vows before them that fear him. [. . .] your heart shall live for ever."

V. 34, "Let the heaven and earth praise him, the seas, and every thing that moveth therein," is parallel with Ps. 22:27.

V. 36 is agreeable to Ps. 22:30.

This Psalm is said to be a Psalm of David in the title, but David did not speak here in his own name, for the things spoken in the Psalm are not applicable to David. They are not applicable to any of the troubles that David was the subject of by Saul's persecution, or before David was exalted to the throne of Israel: for Zion is spoken of, v. 35, "For God will save Zion, and build the cities of Judah," etc. But Zion did not belong to Israel, but was a heathen city, till David was king over all Israel, and in great prosperity, II Sam. 5:1-9. See SSS on the argument of this Psalm.[^001-note38]

Nor does what is said in this Psalm seem to agree to David's circumstances in the time of Absalom's rebellion, for this is spoken of as a very long-continued affliction, v. 3. But David's troubles in the time of Absalom's rebellion were very short. In vv. 4 and 21, the Psalmist speaks of himself as being in the hands of his enemies, and obliged to do what his enemies enjoined him--that is, it must be so understood, if the words are understood of David. And in vv. 7 and 9, the Psalmist speaks of those troubles as what he suffered for God's sake, and as the fruits of a persecution that was brought upon him by his zeal for the worship of God. But this was not the case in Absalom's rebellion. Again, v. 20, he speaks of himself as being wholly forsaken of all, having none to pity or comfort him; but this was not the case with David in the time of Absalom's rebellion, for he then had the most valiant men in Israel about him to defend him. And then the imprecations in vv. 22-28, than which we have none more terrible in all the book of Psalms, are what can't be supposed to be uttered by David in the case of Absalom's rebellion: for Absalom then was the chief cause of all his troubles, and the head of his enemies. But it is plain that David was as remote as possible from uttering such curses against him, for he both exceedingly longed and also hoped for his deliverance and welfare, with the bowels of a most tender, compassionate father. He here prays that his enemies may be "blotted out of the book of the living" and perish forever in another world, and their families be extirpated, vv. 24-25, 28. But how much otherwise was the case with the last with David, as to Absalom.

In v. 32 to the end, the Psalmist speaks of the saving of Zion and her being rebuilt, and the rebuilding the cities of Judah and God's people's being restored to the possession of it, after they had been in a state of bondage and captivity, as the consequence of the deliverance of the person that is the subject of this Psalm, out of trouble; which don't agree with any deliverance of David, and by innumerable passages in other prophecies of the Messiah, shows plainly that it is the Messiah that is here spoken of.

V. 13, "in an acceptable time: O God, in the multitude of thy mercy hear me, in the truth of thy salvation," is agreeable to Is. 49:8, "Thus saith the Lord, In an acceptable time have I heard thee, in a day of salvation have I helped thee."

V. 5, "O God, thou knowest my foolishness; and my sins are not hid from thee," is no sufficient objection against this Psalm's being spoken in the name of the Messiah: because we learn by the 53rd of Isaiah, that although the Messiah was God's righteous servant, yet he bore trouble and the terrible fruits of God's displeasure for sins that were laid upon, and reputed in God's dealing with him, as if they were his own. And this is what is meant by God's *Knowing* his foolishness, and his sins not being *hid* from him; i. e. God, by punishing, brought them to remembrance, did not hide them by forgiving, but found out his sin and beheld iniquity, and punished it, and set the sins in his view to excite his wrath. God forgave nothing to the Messiah, but exacted the uttermost farthing; he would know and behold all his iniquity, and none of his guiltiness (as it is the original, in that 5th verse) should be hid from him, agreeable to the 53rd of Isaiah. See "Miscellanies," no. 1035.[^001-note39]

See concerning the opinion of the rabbies about the subject of this Psalm, SSS on the argument of the Psalm.[^001-note40]

## "Prophecies of the Messiah" Miscellanies no. 1067.3 Misc. 1067.3 (ed.)

Jonathan Edwards80. PSALM 86. In the 86th Psalm there is a prophecy of the times of the Messiah, as that alone sufficiently demonstrates, that here is a prophecy of the calling of the Gentiles and conversion of all nations. V. 9, "All nations whom thou hast made shall come and worship before thee, O Lord; and shall glorify thy name." And this prediction is, in the 40th and following chapters of Isaiah, attended with a declaration of the infinitely superior greatness and glory of God above the gods of the Gentiles, and his being the only true God, and there[^002-note1] being no works like his, vv. 8, 10.

And this prophecy of the calling of the Gentiles is here spoken of as a consequence of God's hearing and answering the prayer of the Psalmist, and delivering him out of trouble, v. 7, as it is in Ps. 22 and Ps. 138.

V. 10, "For thou art great, and dost wondrous things," is agreeable to very many prophecies of the Messiah that speak of great wonders God would work in his day.

V. 16, "give thy strength unto thy servant, and save the son of thy handmaid." This is agreeable to the Messiah's being called the seed of the woman [Gen. 3:15], and is also agreeable to Is. 7[:14], "a virgin shall conceive, and bring forth a son," etc. See note on Ps. 116:16.[^002-note2]

V. 13, "Thou hast delivered my soul from the lowest hell," is agreeable to Ps. 16[:10], "thou wilt not leave my soul in hell."

81. PSALM 58. The 58th Psalm seems not to be spoken with regard to Saul and other rulers in Israel in his days that persecuted him, but to be wholly prophetical concerning the future destruction of the enemies of the church. There are several things that evince that the Psalm has not respect to Saul and his companions and assistants in persecution:

1. Those that David speaks of are a vast multitude of people, v. 1, "*O congregation*": in which there is an allusion either to the whole multitude of Israel, that were wont to meet three times a year, or the seventy elders. And again, it is added, "*do ye judge uprightly, O ye sons of men*," as though it was a great part of mankind that were those that he had respect to. But David's enemies in Saul's time were comparatively but few; the very ground of Saul's persecuting him, was that he had the affections and esteem of the greater part of the nation of Israel, which made Saul jealous of him. I Sam. 18[:5], "And David went out whithersoever Saul sent him, and behaved himself wisely: and Saul set him over the men of war, and he was accepted in the sight of all the people, and also in the sight of Sauls' servants"; and v. 16, "And all Israel and Judah loved David, because he went out and came in before them."

2. The enemies are spoken of as suddenly destroyed and disappointed and vanishing away, even at that very time when they are making great preparations to destroy God's people, and have invaded them, and are at the point of executing their design, vv. 6-10. But this don't agree at all with Saul and his fellow persecuters of David: for when Saul was destroyed, he had been making preparation to go to war with the Philistines, and was gone out to war with them who were great enemies of God's visible church, and had for some time given over pursuing after David, I Sam. 26:25 and 27:1, 4. But these things well agree with what is often prophesied of the destruction of the enemies of the church in the Messiahs days, in Is. 24-35, Ps. 46 and 48, Ezek. 38 and 39, and many others. That, vv. 8[-9], "As a snail which melteth, let every one of them pass away: like the untimely birth of a woman, that he may not see the sun. Before your pots can feel the thorns, he shall take them away as with a whirlwind, both living, and in his wrath," is very agreeable to Is. 29:5-8,

Moreover the multitude of thy strangers shall be like small dust, and the multitude of the terrible ones shall be as chaff that passeth away: yea, it shall be at an instant suddenly. Thou shalt be visited of the Lord of hosts with thunder, and with earthquake, and great noise, with storm and tempest, and the flame of devouring fire. And the multitude of all nations that fight against Ariel, even all that fight against her and her munition, and that distress her, shall be as a dream of a night vision. It shall be as when a hungry man dreameth, and, behold, he eateth; but he awaketh, and his soul is empty: or as when a thirsty man dreameth, and, behold, he drinketh; and he awaketh, and, behold, he is faint, and his soul hath appetite: so shall the multitude of all the nations be, that fight against mount Zion.

See also Is. 41:11-12. Compare also what is said in this Psalm, v. 6-11, with Is. 30:27 to the end.

3. The imprecations and curses that are here, vv. 6-9 together with v. 10, "The righteous shall rejoice when he seeth the vengeance: he shall wash his feet in the blood of the wicked," is not applicable to David with regard to Saul, who was so far from trampling on his blood that he greatly lamented his death, and condemned those that insulted him in his misery. But this 10th verse is exactly parallel with many prophecies of the destruction of the enemies of the church in the days of the Messiah. That "the righteous shall rejoice when he seeth the vengeance," is agreeable to Is. 30:29, 32 and innumerable other prophecies. And that in the same verse, "he shall wash his feet in the blood of the wicked," is agreeable to Ps. 68:23, "That thy foot may be dipped in the blood of thine enemies"; and Is. 26:5-6, "he bringeth down them that dwell on high; the lofty city, he layeth it low, . . . The foot shall tread it down, even the feet of the poor, and the steps of the needy"; Mic. 7:10, "now shall she be trodden down as the mire of the streets"; and Mal. 4:3, "And ye shall tread down the wicked; for they shall be ashes under the soles of your feet."

What is said, vv. 3-5, of the desperate natural corruption and depravity of man, as appearing in the persecutors of the church, is agreeable to Ps. 14 and 53, before taken notice of.[^002-note3]

V. 11, "So that a man shall say, Verily there is a reward for the righteous: verily he is a God that judgeth in the earth," is agreeable to Mal. 3:18, "Then shalt ye return, and discern between the righteous and the wicked, between him that serveth God and him that serveth him not"; and Ps. 102:13, 15, "Thou shalt arise, and have mercy upon Zion: for the time to favor her, yea, the set time, is come. . . . So the heathen shall fear the name of the Lord, and all the kings of the earth thy glory."

82. PSALM 65. That there is a prophecy of the times of the Messiah in the 65th Psalm, is evident from that, that here the calling of the Gentiles and the conversion of all nations to God is here foretold, as v. 2, "unto thee shall all flesh come"; and v. 5, "thou art the confidence of all the ends of the earth, and of them that are afar off upon the sea." To the like purpose is that, v. 8, "thou makest the outgoings of the morning and of the evening to rejoice"--that is, the utmost ends of the earth in the east and west, whence go forth the morning and evening--which is agreeable to v. 5; and to Is. 24:16, "From the utmost parts of the earth have we heard songs, even glory to the righteous"; and Ps. 113:3, "From the rising of the sun to the going down of the same the Lord's name is to be praised"; Is. 59:19, "So shall they fear the name of the Lord from the west, and his glory from the rising of the sun"; and Mal. 1:11, "For from the rising of the sun even unto the going down of the same my name shall be great among the Gentiles"; Is. 43:5, "I will bring thy seed from the east, and gather thee from the west."

Here is a prediction of great salvation that God would accomplish for Zion, in answer to prayer and after waiting for God, vv. 1-2, 5, agreeable to Is. 62:6-8, etc., and Ezek. 36:37 and Gen. 49:18, Is. 25:9 and 26:8 and 33:2 and 64:4, Is. 8:17, Mic. 7:7.

V. 3, "as for our transgressions, thou shalt purge them away," is agreeable to Is. 33:24 and Joel 3:21, Jer. 31:34 and 33:8, Mic. 7:18, Ps. 85:2, Zech. 3:4, Is. 43:25 and 44:22 and 40:2, Jer. 3:12-13, Hos. 14:1-2.

V. 5, "By terrible things in righteousness wilt thou answer us," is agreeable to Ps. 45[:4], "ride forth because of truth, meekness and righteousness; and thy right hand shall teach thee terrible things," and very many places in forementioned prophecies that speak of very terrible things that [God] would do for the deliverance of his people from their enemies.

'Tis signified in the 8th verse, that hereby God would strike a dread upon the hearts of the nations, all over the world; which is agreeable to Ps. 58:9-11, Ps. 48:5-6, Ps. 46:6, Ps:14:5, Is. 2:18-21.

V. 7, "Which stillest the noise of the seas, the noise of their waves, and the tumults of the people," is agreeable to Ps. 46:8-10. See also other places referred to, §47.

What is said, v. 9 to the end, concerning God's watering and blessing the earth, and causing it to yield its fruits in abundance, is agreeable to Ps. 67:6 and 85:12, Is. 30:23-24, Jer. 31:12, Ezek. 34:27, 29 and 36:29-30, Amos 9:13-14.

83. PSALM 66. In the 66th Psalm, there is clear evidence of a respect to the times of the Messiah: for here is mention of all nations, or the whole habitable world, being brought to the acknowledgement and worship of the true God; and it is described in like manner as in other prophecies of the times of the Messiah, viz. by all lands rejoicing in God, and praising, worshipping him, and joyfully singing to his name, vv. 1-2, 4, 8.

What is said, vv. 3, 5, of God's terrible doings, is agreeable to what is said in the foregoing Psalm, v. 5, and innumerable other prophecies before mentioned.

What is said, v. 3, of God's enemies being affrighted through his terrible works, and so being afraid any longer to openly oppose, but submitting through fear, is agreeable to the foregoing Psalm, v. 8, and Ps. 46:6 to the end, and Ps. 58:9-11 and 48:5-6 and 14:5, and Is. 2:16, 19.

This Psalm speaks of these great and terrible works being wrought for the deliverance of his people, after they had been in great affliction, in subjection to the power and insults of their cruel enemies, as v. 10, etc.; which is agreeable to other prophecies of the redemption of the Messiah.

84. PSALM 80, ESPECIALLY VV. 15-17. Ps. 80:17, "Let thine hand be upon the man of thy right hand, upon the son of man whom thou madest strong for thyself." The person here spoken seems evidently to be the Messiah, for the context makes it evident that it was some great person that God would raise up and strengthen to that end, that he might turn his people to God and accomplish deliverance for his people, and put new life into them, and save his vine that he brought out of Egypt after her hedges had been broken down, and it had been exposed to all its enemies without any protection, and after "the boar out of the wood" had wasted it and "the wild beast of the field" had devoured it; which by the prophecies of the Messiah everywhere, can be no other than the Messiah.

His being called the man of God's right hand, is agreeable to Ps. 110[:1], "Sit thou on my right hand"; and Ps. 2:6-7, "I have set my king . . . Thou art my son"; and Ps. 89:27, "I will make him my firstborn, higher than the kings of the earth."

The whole verse, "Let thine hand be upon the man of thy right hand, upon the son of man whom thou madest strong for thyself," is agreeable to Ps. 89:19, 21, "I have laid help upon one that is mighty; . . . With whom my hand shall be established: mine arm also shall strengthen him"; and Is. 42:6, "I the Lord have called thee in righteousness, and will hold thine hand, and will keep thee, and give thee for a covenant of the people"; and Is. 49:8, "I have helped thee: and will preserve thee, and give thee for a covenant of the people, to establish the earth, and to cause to inherit the desolate heritages."

He seems here to be mentioned in this verse as a man and "the son of man," because it is a remarkable and wonderful thing, that a man that is a worm, and the son of man that is but a worm, and is so often spoken of as the grass and flower of the field, and a blast of wind, and one in whom is no help and altogether vanity, and by no means to be trusted in, should be made strong.

The son of man spoken of, v. 17, that God had made strong for himself, that was the man of God's right hand, seems to be the same with the BRANCH that he had made strong for himself, spoken of, v. 15 foregoing; which is the same man whose name is the BRANCH, spoken of, Zech. 6[:13], that was to sit on God's throne in the temple, and the same with that tender twig that God would set on the mountain of the height of Israel, and cause it to "bring forth boughs, and bear fruit, and to be a goodly cedar," etc., Ezek. 17:22-24.[^002-note4]

'Tis no argument against this, that this Branch is spoken of, v. 16, as being "burnt with fire," and so needing deliverance as well as the rest of the vine: for that is agreeable to Is. 53, and it is also agreeable to Ps. 22 and 69 and other psalms that have been already considered, that the Messiah as well as his people should be oppressed and as it were consumed by his and their enemies, and that in his deliverance they have deliverance.

One benefit here mentioned of God's hands being upon the man of his right hand, and his being strengthened to deliver and save his people, is that they should no more go back from God, v. 18. This is agreeable to Jer. 32:40, "And I will make an everlasting covenant with them, that I will not turn away from them, to do them good; but I will put my fear in their hearts, that they shall not depart from me"; so Jer. 31:32-33, and Ezek. 36:26:27 and 39:29.

V. 1, "shine forth," and v. 3, "cause thy face to shine," [and] so vv. 7 and 19, is agreeable to Ps. 67:1, "and cause his face to shine upon us"; to Is. 60, at the beginning, "Arise, shine; for thy light is come, for the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee"; and Mal. 4[:2], "The Sun of righteousness shall arise," etc., and many other parallel passages that have been before mentioned.

V. 1, "thou that dwellest between the cherubims, shine forth." This is an earnest request of the church and a prophecy that God, that hid himself and veiled his glory, and as it were dwelt in a secret place in those ages of the Mosaic dispensation while the Mosaic temple with its veil was standing, would openly manifest his glory to all his people without a veil, and give free access and communication, and not only to the high priest once a year. God's place where his glory, or Shechinah, dwelt, was in the thick darkness, in the Holy of Holies, within the veil between the cherubims; which was called the secret of God's presence and of his pavilion [Ps. 18:11], the secret of God's tabernacle, and "the secret place of the most High." There his glory was hid first by the veil of the temple, and then with the wings of the cherubims; see Ps. 27:5 and 31:20 and 91:1. This is a prediction of his glorious shining forth, that all might behold it and have access at all times to it, which the high priest had but once a year. This is agreeable to Is. 45:14-15, "they shall fall down unto thee, and make supplication unto thee, saying, Surely God is in thee; and there is none else, there is no God. Verily thou art a God that hidest thyself, O God of Israel, the Savior"; and v. 17, "But Israel shall be saved in the Lord with an everlasting salvation: ye shall not be ashamed nor confounded world without end"; v. 19, "I have not spoken in secret, in a dark place of the earth: I said not to the seed of Jacob, Seek ye me in vain"; v. 22, "Look unto me, and be ye saved, all ye ends of the earth"; and Ps. 89:15, "they shall walk, O Lord, in the light of thy countenance"; and Is. 2:[:5], "O house of Israel, come, let us walk in the light of the Lord"; Is. 60[:1-2, 19], "the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee. [. . .] the Lord shall arise upon thee, and his glory shall be seen upon thee. [. . .] The sun shall be no more thy light by day; . . . the Lord shall be thine everlasting light"; Is. 24[:23], "Then shall the moon be confounded, and the sun ashamed, when the Lord of hosts shall reign on mount Zion, [. . .] before his ancients gloriously"; and Jer. 3:16, etc., "they shall [say] no more, The ark of the covenant," etc., "but Jerusalem shall be the throne of the Lord"; Is.[^002-note5] 52:8, "they shall see eye to eye, when the Lord shall bring again Zion"; Ps. 102:16,[^002-note6] "When the Lord shall build up Zion, he shall appear in his glory"; Is. 66:18, "I will gather all nations and tongues; and they shall come, and see my glory"; Is. 35:2, "they shall see the glory of the Lord, and the excellency of our God"; and 33:17, "Thine eyes shall see the king in his beauty"; Is. 40:5, "And the glory of the Lord shall be revealed, and all flesh shall see it together."

[Vv.] 1-2, "shine forth. Before Ephraim and Benjamin and Manasseh stir up thy strength, and come and save us," i. e. "Appear for our deliverance from our enemies, under whom we have been in captivity and bondage, as thou didst for the deliverance of [Israel], as thou didst when thou deliveredst Israel from their Egyptian bondage, and leddest them from Egypt to Canaan, when thou wentest before Ephraim, Benjamin and Manasseh, whose camps were westward behind the ark and the tabernacle of the congregation "; see Num. 2:18-23. This shows that the bringing of God's church out of captivity and bondage to foreign enemies is spoken of in this Psalm, agreeable to most other prophecies of the redemption of the Messiah; and 'tis also agreeable to other prophecies of the Messiah, that his redemption should be compared to the going forth of the children of Israel out of Egypt. Is. 11:15-16, "And the Lord shall utterly destroy the tongue of the Egyptian sea; and with his mighty wind he shall shake his hand over the river, and shall smite it in the seven streams, and make men go over dryshod. And there shall be an highway for the remnant of his people, which shall be left, from Assyria; like as it was to Israel in the day when he came up out of the land of Egypt." So Num. 23:33, Mic. 7:15, Is. 43:16-19 and 63:11-13 and 64:3-4, and Jer. 16:14-15 and 23:7-8, and Ezek. 20:35-37, Hos. 2:15.

In that latter expression, "*stir up thy strength, and come and save us*," v. 2, there seems to be a reference to God's arising, the cloud of glory being taken up, and the ark being taken up and setting forward, when God led the armies of Israel onward in their march towards Canaan to fight with their enemies, when Moses said, Num. 10:35, "Rise up, Lord, let thine enemies be scattered; and let them that hate thee flee before thee." And David and Solomon, on the like occasion of the removing of the ark, express themselves to the like purpose, thus: "Arise, O Lord, thou, and the ark of thy strength," Ps. 132:8, II Chron. 6:41-42.

Vv. 3, 7, 19, "Turn us again, O God of hosts, cause thy face to shine; and we shall be saved," is agreeable to Jer. 31:18, "turn thou me, and I shall be turned."

85. PSALM 24. The 24th Psalm seems, from the beginning to the end, to have respect to the times of the Messiah. This Psalm speaks of the privileges of God's people--such as having an interest in his temple and ordinances, going up to the house of the Lord and standing in his holy place--as not confined to any particular nation, but such as should be bestowed on all that have clean hands and a pure heart, of [all] nations and all the inhabitants of the earth indifferently, for all nations are alike God's: for "the earth is the Lord's, and the fullness thereof; the world, and all that dwell therein," they are alike the work of his hand, as vv. 1-2. And therefore the privileges of God's people shall not be confined to any particular family or generation of men, but whosoever have pure hearts and clean hands, of all that dwell upon the earth, are God's generation or nation, his Israel, and shall be accounted as those that belong to Jacob; as v. 6, "This is the *generation* of them that seek him, that seek thy face, O Jacob." The like reason is here given why the Gentiles should be called and accepted, as in Ps. 86:9, "All nations whom thou hast made shall come and worship before thee, and shall glorify thy name"; and Ps. 89:11-12, "The heavens are thine, the earth also is thine: as for the world and the fullness thereof, thou hast founded them. The north and the south thou hast created them: Tabor and Hermon shall rejoice in thy name"--i. e. Hermon, a desert mountain, a noted haunt of wild beasts of prey, at a great distance, and the border of the vast deserts of Arabia (see Ps. 42:6 and Cant. 4:8), as well as Tabor, a mountain in the midst of the land of Israel; and Is. 42:4-6, "He shall not fail nor be discouraged, till he have set judgment in the earth: and the isles shall wait for his law. Thus saith God the Lord, he that created the heavens, and stretched them out; he that spread forth the earth, and that which cometh out of it; he that giveth breath unto the people upon it, and spirit to them that walk therein: I the Lord have called thee in righteousness, and will hold thine hand, and will keep thee, and give thee for a covenant of the people, for a light of the Gentiles."

In this Psalm, it is not only foretold that all of every nation that have clean hands and pure hearts, and han't left their soul to vanity nor sworn deceitfully, shall ascend into the hill of the Lord and stand in his holy place. But 'tis implied that such only shall be admitted to this privilege, not any other, though they belong to the nation of Israel; which is agreeable to many forementioned prophecies of the Messiah, as Is. 33:14-17, "The sinners in Zion are afraid; fearfulness hath surprized the hypocrites. . . . He that walketh righteously, and speaketh uprightly; he that despiseth the gain of oppressions, that shaketh his hands from holding of bribes, that stoppeth his ears from hearing of blood, and shutteth his eyes from seeing evil; he shall dwell on high: his place of defense shall be the munitions of rocks: [. . .] Thine eyes shall see the king in his beauty"; Is. 26:2, "Open ye the gates, that the righteous nation which keepeth the truth may enter in" (here, the righteous are represented as being a nation or generation, God's Israel, agreeable to Ps. 24:6); Ezek. 44:9, "Thus saith the Lord, No stranger, uncircumcised in heart, nor uncircumcised in flesh, shall enter into my sanctuary, of any stranger that is among the children of Israel"; Is. 57:13, "he that putteth his trust in me shall [. . .] inherit mine holy mountain." See also Is. 56:6-7 and Ezek. 20:38-40. Ps. 118:19-20, "Open to me the gates of righteousness: I will go into them, and I will praise the Lord: this gate of the Lord, into which the righteous shall enter." See also Is. 53:1 and 60:21, and Ezek. 49:7, 9; Joel 3:17, Ps. 22:25-26, 30 and 69:31-32, 36; Is. 35:8 and 4:3, Ezek. 20:38, Ps. 85:8-9.

And whereas those that have pure hearts, etc., in this Psalm, [are described] as being a distinct race, the nation or generation that are God's people, v. 6, "This is the generation of them of them that seek him, that seek thy face, O Jacob": this is agreeable to Is. 26:2, "Open ye the gates, that the righteous NATION that keepeth the truth may enter in"; and Ps. 22:25-26, "My praise shall be of thee in the great congregation: I will pay my vows before them that fear him. The meek shall eat and be satisfied: they shall praise the Lord that seek him," with v. 30, "A seed shall serve him; it shall be accounted to the Lord for a generation"; and Ps. 69:35-36, "For God will save Zion, and will build the cities of Judah: that they may dwell there, and have it in possession. The seed also of his servants shall inherit it: and they that love his name shall dwell therein."

V. 7, to the end, is a prophecy of the Messiah's coming to Zion, ascending God's holy hill and entering God's holy temple, to sit on his throne there, in order to the setting up of his kingdom in the world. By the connection of the Psalm, we may doubtless conclude that these gates and everlasting doors here spoken of, are the gates and doors of God's holy place that was in his holy hill, spoken, v. 3 foregoing, and the same with the gates mentioned, Is. 26:2, "Open ye the gates, that the righteous nation that keepeth the truth may enter in"; and Ps. 118:19-20, "Open to me the gates of righteousness: I will go into them, and praise the Lord: this gate of the Lord, into which the righteous shall enter." The person here speaking is the Messiah, the same that says, the next verse but one preceding [Ps. 118:17], "I shall not die, but live, and declare the works of the Lord"; and the same of whom it is said, in the next verse but one [v. 22], "The stone which the builders refused is become the head of the corner," and v. 26, "Blessed is he that cometh in the name of the Lord." So that this prophecy is very parallel with that, and also with that, Mal. 3:1, "and the Lord, whom ye seek, shall suddenly come into his temple, even the messenger of the covenant, whom ye delight in"; and Ezek. 43:2-4, "And, behold, the glory of the God of Israel came from the way of the east: and his voice was as the noise of many waters: and the earth shined with his glory. . . . And the glory of the Lord came into the house by the way of the gates whose prospect is towards the east"; and ch. 44:2-3, "This gate shall be shut, it shall not be opened, and no man shall enter in by it, . . . It is for the prince; . . . he shall enter in by the way of the porch of that gate"; and so ch. 46:1-2. There, in Ezekiel, the chief gate of the temple is represented as reserved only for God and the Messiah; so here, the heads of the gates, or the chief gates, are called upon to open themselves to the king of glory.

'Tis an argument that this latter part of the Psalm is prophetical, because it speaks of a temple; but there was no temple in being in David's times. It speaks of "everlasting doors," which could not be meant of the door of the tabernacle, for the very nature of a tabernacle is not to be a durable dwelling, II Sam. 7:6, 10. And this is an argument that this latter part of the Psalm speaks of the times of the Messiah, and that the temple that it refers to is not Solomon's temple, but the temple that was to be in the Messiah's time, because the doors are once and again called everlasting doors: for neither the door of the tabernacle, nor Solomon's temple, were everlasting. The nature of a tabernacle is to be a transient building, made to be taken down; and the doors of the tabernacle, wherein the ark was in David's time, were so far from being everlasting, that they were very near to an end, and ceased when Solomon built the temple. And the doors of Solomon's temple were not everlasting; the destruction of that temple is spoken of in this book of Psalms by Asaph, Ps. 74. But 'tis foretold that the temple that should be in the Messiah's time, should be everlasting, in Ezek. 43:7 and ch. 37:26; which is also implied in very many other places.

'Tis probable this Psalm was penned on occasion of the removing of the ark, and therefore we may the more easily and reasonably suppose that it speaks of the Messiah, because it has before been shown that the 68th and 132nd Psalms, that were penned on the same occasion, do respect the Messiah; and that by God's entering the everlasting doors on his holy hill, spoken of in this Psalm, is meant the same thing as his ascending into his holy hill, Ps. 68:15-18, "The hill of God is as the hill of Bashan; . . . Thou hast ascended on high." And God is mentioned here in this Psalm as entering into the sanctuary on his holy hill, in the same character and in the same manner as in Ps. 68, viz. as the Lord of armies, "the Lord strong and mighty, the Lord mighty in battle" [Ps. 24:8]; and as returning to Zion in glorious triumph, after a great display of his mighty power in conquering his enemies. So is he represented ascending into the sanctuary in Mt. Zion, in Ps. 68: "Let God arise, let his enemies be scattered: . . . As smoke is driven away, . . . as wax melteth before the fire, so let the wicked perish at the presence of God," vv. 1-2; "Kings of armies did flee apace," v. 12, "Thou hast ascended on high, thou hast led captivity captive," [v. 18].

The repeating that interrogation, "Who is this King of glory?," on this occasion of his return in triumph from a glorious victory over his enemies, together with the answer to it, is agreeable to the like interrogation on the same occasion, and the answer, in Is. 63:1, "Who is this that cometh from Edom, with dyed garments from Bozrah? this that is glorious in his apparel, travelling in the greatness of his strength? I that speak in righteousness, mighty to save."

When the Messiah comes in such glorious triumph and high exaltation and magnificence , the inquiry might well be made with great admiration, "Who is this?," seeing he had before been seen, as is declared in other prophecies, to be "a tender plant, a root out of a dry ground, having "no form nor comeliness" [Is. 53:2]; and as when David, who was but a stripling, returned in glorious triumph to Jerusalem, after the battle with the Philistines with the head of Goliath in his hand, Saul inquired once and again with great admiration, who he was. I Sam. 17:55, "Whose son is this youth?," and v. 58, "Whose son art thou then, thou young man?" And the answer made, "The Lord strong and mighty, the Lord mighty in battle," is much like the song of the daughters of Israel when they came forth out of the cities of Israel to meet David, when he came from the slaughter of the Philistine, saying, "David hath slain his ten thousands" [I Sam. 18:7].

That such an inquiry should be made repeatedly in this Psalm, betokens him a very wonderful person, agreeable to Is. 9[:6], "his name shall be called Wonderful."

If we consider the connection of this latter part of the Psalm with the former part, it seems to be implied that the King of glory here spoken, was in a peculiar manner free from sin, of clean hands and a pure heart, and therefore all others had a right to ascend God's holy hill and to stand in his holy place. This is agreeable to what is said in that parallel place, Is. 63:1, "I that speak in righteousness"; and Is. 53:9, "because [he] had done no violence, neither was any deceit in his mouth"; and v. 11, "my righteous servant"; and Ps. 45:7, "Thou lovest righteousness, and hatest wickedness: therefore God hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows." And his being here called "the King of glory" and "the Lord strong and mighty, the Lord mighty in battle," is agreeable to vv. 1-4[^002-note7] of the same Psalm 45: "I speak of the things which I have made touching the king: [. . .] Thou art fairer than the children of men: . . . Gird thy sword upon thy thigh, O most mighty, with thy glory and thy majesty. And in thy majesty ride prosperously . . .: and thy right hand shall teach thee terrible things."

86. PSALMS 145, AND 146, AND 147, AND 148, AND 149, AND 150. The subjects of praise mainly insisted, Ps. 145 to the end of the book of Psalms (which all seems to be one continued song under that title at the beginning of Ps. 145, "David's Psalm of praise"), seem to be the glorious things of the Messiah's salvation and kingdom.

Ps. 145:4-7 is evidently prophetical of the great and long-continued future joy and prosperity of the church of God, and their exalted praises for a glorious future manifestation of his glorious greatness and majesty, and of his great goodness and faithfulness to his church, and terribly destroying their enemies.

V. 8 to the end, the Psalmist [speaks] of the calling of the Gentiles, and showing mercy and extending his goodness indifferently to all, of all nations that fear him and call upon him. This is largely insisted on, and terms of universality in this matter are repeated over and over. "The Lord is good to ALL: and his tender mercies are over ALL his works. ALL thy works shall praise thee, . . . To make known to the SONS OF MEN his mighty acts, . . . The Lord upholdeth ALL that fall, and raiseth up ALL those that be bowed down. The eye of ALL wait upon thee; . . . Thou [. . .] satisfiesth the desires of EVERY living thing. . . . The Lord is nigh unto ALL that call upon him, to ALL that call upon him in truth. . . . The Lord preserveth ALL them that love him: . . . let ALL FLESH bless his holy name."

V. 9, "The Lord is good to all: and his tender mercies are over all his works. All thy works shall praise thee." By all God's works are here meant all mankind that God hath made, for as to inanimate things, they are not capable of being the subjects of God's tender mercies. V. 10, "*All thy works shall praise thee*." This is the same thing, in other words, with Ps. 86:9, "All nations whom thou hast made shall come and worship before thee, and shall glorify thy name." In those words, "All thy works shall praise thee," the Psalmist don't only call upon all to praise God, but the words are a plain prediction that hereafter they should do so. And this prediction is introduced in these two verses in like manner, and by setting forth the same reason of it, as in Ps. 24 at the beginning, and Ps. 89:11-12 and Is. 42:4-6.

V. 13, "Thy kingdom is an everlasting kingdom, and thy dominion endures through all generations." This is agreeable to Ps. 45:6, 17, and Ps. 72:5, 7, 17, and Ps. 4:5-6, and Is. 9:7, and Ezek. 37:25, and Ps. 89:4, Jer. 33:15-17, and innumerable other prophecies that represent the kingdom of the Messiah as everlasting.

Fourteenth verse, "The Lord upholdeth all that fall he raiseth up all those that be bowed down," is agreeable to innumerable prophecies of the salvation of the Messiah as being granted to those that are in fallen, low, oppressed and helpless circumstances.

The 15th and 16th verses may either be understood as a prediction of all mankind being brought in the days of the Messiah to look to God, and wait on him for their supplies, and his granting supplies to all; or else they may be understood as a reason of that great mercy God will show to all nations in calling and accepting them, spoken of in the foregoing and following, viz. that his mercies extend to all, and even to the birds, beasts and fishes, and creeping things, in supplying their wants, and therefore much more will he be gracious to all of mankind that call upon him, though they be Gentiles.

V. 18, "The Lord is nigh to all that call upon him," etc. He is not only near to the Jews, that are said to be a people near to him; but he is near to all, of all nations, that call upon him, and he will be their God. This is agreeable to Joel 2:32,[^002-note8] "And it shall come to pass, that whosoever shall call on the name of the Lord shall be delivered."

V. 21, "let all flesh bless his name for ever and ever." These words are a key to open the meaning of the preceding part of the Psalm, from v. 8, and clearly explains the force of those universal expressions so often repeated, viz. that thereby is intended all mankind of all nations.

PSALM 146. What is said, vv. 3-4, of the vanity of man, is agreeable to what we have often elsewhere in prophecies of the Messiah's kingdom, particularly Is. 40:6-8, Ps. 60:11, Ps. 118:8-9, Jer. 3:23.

Vv. 7-8, "Which executeth judgment for the oppressed: . . . The Lord looseth the prisoners: . . . the Lord raiseth up them that are bowed down," is agreeable to the preceding Psalm, 14th verse, and to innumerable forementioned prophecies of the Messiah.

V. 8, "The Lord openeth the eyes of the blind," is agreeable to too many passages in forementioned prophecies to be now repeated.

V. 9, "The Lord preserveth "(or "saveth") "the strangers," is agreeable to all forementioned prophecies of the calling of the Gentiles.

Vv. 7, 9, he "giveth food to the hungry. . . . He relieveth the fatherless and the widow." This is agreeable to Zeph. 3:12, "I will also leave in the midst of thee an afflicted and poor people, and they shall trust in the name of the Lord," with the context; and Ps. 72:4, "He shall judge the poor of the people, he shall save the children of the needy, he shall break in pieces the oppressor"; and vv. 12-14, "For he shall deliver the needy when he crieth; the poor also, and him that hath no helper. He shall spare the poor and needy, and shall save the souls of the needy. He shall redeem their soul from deceit and violence," and many other passages that speak of the salvation of the Messiah to the poor. Hos. 14:3, "in thee the fatherless find mercy"; Is. 63:15-16, "where are [. . .] the sounding of thy bowels . . . Doubtless thou art our father, though Abraham be ignorant of us," etc.; and Is. 54:1, "Sing, O barren, . . . more are the children of the desolate than the children of the married wife"; vv. 4-5, "thou shalt forget the reproach of thy widowhood. For thy Maker is thy husband"; Jer. 3:8, "I had put her away, and given her a bill of divorce"; v. 14, "Turn, O backsliding children, saith the Lord; for I am married unto you"; Hos. 2[:2],[^002-note9] "Plead with your mother, for she is not my wife, neither am I her husband"; and in the following verses [vv. 9-11], "I will take away from her my corn [. . .], and my wine, [. . .] my wool and my flax [. . .]. I will cause all her mirth to cease"; v. 14, "I will allure her, and bring into the wilderness, and speak comfortably unto her"; v. 16, "thou shalt call me *Ishi*"; vv. 19-20, "I will betroth thee unto me"; Is. 62:4-5,[^002-note10] "Thou shalt no more be termed Forsaken; neither shall thy land be any more termed Desolate: but thou shalt be called Hephzibah, and thy land Beulah: for the Lord delighteth in thee, and thy land shall be married. . . . as the bridegroom rejoiceth over the bride, so shall the Lord rejoice over thee."

V. 10, "The Lord shall reign for ever, even thy God, O Zion, to all generations," is agreeable to v. 13 of the preceding Psalm, where, see how it is agreeable to other prophecies.

PSALM 147:2, "The Lord doth build up Jerusalem: he gathereth together the outcasts of Israel." This verse contains that which is a plain note of a prophecy of the times of the Messiah, according to other prophecies everywhere of the Messiah's salvation.

V. 3, "He healeth the broken in heart, and bindeth up their wounds." This is agreeable to Is. 61:1 and 57:15 and 66:2.

V. 4, "He telleth the number of the stars; he calleth them all by their names." By the context and other places in the Old Testament, where the saints are called "stars," 'tis probable that by stars here is meant God's people. And this signifies as much as that he knows them all by name, and will find every one, and will leave none of them in bondage and misery, agreeable to Ezek. 39:28, "I have gathered them unto their own land, and have left none of them any more there"; and Is. 43:6-7, "I will say to the north, Give up; and to the south, Keep not back: bring my sons from far, and my daughters from the ends of the earth; every one that is called by my name: for I have created him for my glory, I have formed him; yea, I have made him"; Ps. 87:6, "The Lord shall count, when he writeth up the people, that this man was born there"; Mal. 3:17, "they shall be mine [. . .] in the day that I make up my jewels."

V. 6, "The Lord lifteth up the meek: he casteth the wicked down to the ground," is agreeable to Ps. 45:4 and 22:26 and 69:32, Is. 11:4 and 29:19 and 61:1.

Vv. 8-9 are either mystical representations of the plentiful supply of God's people, even of the poorest, most miserable and most unworthy and helpless of them; or else they declare those things that are arguments to move God's people to trust in him, and evidences that he will not forsake them.

V. 10, "He delighteth not in the strength of the horse," etc. See what was observed on the preceding Psalm, 3rd and 4th verses.

In [v.] 12, Jerusalem and Zion are called upon to praise much after the same manner as in very many prophecies of the Messiah's times.

V. 13, "For he hath strengthened the bars of thy gates."
