This is agreeable to Is. 26:1, "We have a strong city; salvation will God appoint," etc.; and Ps. 48[:13], "mark well her bulwarks"; and many other prophecies that speak of the defense and safety of God's people, and their everlasting security, quietness and rest in the days of the Messiah.

V. 13, "and hath blessed thy children within thee." See Is. 61:9, "And their seed shall be known among the Gentiles, and their offspring among the people: all that see them shall acknowledge them, that they are the seed which the Lord hath blessed"; and 65:23, "for they are the seed of the blessed of the Lord, and their offspring with them."

V. 14, "He maketh peace in thy borders." This is agreeable to Hos. 2:18, "And in that day I will make a covenant for them with the beasts of the field, and with the fowls of heaven, and with the creeping things of the ground: and I will break the bow and the sword, and the battle out of the earth, and will make them to lie down safely"; and innumerable other places.

In the 14th, 15th, 16th, 17th, 18th, 19th verses, God's sending forth his word on the earth to bring his pleasure to pass, and giving his word to his people, is compared to his sending forth his wind, snow, frost and heat, very much in the same manner as in Is. 55:10-11.

In the 148TH PSALM, everything in heaven and earth, and even inanimate creatures, mountains, hills and deep places, the seas, and trees of the field, the kings and all the nations of the world, are called upon joyfully to praise God, on this great occasion of God's redeeming Israel and exalting the horn of his people, as in the last verse. Which is agreeable to many other forementioned prophecies. Is. 44:23,[^002-note11] "Sing, O ye heavens; for the Lord hath done it: shout, ye lower parts of the earth: break forth into singing, ye mountains, O forest, and every tree therein: for the Lord hath redeemed Jacob, and glorified himself in Israel"; Ps. 69:34-35, "Let the heaven and earth praise him, the seas, and every thing that moveth therein. For God will save Zion, and will build the cities of Judah: that they may dwell there, and have it in possession"; Ps. 96, "O sing unto the Lord a new song: sing unto the Lord, all the earth. [. . .] Declare his glory among the heathen, his wonders among all people. [. . .] the Lord made the heavens. [. . .] Give unto the Lord, O ye kindreds of the people, give unto the Lord glory and strength. [. . .] he shall judge the people righteously. Let the heavens rejoice, and let the earth be glad; let the sea roar, and the fullness thereof. Let the field be joyful, and all that is therein: then shall all the trees of the wood rejoice. [. . .] for [. . .] he shall judge the word with righteousness, and his people with truth"; and Ps. 97:1, "The Lord reigneth; let the earth rejoice; let the multitude of the isles be glad thereat"; and Ps. 98, vv. 3-4, "He hath remembered his mercy and his truth towards the house of Israel: all the ends of the earth have seen the salvation of our God. Make a joyful noise unto the Lord, all the earth: make a loud noise, and rejoice, and sing praise"; vv. 7-8, "Let the sea roar, and the fullness thereof; the world, and they that dwell therein. Let the floods clap their hands: let the hills be joyful together"; Is. 49:13, "Sing, O heavens; and be joyful, O earth; and break forth into singing, O mountains; for the Lord hath comforted his people, and will have mercy upon his afflicted"; Is. 42:10-11, "Sing unto the Lord a new song, and his praise from the end of the earth, ye that go down to the sea, and all that is therein; the isles, and the inhabitants thereof. Let the wilderness and the cities thereof lift up their voice, the villages that Kedar doth inhabit; let the inhabitants of the rock sing, let them shout from the top of the mountains"; Is. 55:12, "For ye shall go out with joy, and be led forth with peace: the mountains and hills shall break forth before you into singing, and all the trees of the field shall clap their hands."

PSALM 149. Israel's being called upon, in the 2nd verse, to rejoice, and Zion to "be joyful in her King," is agreeable to Zech. 9[:9], "Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion; . . . behold, thy King cometh unto thee," and innumerable other places.

V. 4, "the Lord taketh pleasure in his people," is agreeable to Is. 62:4-5, "thou shalt be called Hephzibah" (i. e. "my delight is in her"), "for the Lord delighteth in thee, . . . as the bridegroom rejoiceth over the bride, so shall the Lord rejoice over thee"; and Is. 65:19, "And I will rejoice in Jerusalem, and joy in my people"; Zeph. 3:17, "The Lord thy God in the midst of thee is mighty; he will save, he will rejoice over thee with joy; he will rest in his love, he will joy over thee with singing"; Ps. 45[:11], "So shall the king greatly desire thy beauty."

V. 4, "he will beautify the meek with salvation" (see Ps. 147:6, and what is observed upon it). This is agreeable to Is. 61:10, "I will greatly rejoice in the Lord, my soul shall be joyful in my God; for he hath clothed me with the garments of salvation, he hath covered me with the robe of righteousness, as a bridegroom decketh himself with ornaments, and as a bride adorneth herself with her jewels."

What is said, 6th, 7th, 8th, and 9th verses, speaking of the saints executing vengeance on the heathen, and binding their kings in chains, "to execute upon them the judgment written: it is said, this honor have all the saints," show beyond all dispute that the great salvation of God's people that is the subject of these Psalms, is what was to be in future ages, and therefore that these Psalms are prophetical; and also that the salvation spoken of in them, is the great salvation of the Messiah. For this great instance of God's favor to his people, to make 'em conquerors of the heathen and instruments of God's vengeance on them, and having their kings in subjection under their feet as it were, is evermore spoken of as what is to be given in the days of the Messiah, and through him: as where God speaks of making his people "a new sharp threshing instrument " [Is. 41:15], and making their horns iron and hoofs brass [Mic. 4:13], and bringing kings to come bowing down to the church to lick the dust of her feet [Is. 49:23], their coming over in chains, and falling down to her and making supplication to her, and the like. It would be endless to rehearse all that is parallel to this in other prophecies.

The 150TH PSALM is parallel with the 148th. It is the conclusion of [the] whole matter in calling upon all, of all nations, v. 6, in all ways imaginable, and to the utmost that is possible, to praise God for those mighty acts by which he accomplishes this great salvation.

N. B. 'Tis the more reasonable to suppose that this, that is eminently David's Psalm of praise for God's salvation of his church, which shuts up the book of Psalms, is concerning the great salvation of the Messiah; because he, in his last words that he uttered in the character of the sweet Psalmist of Israel, II Sam. 23, declares that this great salvation was that which his mind was wholly intent upon, and what he accounted all his salvation, as though there were no other salvation worthy to be mentioned in comparison of it. A certain great salvation of the church is manifestly the great subject of David's praise in these psalms, as appears by Ps. 145:4-6, 14, 18-20; and 146:7-9, and 147:2-3, 10-14; and 148:14, and 149:4-9. And this salvation that he here speaks of, 'tis evident, his mind is greatly swallowed up in. There surely is nothing that he ever speaks of in the book of Psalms, that his heart is more engaged [in] and his affections more raised [by]. Now, if we would know what that great salvation is that so exceedingly engages his mind and swallows up his soul in II Sam. 23:5, viz. that it was what God had revealed and promised to him of the salvation of the Messiah, whereby he should "be as the light of the morning, when the sun ariseth, as a morning without clouds; as the dew upon the grass, and as the tender grass springing out of the earth by clear shining after rain": he says of this expressly, "this is all my salvation, and all my desire." This therefore was the sum of what engaged his mind, and his affections and praises; in this he sums up, in his last words that he uttered as sweet Psalmist of Israel, all that which had been the subject of all the former words that he had uttered as Psalmist. And this is without doubt the grand subject of that which, by way of eminency, is called David's Psalm of praise, with which he ends all his divine songs, and shuts up his whole book of Psalms.

87. THE BOOK OF PSALMS, IN GENERAL, IN GREAT PART RELATES TO THE MESSIAH. Add this to §60. This is a great argument the book of Psalms is, a great part of it, prophetical, and also that very much of it relates to the times of the Messiah, even in those parts of it where the speech is in the present or preter tense, viz. that God's people are so often spoken of as being in captivity, and the temple, city and land in desolation, or as being restored from a state of captivity and desolation, in psalms that were penned at a time when no such thing was, nor had lately been.

The 9th Psalm, which [is] a Psalm of David, speaks of [the] destructions of God's people and their cities, even a long-continued desolation, so that their memorial had perished with them, and that, by their enemies that were heathen, vv. 5-6; and of God's people as having been oppressed by them, v. 9; and their blood shed, v. 12; and God's people becoming poor, afflicted and needy by means of those oppressions, v. 18; but their heathen enemies becoming exceeding proud and insolent, v. 20; but, as consequent on this, God's arising to judgment to plead the cause of his people, and utterly destroying their heathen enemies and oppressors, and delivering his people and judging the world in righteousness, and hereby giving great occasion for the rejoicing and praises of those that dwell in Zion, vv. 11, 14; and that God would thus destroy all the wicked of the earth, even all the nations that forget God, v. 17; and that God would thus arise and gloriously appear, and judge and deliver and avenge the cause of his people to their exceeding joy, after their long waiting for him, v. 18; and God's hereupon setting up an everlasting kingdom, vv. 7-8, agreeable to many of the forementioned prophecies of the Messiah. [In] that Psalm that is recorded, I Chron. 16:8-36, which David delivered to Asaph on occasion of the carrying the ark into Zion, David speaks of gathering the people together and delivering them from the heathen, v. 35.

The 14th Psalm, which is also a Psalm of David, speaks of God's looking down from [heaven] to take notice of the affairs of mankind, after [he] had seemed as it were for a long time to neglect them; and particularly to take notice of the oppressions of God's people by their enemies, whereby God's people become their prey, and were wasted and destroyed by 'em without restraint; and God's awaking as it were for the help of his people and as visibly present with them, to rebuke their enemies and put them to flight. And the last verse speaks of the salvation of Israel's coming out of Zion, and God's bringing back the CAPTIVITY of his people, that Jacob might rejoice, and Israel be glad.

So also Ps. 53, which [is] almost the same with Ps. 14. See §51.

The 44th Psalm evidently speaks of a state of desolation of the land and captivity of the people, and their dispersion in heathen lands. It speaks of God's having cast off his people, v. 9; and his leaving them to be conquered in battle and put to flight by their enemies, and their possessions being delivered into the enemies' hand as their spoil, and they themselves being given up to them as their prey, to be like "sheep for their meat," vv. 9-12; and of their being scattered among the heathen, v. 11; and their being made "a byword among the heathen, and a shaking of the head among the people," by being given up to such destruction, v. 14; and God's people's being as it were "sore broken in the place of dragons," v. 19; and this desolation long-continued, so that God seemed as it were to be asleep and to neglect his people, and as though he had forgotten them, vv. 23-24; and God's people's being in a very helpless state, v. 25. And there is all reason to think that this Psalm was penned in David's time by the title, "For *the Sons of Korah*." By which seem to be meant Heman and his sons, mention[ed], I Chron. 25:4-6, who were appointed by David to praise God by vocal music, while Asaph and Jeduthun and their sons were set over the instrumental music. There were descendants of one Korah, as appears by I Chron. 6:33-37.

The 60th Psalm, which the title plainly shows was penned by David, speaks of God's having cast off his people in anger and scattered them abroad in his anger, vv. 1 and 10; and having showed his people hard things, and made them "to drink the wine of astonishment," and giving their enemies the victory in battle, vv. [3,] 10. But these times by no means agree to the time wherein it appears that this Psalm was penned, by the title. The Psalm also speaks of a restoration of the land of Israel, God's owning it anew as his inheritance, and its being anew divided to Israel for their inheritance, v. 6, etc.

Ps. 66 (which there is reason to think to be one of David's, being amongst his psalms) speaks of God's proving his people and trying them "as silver is tried," by bringing them into the net and laying affliction on their loins, and causing men to ride over their heads, and leading his people "through fire and through water," vv. 10-12.

Ps. 68, which the title says is a Psalm of David, speaks of God's leading captivity captive, delivering those that had been carried into captivity for their rebellion against God, v. 18; and bringing his people again from Bashan, and "from the depth of the sea," v. 22.

Ps. 69, which is a Psalm of David by the title, speaks of God's regarding his prisoners, v. 33; and saving Zion, and rebuilding the cities of Judah and restoring them to the possession of his people, that they may dwell there again after they had been removed from thence, vv. 35-36.

Ps. 74, which is a Psalm of Asaph (and therefore penned in David's or Solomon's time, as appears by I Chron. 25:1-6 and ch. 6:31-44), speaks of God's anger smoking against the people of his pasture and his casting them off, v. 1; and of Zion and the sanctuary being perpetual desolation, vv. 2-3; and the enemies roaring and setting up their ensign in the place where God's congregation used to meet, and their breaking down the carved work of the sanctuary with axes and hammers, and burning up the sanctuary, and God's defiling it by levelling it with the ground, and the enemies burning up all the synagogues of God in the land, and there being no signs and no prophet for a long time amongst the people during their long-continued desolation, and the enemies reproaching and blaspheming God's name, and God's neglecting his people, and as it were hiding his hand in his bosom, doing nothing to help them, vv. 3-11; and long forgetting the congregation of the poor and oppressed, while they were subject to the cruelty and reproaches of those that dwelt in "the dark places of the earth," vv. 20-22.

Ps. 79, which is a Psalm of Asaph, speaks of the heathen's coming into God's inheritance and defiling God's holy temple and laying Jerusalem in heaps, and giving the dead bodies of God's people to the fowls of heaven and the beasts of the earth, and shedding their blood like water round about Jerusalem, and there being none to bury them, and their becoming a reproach to their neighbors and a scorn and derision to [them] that were round about 'em, and God's continuing these fruits of his great displeasure very long, vv. 1-5; and the heathen's devouring Jacob and laying waste his dwelling place, vv. 6-7; and v. 11 speaks of God's delivering his prisoners.

Ps. 80, a Psalm of Asaph, speaks of God's appearing, after he had hid himself, to turn his people again, and lead 'em like a flock and save 'em from their enemies, in like manner as he did when he redeemed them out of Egypt, vv. 1-3; and vv. 4-6 speak of God's long manifesting his anger against his people and stopping his ears against their prayers, and making them the derision of their neighbors, by bring[ing] upon them upon them unmeasurable affliction; vv. 12-13 speak of God's breaking down the hedges of the vine he had planted, that all that pass by the way might pluck her, and "the boar out of the wood" wasting it, and "the wild beast of the field" devouring it; and v. 16, of its being burnt with fire, and cut down and perished at the rebuke of God's countenance.

The 85th Psalm--this is "A Psalm for the Sons of Korah"--speaks of God's bringing back the captivity of Jacob, v. 1, and this after a very long-continued manifestation of God's great displeasure, vv. 5-6.

The 102nd Psalm speaks of the stones and dust, i. e. the ruins of Jerusalem, that God's servants take pleasure in, v. 14; and of God's favoring Zion, and building up Jerusalem, and hearing the cry of his destitute people after long waiting, vv. 13, 16-17; and God's looking down from heaven to take notice of the calamities of his people, and "hear the groaning of the prisoner," after he had seemed long to neglect them, vv. 19-20. And this Psalm in these things seems to declare itself prophetical, or speaking of what should be in future generations, v. 18.

The 107th Psalm speaks of redeeming God's people, that were scattered in foreign lands, from the east, west, north and south, vv. 2-3; and of God's delivering those that were in bonds, in "darkness and the shadow of death," and having their heart brought down with hard service or bondage because they had rebelled against the Lord and contemned the counsel of the Most High, and God's breaking the gates of brass and cutting asunder the bars of iron, vv. 11-14, 16; and of turning "a fruitful land into barrenness, for the wickedness of them that dwell therein," v. 34; and again delivering them and turning "the wilderness into a standing water, and the dry ground into water springs," and there making "the hungry to dwell, that they may prepare a city for habitation, and sow fields, and plant vineyards," and increasing and multiplying them greatly, and setting "the poor on high from affliction" and making them "families like a flock," vv. 35-38, 41.

The 108 Psalm, which is said in the title to be a Psalm of David,speaks of God's restoring the land of Israel and acknowledging it anew as his inheritance, and dividing it out anew to his people, triumphing over the enemies of Israel, and giving them help from trouble after he had cast them [off] and given their enemies the conquest of them, v. 6 to the end.

David's song of praise in the six last psalms, speaks of God's executing judgment for the oppressed, and loosing the prisoners, and raising up them that are bowed down, Ps. 146:7-8; and his building up Jerusalem and gathering together the outcasts of Israel, Ps. 147:2; and his strengthening the bars of the gates of Jerusalem, v. 13; and exalting the horn of his people, 148:14; and granting to them "to execute vengeance on the heathen," and "bind their kings in chains, and their nobles with fetters of iron," 149:7-9.

And we can't conclude that the 126th and 134th Psalms, that speak plainly of the captivity of Israel as a thing present or past: I say, we can't conclude from the subject matter of these psalms, that they were written in the time of the captivity, or after it; any more the 74th and 79th Psalms, that speak no less expressly of--yea, more particularly of--the captivity and desolation of the land, that, we are told, were penned by Asaph. Those expressions in the beginning of Ps. 126, "When the Lord turned again the captivity of Zion, we were like them that dream. Then was our mouth filled with laughter, and our tongue with singing," is very parallel with those words of David, Ps. 14:7 and 53:6, "When the Lord bringeth back the captivity of his people, Jacob shall rejoice, and Israel shall be glad."

Again, 'tis an argument that the penmen of the psalms, when they speak in the first person singular, have not a chief respect to themselves personally, but speak in the name of the Messiah, or of the church of God; because the penmen of those psalms, when they wrote them, they wrote them for public use, or for the use of the church of Israel in their public praises in the sanctuary. And this is evident by their inscriptions: they are inscribed and directed in their titles, very many of them, to the musicians that were in the sanctuary. And that these inscriptions were prefixed to the psalms by the penmen themselves, may be argued from this, that some of them are inscribed to principal musicians in the sanctuary, by name, that, it appears by the Scripture history, were living at that time and were contemporary with the writers of the psalms; as particularly, Ps. 39 and 62, that were psalms of David, are[^002-note12] inscribed to Jeduthun, who was one of the leading musicians that David himself had appointed for the public praises of Israel in the sanctuary, as appears by I Chron. 25:1-6. So is Ps. 77, that is a Psalm of Asaph, inscribed to Jeduthun, that was Asaph's contemporary. And as there is such plain reason to conclude that the inscription of these psalms were by the penmen them[selves], so there is no otherwise of such kind of inscriptions as are prefixed to other psalms.

Now it is not to be supposed that David, and Asaph and others, would write their own personal prayer, containing their private complaints and things concerning them only as particular persons, and send 'em to the temple to be sung there as the voice of the whole congregation of Israel to God, in their most public and solemn worship.

Again, as to some of the psalms, wherein the Psalmist speaks in the first person, the very matter of the psalm plainly shows that the Psalmist don't speak in his own name, but in the name of the church of God, or of the head of the church; because in the same psalm wherein the Psalmist speaks of his affliction, distress and his cries for deliverance, he speaks of Zion's deliverance and prosperity as that deliverance that he speaks of, and as an answer to those cries. This is very manifest particularly in the 102nd Psalm and Ps. 66, compare the former part with the latter, beginning with v. 13; and Ps. 22, observe especially vv. 23-24 and 26.[^002-note13]

See this argument more fully handled in "Supplement to Prophecies of the Messiah" at the beginning of this book, no. 1, and also other arguments.[^002-note14]

88. HOSEA 5:15 AND 6:1-3. That prophecy that we have in Hos. 5:15 and 6:1-3, is a prophecy of what shall be accomplished in the days of the Messiah: for it is a promise of the restoration of Israel from that desolation threatened and foretold in the prediction immediately preceding, in the 4th and 5th chapters; and therefore there is all manner of reason to suppose that it is the same restoration of Israel that is spoken of in the 1st, 2nd and 3rd chapters, which I have before shown to be that which [is] accomplished by the Messiah. In those chapters, there is first a threatening and description of desolation that God will bring on the land, and then follows a promise of a restoration, as here; and 'tis evident that the desolation that is there described, and which there we have a promise of restoration from, is the very same with the desolation described here,[^002-note15] of which these verses contain a promise of restoration from. The desolation is spoken of in like manner. The mother of the children of Israel is spoken of as being destroyed, ch. 4:5; so ch. 2:2-5, etc. This desolation is spoken of as an utter desolation, ch. 4, v. 3; so ch. 2:6-14. The sin for which the desolation is threatened is the same, and spoken of in the same manner, viz. whoredom of the church of Israel against God her husband. Compare ch. 4:10 to the end and ch. 5:3-4, 7. 'Tis here in like manner represented that God shall begin to afflict and punish the children of Israel, as seeking in vain for help from her lovers. Compare ch. 5:13 with ch. 2:6-7. Judah and Ephraim, or Israel, are both spoken of distinctly in the threatenings of desolation here, as in the foregoing chapters; and it is foretold here that the desolation of Israel or Ephraim shall be first, and that after Ephraim is destroyed, Judah shall receive great favor from God, ch. 4:15-17, as in ch. 1:6-7.

Nothing can be more evident than that it is the same destruction that is threatened here as in the preceding chapters, and therefore undoubtedly the restoration here spoken of from the same desolation, is the same restoration. In the preceding chapters we see the manner of the Prophet, first to foretell the people's desolation, and then to foretell their restoration by the Messiah; and then to return again and speak of the same desolation, and then to speak again of the redemption of the Messiah; and so again and again. And so 'tis evident he goes on to do in these chapters, which are a continuation of and further amplification of the same prophecies.

And further, the restoration is represented in the same manner: first, by their way being hedged up, and the people being brought to see that none could help them, and despairing in the help of such as they sought help from (compare ch. 5:13-14 with ch. 2:6-7); and their being brought earnestly to seek God by sore affliction, and so having comforts restored (compare ch. 5:14-15 and [ch.] 6, at the beginning, with ch. 2:14-15); their hope restored in a way of their putting away sin the Achan, or troubler, and returning from sin to God (compare ch. 5:15, ch. 6:1 with ch. 2:15.

The restoration of God's people here foretold is represented as a resurrection from the dead, ch. 6:2, as is very frequent in prophecies of the restoration of the Messiah. God's going forth at this time is represented as the rising of the morning, agreeable to II Sam. 23[:4], "he shall be as the light of the morning"; and Is. 60 at the beginning; and Mal. 4[:2], "the Sun of righteousness shall arise," etc.; Ps. 46:5, "God shall help her, when the morning appears," as in the original. Ch. 6:3, it is said, "he shall come unto us as the rain, as the latter and former rain unto the earth," which is agreeable to David's last words, "as the dew," etc.; and Ps. 72[:6], "He shall come down as the rain on the mown grass."

89. DANIEL 2:34-35, 44-45. 'Tis the kingdom of the Messiah that is prophesied of, Dan. 2:34-35, 44-45. V. 44, "in the days of these kings shall the God of heaven set up a kingdom." This is agreeable to innumerable forementioned prophecies that speak of God's reigning, the Lord himself being king, the Lord's sitting king, the Lord's judging among the nations, the Lord's reigning in Mt. Zion and Jerusalem "before his ancients gloriously," so "the moon shall be confounded, and the sun ashamed" [Is. 24:23]; the Lord's being the king and judge and captain of his people, and the kingdom's being the Lord's; the Lord's sending forth his law out of Zion [Mic. 4:2]; God's overturning till he comes whose right it is to wear the diadem, and its being given him [Ezek. 21:26-27]; the kingdom's being the Lord's, and his being the governor among the nations [Ps. 22:28], etc.

What is said here, vv. 35, 45, of all other kingdoms that stand in opposition to this kingdom, of being broken in pieces together, and becoming like "the chaff of the summer threshing floors," and the winds carrying them away, that no place should be found for them, is agreeable to Is. 41:15-16, "Behold, I will make thee a new sharp threshing instrument having teeth: thou shalt thresh the mountains, and beat them small, and make the hills as chaff. Thou shalt fan them, and the wind shall carry them away, and the whirlwind shall scatter them"; Mic. 4:13, "Arise and thresh, O daughter of Zion: for I will make thine horn iron, and thy hoofs brass: and thou shalt beat in pieces many people"; Ps. 2[:9], "Thou shalt rule them with a rod of iron; as the vessels of the potter shall they be broken to shivers"; Ps. 110[:1-2, 5-6], "Sit thou on my right hand, till I make thine enemies thy footstool. The Lord shall send the rod of thy strength out of Zion: rule thou in the midst of thine enemies. . . . The Lord at thy right hand shall strike through kings in the day of his wrath. He shall judge among the heathen, he shall fill the places with the dead bodies; he shall wound the heads over many countries"; Is. 54:15, "whosoever shall gather together against thee shall fall for thy sake"; Is. 60[:12], "For the nation and kingdom that will not serve thee shall perish; yea, those nations shall be utterly wasted"; and innumerable other places.

This kingdom of God's becoming great and filling the whole earth, is agreeable to what is elsewhere said of God's being king over all the earth, and the kingdom's being the Lord's, and his being the governor among the nations [Ps. 22:28]; the isles waiting for his law [Is. 42:4]; every knee's bowing and every tongue's confessing to him [Rom. 14:11]; God's reigning over the nations, his reigning in the earth, his judging among the nations; all nations being brought to bow down, and all kings bowing down to him, and all nations serving him; and innumerable such like expressions.

"Rabbi Levi Ben Gerson tells us, that that stone, by the blow whereof that image which represented the empires, should be broken to pieces, was the Messiah. Rabbi Solomon, R. Abenesdras, and R. Saaida say, that that kingdom which would consume the rest of the kingdoms, was the kingdom of the Messiah." Grotius, *de Verit*., Bk. 5, §14, marginal note.[^002-note16]

This kingdom of God's standing forever is agreeable to Is. 9:7, "Of the increase of his government and peace there shall be no end, upon the throne of David, and upon his kingdom, to order it, and to establish it with judgment and with justice from henceforth even for ever"; Ezek. 37:25, "my servant David shall be their prince for ever"; Ps. 72:5, "They shall fear thee as long as the sun and the moon shall endure, throughout all generations"; v. 7, "In his days shall the righteous flourish; and abundance of peace so long as the moon endureth"; [v.] 15, "he shall live, and to him shall be given of the gold of Sheba: prayer also shall be made for him continually; and daily shall he be praised"; [v.] 17, "His name shall endure for ever: his name shall be continued as long as the sun: and men shall be blessed in him: all nations shall[^002-note17] call him blessed"; Ps. 145:13, "Thy kingdom is an everlasting kingdom, and thy dominion endureth throughout all generations"; and 146:10, "The Lord shall reign for ever, even thy God, O Zion, unto all generations"; Mic. 4:1-3, 7, "All nations shall flow into it. . . . many nations shall say, . . . the law shall go forth of Zion, . . . And he shall judge among many people, . . . and the Lord shall reign over them in mount Zion from henceforth, even for ever"; and there are innumerable other places of forementioned prophecies that imply the same thing.

90. DANIEL 7:9-14, 18, 22, 26-28. In the 7th chapter of Daniel, we have a very remarkable prophecy of the Messiah and his kingdom. 'Tis manifestly the same kingdom that the God of heaven shall set up, spoken of in the 2nd chapter: for, as there, 'tis a kingdom that God himself shall set up, vv. 9-10, 22, 27; and a kingdom that he shall set up after the four monarchies, as there, and a kingdom set up in the days of the ten kings that should be in the latter end of the fourth monarchy, as there, vv. 7-9, and also vv. 20-22 and vv. 24-26. And a kingdom shall destroy the other kingdoms, as there; see v. [9], "I beheld till the thrones were cast down, and the Ancient of days did sit"; and vv. 11-12, "I beheld even till the beast was slain, and his body destroyed, and given to the burning flame. As concerning the rest of the beasts, they had their dominion taken away"; v. 26, "But the judgment shall sit, and they shall take away his dominion, to consume and to destroy it to the end." And it is a kingdom that fills the whole earth, as that, v. 14, "And there was given him dominion, and glory, and a kingdom, that all people, nations, and languages, should serve him"; and v. 27, "And the kingdom and dominion, and the greatness of the kingdom under the whole heaven, [. . .] and all dominions shall serve and obey him." 'Tis an everlasting kingdom, as that, see v. 14, "his dominion is an everlasting dominion, which shall not pass away, and his kingdom that which shall not be destroyed"; v. 18, "But the saints of the most High shall take the kingdom, and possess the kingdom for ever, even for ever and ever"; v. 27, "whose kingdom is an everlasting kingdom." And therefore, see in the foregoing section for parallel places in other prophecies that represent those things of the kingdom of the Messiah.

And whereas the duration of this kingdom, and of the saints' enjoyment of the blessings of it, is spoken, v. 18, as everlasting in the highest and most absolute sense, in such terms as these, "But the saints of the most High shall take the kingdom, and shall possess the kingdom for ever, even for ever and ever," which must be understood of an absolute eternity and a duration therefore extending beyond the end of these heavens and earth; this is agreeable to Is. 55:6 and Is. 54:7-10 and 65:17-18, together with ch. 66:22, Ps. 102:26-28.

Here, the person that is to appointed as king over the kingdom of God is said to be "one like the Son of man," v. 13.[^002-note18] This is agreeable to what is elsewhere said: "a virgin shall conceive, and bring forth a son" [Is. 7:14]; and, "to us a child is born, to us a son is given" [Is. 9:6]; "the MAN whose name is the Branch" [Zech. 6:2]; and the Messiah's being often represented as of the seed of David, etc.

Though here he is represented as man, yet he is also represented as more than man, as one that had the divine nature and glory, for he comes "with clouds of heaven" [Dan. 7:13], that were the symbol of the divine glory; which is agreeable to what is said of the man whose name is the Branch in Zechariah [6:13], "he shall bear the glory," and all those prophecies that represent him as both God and man, and where he is called Immanuel, "a child born, and a son given," and yet the everlasting Father, the Branch of the Lord, and the fruit of the earth.

And whereas here 'tis represented that the saints of the Most High shall take the kingdom and reign over the whole world, this is agreeable to other prophecies that speak of the set time to favor Zion [Ps. 102:13], and subduing all under God's people, and causing all nations to serve them, to bow down to them and lick the dust of their feet [Is. 49:23], and their binding kings in chains [Ps. 149:8], their making supplication to them [Is. 45:14], etc.

"The great Hebrew doctors, such as Solomon Jarchi, Rabbi Josue, quoted by Abenesdras and Saadias, agree that the Son of man in Daniel, is the Messiah." Rabbi Josue lived at the time of the destruction of Jerusalem. Grotius, *de Verit*., Bk. 5, §14, marginal note. And Rabbi Levi Ben Gerson and Saaida affirm the Son of man in Daniel to be the messiah. Ibid., another marginal note.[^002-note19]

91. DANIEL 9:24 TO THE END. I am come now to consider that great prophecy of the Messiah in the 9th chapter of Daniel, [v.] 24 to the end, which is one of the most remarkable and plain of all in the Old Testament. I have hitherto called the person spoken of in forementioned prophecies "the Messiah," for distinction's sake, meaning thereby no more than the great king that was spoken of in prophecies mentioned before; which person, in some of the forementioned prophecies, is called God's Messiah or Anointed. But it now remains to be shown that the person here called by the name of the Messiah or the Anointed, as his proper and distinguishing name, is the same great king so often spoken of in prophecies that have been already taken notice of.

And here, first, the name of the MESSIAH or the Anointed. If we consider what must be understood by such a name, according to the common acceptation and use of such like appellations in the Old Testament, viz. some person chosen and singled out by God to some great office, dignity and honor, and for some eminent service and benefit to God's Israel, and called and appointed of God to that dignity and work, and peculiarly fitted of him for it: but who can that great person be that is foretold in this prophecy, as one that should arise in ages to come in Israel, that is in these respects eminently and above all others called the Messiah or the Anointed of God, but the person spoken of in forementioned prophecies? What other great person that was to arise in Israel in after ages, could there be more worthy of such a name, than that great king so often spoken of in other prophecies as the greatest king of Israel, their greatest deliverer and redeemer, captain and protector, the grand instrument of their good and minister of divine blessings to them, spoken of all along and revealed by God as the grand promise to that people from the first calling of Abraham to that time; that is called God's elect, in whom his soul delights, Is. 42:1; God's servant, whom he had chosen, Is. 43:10; to whom God had said, "Thou art my servant, in whom I will be glorified," and one that God had formed "from the womb to be his servant, to bring Jacob again to him, [. . .] to raise up the tribes of Jacob, and to restore the preserved of Israel, and to be "for a light to the Gentiles" and God's "salvation to the ends of the earth," and one that God would choose and would give for "a covenant of the people, to establish the earth," and one that God called from the womb and made mention of his name from the bowels of his mother, Is. 49:1-9; one that God had called in righteousness, and would give "for a covenant of the people, and light of the Gentiles," Is. 42:6; and one that was in an eminent manner his servant and the messenger whom he sent, and one that is God's king that he had set on his holy hill of Zion, and that by an immutable decree he had appointed to the dignity of his son, Ps. 2; and that was the stone that God had laid in Zion, Is. 28; and the son of man whom he had made strong for himself, Ps. 80:15, 17; one that God had found, and chosen, and exalted out of the people to be his servant, and that he would make his first born, higher than the kings of the earth? If there was any other greater person that God intended to raise up for greater benefit to his people, and more worthy on these accounts to be called, by way of eminency, THE MESSIAH or THE ANOINTED, why are all other prophecies silent about him?

Again, if we consider what those offices were to which persons were introduced by anointing among the Jews, it will confirm that he that is spoken of in forementioned prophecies, is this person here spoken, to whom belongs, in a peculiar manner and above all others, the name of the ANOINTED. And they were kings and priests and prophets, but the Messiah is ever spoken of as *a King*, and *THE KING*, *THE GREAT KING* that God had appointed to have the dominion over his people and over all nations, and to have it alone, and to have it forever. And he is spoken of also as the *great priest* of God, *the high priest*, a priest not only coming before the throne of God in the Holy of Holies once a year, as the high priests of old, but a priest on God's throne, Zech. 6; and consecrated and established in the priesthood immediately by God himself, and with remarkable solemnity, and that not only to be above all other priests but to be priest forever, to have no successor, so that there should be no high priest but he--Ps. 110[:4], "The Lord hath sworn, and will not repent, Thou art a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek." And also the grand PROPHET that God would raise up in Israel like to Moses [Acts 3:22], and far above Moses. See §44.

Again, if we consider what the Old Testament teaches us was signified by the anointing oil, it further confirms that the person spoken of in forementioned prophecies is this great person here foretold that is, by way of eminency, called the Anointed: and that is the Spirit of God. 'Tis evident that the Spirit of God was signified by oil of old. It is apparent by Zech. 4. There, Zechariah has a vision of two olive trees conveying their oil through golden pipes into the bowl of the candlestick; and the angel, in the 5th and 6th verses, explains this vision to Zechariah as signifying the communication of the Spirit of God to the church of Israel, to help them and cause them to prosper in the affairs of the temple, and his worship and service. "Then the angel that talked with me answered and said unto me, Knowest thou not what these be? And I said, No, my lord. Then he answered and spoke unto me, saying, This is the word of the Lord unto Zerubbabel, saying, Not by might, nor by power, but by my spirit, saith the Lord of hosts." And it is evident, by Is. 61:1, that by anointing a person to an office, was signified the qualifying of him for his office, by putting God's spirit on him: "The Spirit of the Lord God is upon me; because the Lord hath anointed me." But he that is spoken of in forementioned prophecies, is spoken of as one that should, in an eminent and peculiar manner, have [the] Spirit of God put upon [him], as in the last mentioned text, Is. 61:1, "The Spirit of the Lord God is upon me; because the Lord hath anointed me"; and so Is. 11:2, "the spirit of the Lord shall rest upon him, the spirit of wisdom and understanding, the spirit of counsel and of might, the spirit of knowledge and of the fear of the Lord"; and Is. 42:1, "Behold my servant, whom I uphold; mine elect, in whom my soul delighteth; I have put my spirit upon him"; Is. 59:19-21, "the spirit of the Lord God shall lift up a standard against him. And the Redeemer shall come to Zion," etc. "As for me, this is my covenant with them, [. . .] My spirit that is upon thee, and my words which I have put in thy mouth," etc.

Again, there is no person that we read of, in all the history of the Old Testament, that seems to be spoken as being so eminently the Lord's "Anointed," as "David," and therefore who is so likely to be the person intended by the great, future prince of God's people here spoken of, that is called, by way of eminency, THE ANOINTED, THE PRINCE, as that greatest of all kings, so often foretold in other prophecies and spoken of there under the name of david; being very often called by that name, as being the great antitype of David, and is called the "rod out of the stem of Jesse," and the Branch "out of his roots" [Is. 11:1]; and the righteous Branch growing up unto David [Jer. 33:15], and a Branch out of his roots; and the great king that shall sit on the throne of his father David [^002-note20]

Lastly, the person spoken of in forementioned prophecies is expressly said to be anointed, and to be anointed above other princes, and one whom God hath anointed, and is spoken of as he that is by way of eminency God's ANOINTED or MESSIAH, as Is. 61:1, "the Lord hath anointed me"; Ps. 45:7, "thy God hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows"; Ps. 2:[1-]2, "Why [do] the heathen rage [. . .] against the Lord, and against his Messiah," or "anointed"; v. 6, "Yet have I anointed my king on my holy hill of Zion" (as it is in the original); Ps. 20:6, "Now I know that the Lord saveth his anointed"; Ps. 89:20, "I have found David my servant; with my holy oil have I anointed him."

And as the person here prophesied of is called the Messiah, so he is called THE PRINCE. The word is dygin;, which properly signifies "leader." The word is used in Scripture to signify the same as "king," as I Kgs. 14:7, "Go, tell Jeroboam, Thus saith the Lord God of Israel, Forasmuch as I exalted thee from among the people, and made thee prince over my people Israel," where the word in the original is the same as here. So I Kgs. 16:1-2, "Then the word of the Lord came to Jehu the son of Hanani against Baasha, saying, Forasmuch as I exalted thee out of the dust, and made thee prince over my people Israel"; and Prov. 28:16, "The prince that wanteth understanding is also a great oppressor." But who can this great, anointed prince be but that same great king everywhere spoken of by foregoing prophets, that God should raise to deliver and reign over his people in the latter days?

In the angel's manner of speaking here, there is implied a plain reference to foregoing prophecies of this Messiah, the prince, that he speaks of. The names are made use as names well known already, and the person is spoken of as a person that Daniel and God's people expected from foregoing prophecies; and he only declares to him how long it will before he will appear, and what will follow upon it. Daniel had the books of the foregoing prophets in his hands, as appears by v. 2, where he read much of a certain great king or prince that God would anoint and send for the salvation of his people. He had been earnestly seeking to God for the redemption and salvation of his people. God, in answer and great favor to him as a man greatly beloved, makes known to him for his encouragement something more particularly concerning the coming of this glorious redeemer and head of prosperity and happiness of his people than had hitherto been known, and particularly concerning the time of his coming. But what other great, future, anointed prince and savior of Israel is foretold or promised by foregoing prophets, but he that is spoken of in those prophecies that I have already observed? The Messiah is called by the same name, dygin;, in Isaiah. Is. 55:4, "Behold, I have given him for a witness of the people, a leader and commander of the people." The word translated "leader" is the same as here.

There is probably also some reference to what God had before revealed to Daniel. Daniel before had had a vision, ch. 7, of a glorious prince, "one like the Son of man" coming with clouds of heaven to the seat of "the Ancient of days," and of their being given to him by that Ancient of days "dominion and glory and a kingdom, that all people, nations and languages should serve him"; whose dominion "is an everlasting dominion, which shall not pass away, and his kingdom that which shall not be destroyed"; under whom "the people of the saints of the most High God" should possess "the kingdom and dominion, and greatness of the kingdom under the whole heaven," and should reign forever, even forever and ever. Doubtless, this glorious vision deeply impressed and very much took up Daniel's mind, who was so concerned for the welfare of God's people. And therefore when the angel tells him how long it will be till the great anointed prince appears, he must naturally understand it of this great prince that he had this vision [of]. And indeed, the vision leaves no room for any other prince over God's people in their future prosperous day: for he is represented as reigning alone absolutely over all the inhabitants, and reigning forever without his kingdom's ever passing away, or ever having any succession.

And besides, 'tis signified to Daniel at this time that this that is here foretold, even the coming of this Messiah, the prince, and what he should accomplish at his coming, was the grand event so often exhibited in foregoing visions and prophecies, and that had been the main subject of prophecies. "Seventy weeks are determined . . . to bring in everlasting righteousness, and to seal up the vision and prophecy, and to anoint the most Holy" [v. 24]; "to seal up the vision and prophecy," i. e. to finish it, or bring the prophecy to its grand event and accomplishment, in that which it chiefly and ultimately aimed at, and to accomplish that which is as it were the sum of all prophecy. Ezek. 28:12, "Thou sealest up the sum, full of wisdom, and perfect in beauty."

The Messiah's being here called "the most Holy," is agreeable to Ps. 16, "nor suffer thine Holy One to see corruption"; Ps. 45:7, "Thou lovest righteousness, and hatest wickedness: therefore God, thy God, {hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellow}"; his being called God's righteous servant [Is. 53:11], and being often said to be "just, ruling in the fear of God" [II Sam. 23:3], reigning in righteousness, being the righteous Branch [Jer. 33:15], God's being "well pleased for his righteousness' sake" [Is. 42:21]; his having "done no violence," nor guile being found in his mouth [Is. 53:9], and his having a pure heart and clean hands [Ps. 24:4], etc.

That, v. 24, "to finish the transgression, and to make an end of sins, and to make reconciliation for iniquity," is agreeable to Jer. 31:34, "for I will forgive their iniquity, and remember their sin no more"; and 33:8, "And I will cleanse them from all their iniquity, whereby they have sinned against me; and I will pardon all their iniquities, whereby they have sinned, and whereby they have transgressed against me"; Mic. 7:19, "he will subdue our iniquities; and thou wilt cast all their all their sins into the depth of the sea"; Is. 43:25, "I, even I, am he that blotteth out thy transgressions [. . .], and will not remember thy sins"; and Is. 44:22, "I have blotted out, as a thick cloud, thy transgressions, and, as a cloud, thy sins"; and many other places.

"And to bring in everlasting righteousness." Here seems to be a reference to what had been often foretold by the former prophets, concerning a future bringing in of everlasting righteousness, probably in such passages as those in Is. 56:1, "my salvation is near to come, and my righteousness to be revealed"; and 46:13, "I will bring near my righteousness; it shall not be far off, and my salvation shall not tarry: and I will place salvation in Zion for Israel my glory"; and 51:5-6, "My righteousness is near; my salvation is gone forth, . . . the heavens shall vanish away like smoke, and the earth shall wax old like a garment, and they that dwell therein shall die in like manner: but my salvation shall be for ever, and my righteousness shall not be abolished"; and v. 8, "For the moth shall eat them up like a garment, and the worm shall eat them like wool: but my righteousness shall be for ever, and my salvation from generation to generation"; and 32:17, "the work of righteousness shall be peace; and the effect of righteousness quietness and assurance for ever"; and 61:11, "For as the earth bringeth forth her bud, and as the garden causeth the things that are sown in it to spring forth; so the Lord will cause righteousness and praise to spring forth before all nations"; and in the next verse, viz. ch. 62:1, "For Zion's sake will I not hold my peace, and for Jerusalem's sake I will not rest, until the righteousness thereof go forth as brightness, and the salvation thereof as a lamp that burneth"; Ps. 85:10-11, "righteousness and peace have kissed each other. Truth shall spring out of the earth; and righteousness shall look down from heaven"; v. 13, "Righteousness shall go before him; and shall set us in the way of his steps." And if we take the righteousness spoken of here, that the Messiah brings in, more especially for that which is reckoned to his people in their justification, which he brings in, as well as makes an end of sins and makes reconciliation for iniquity, this is agreeable to Is. 53:11, "by his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many; for he shall bear their iniquities"; and Jer. 23:5-6, "and this is the name by which he shall be called, The Lord Our Righteousness"; and Is. 45:24-25, "Surely, one shall say, in the Lord have I righteousness and strength: . . . In the Lord shall all the seed of Israel be justified, and shall glory."

If we take finishing the transgression, and making an end of sins, and bringing in everlasting righteousness, for the taking away sin itself from God's people and giving them everlasting, inherent righteousness by sanctification, this is agreeable to Jer. 32:39-40, "And I will give them one heart, and one way, that they may fear me FOR EVER, for the good of them, and their children after them: . . . I will put my fear in their hearts, and they shall not depart from me"; and ch. 31:33-34, "But this shall be the covenant that I will make with the house of Israel; After those days, saith the Lord, I will put my law in their inward parts, and write it in their hearts; and will be their God, and they shall be my people. And they shall teach no man every man his neighbor," etc. To the like purpose, Ezek. 11:19-20 and Ezek. 36:25-27, "Then will I sprinkle clean water upon you, and ye shall be clean: from all your filthiness, and from all your idols, will I cleanse you. A new heart also will I give you, and a new spirit will I put within you: and I will take away the stony heart out of your flesh, and I will give you an heart of flesh. And I will put my spirit within you, and cause you to walk in my statutes, and ye shall keep my judgments, and do them"; v. 29, "I will also save you from all your uncleanness." See also ch. 37:23, and Is. 32:16-17 and 45:8 and 61:3, and innumerable other places.

In this prophecy 'tis foretold that, about the end of the seventy weeks, reconciliation should be made for iniquity and the Messiah should "be cut off, but not for himself," and that he should so make an end of sin, and finish the transgression, and complete reconciliation for iniquity, that he should make "the sacrifice and oblation to cease," vv. 24, 26-27. 'Tis agreeable to other prophecies that he should be cut off, or die. [This] is agreeable to Ps. 22:15, "thou hast brought me into the dust of death," and is implied in other prophecies where it is said, "my flesh shall rest in hope" [Ps. 16:9], etc., "Thy dead men shall live, with my dead body shall they come" [Is. 26:19]. By this expression, the Messiah shall be "cut off," is implied that he should die, and also that he should die a violent death, which is agreeable to Is. 53, where the great, future Savior foretold by Isaiah, his dying a violent and terrible death, is very plainly and fully foretold. V. 5, "he was wounded" and "bruised"; v. 7, "He was oppressed, and he was afflicted, . . . he is brought as a lamb to the slaughter"; v. 8, "He is taken from prison and from judgment: . . . he was cut off out of the land of the living," where the like expression is used, as here in this place in Daniel--he was "*cut off*"; v. 9, "he made his grave with the wicked, and with the rich in his death"; v. 10, "it pleased the Lord to bruise him; and put him to grief: . . . thou shalt make his soul an offering for sin"; v. 12, "he hath poured out his soul unto death."

It is said that he should be "cut off, not for himself," i. e. for others, as a sacrifice and oblation, making an end of sin and making reconciliation for iniquity. This is agreeable to Is. 53, in a great variety of expressions there.

And whereas here 'tis intimated that this sacrifice for sin should put an end to all other sacrifices and oblations, and cause the sacrifices of Moses' Law to cease: this necessarily follows from other prophecies, for 'tis there foretold that he should be priest forever, and should have no successor; and in the 53rd of Isaiah 'tis foretold that his own blood, or life, or soul is the sacrifice that this priest should offer for sin, and that this sacrifice should be acceptable with God as a satisfaction for sin, so that we should have peace by it, and should be healed, v. 5; and that "the iniquity of us all" should be laid upon him, v. 6; and that he should justify many, because he should bear their iniquities, v. 11. And 'tis there implied that he should offer this sacrifice but once; for 'tis promised, vv. 10-11, that if he should make his soul once "an offering for sin," he should thenceforward "prolong his days" and should "see of the travail of his soul, and be satisfied." And 'tis in other prophecies implied that the sacrifices of the Law of Moses should cease in the Messiah's days, as Is. 66 at the beginning, "Thus saith the Lord, The heaven is my throne, . . . where is the house that ye build unto me? . . . He that killeth an oxen," etc.; Ps. 50:7, etc., "Hear, O my people, and I will speak; O Israel, and I will testify against thee: I am God, even thy God. I will not reprove thee for thy sacrifices or thy burnt offerings, to have been continually before me. I will take no bullock out of thy house, nor he goats out of thy folds. . . . Will I eat the flesh of bulls, or drink the blood of goats?"; Mic. 6:6-8, "Wherewith shall I come before the Lord, and bow myself before the high God? shall I come before him with burnt offerings, with calves of a year old? Will the Lord be pleased with thousands of rams," etc. "He hath showed thee, O man, what is good; and what doth the Lord require of thee, but to do justly, and to love mercy, and to walk humbly with thy God?"

Dan. 9:24-27. "Locum insignem hunc de Messiah agere ipsimet Judæi, vel saltem plurima illorum pars confitentur." Stapfer, [*Theolog. Polem.*, Tom. III,] p. 49. [^002-note21]

92. DANIEL 12:1-3. The three first verses of the 12th chapter of Daniel, seem very plainly to be a prophecy of the appearing of the Messiah. "At that time shall Michael stand, the great prince which standeth for the children of thy people," etc. This seems to be the same great, anointed prince spoken of in the 9th chapter. Though the word in the original is not the same, yet both are well rendered "prince," as being much of the same signification. And the word is the same that is used for the Messiah, Is. 9:6-7, "to us a child is born, to us a son is given: and the PRINCIPALITY shall be upon his shoulders" (as it is in the Hebrew, the word being of the same root with "prince" here): "and his name shall be called Wonderful, Counselor, The mighty God, The everlasting Father, The PRINCE of Peace" (the same word as in this place).

The signification of the word "Michael" agrees well to the Messiah, viz. the LOWLINESS of God, or the poverty of God, or the smiting of God. This agrees with what is said of the humiliation, lowliness, affliction and sufferings of the Messiah in other prophecies.

The compellation here given him of the great prince well agrees to the Messiah, who is said to be one that is mighty, and to whom God says, "Gird thy sword on thy thigh, O most mighty" [Ps. 45:3], and is everywhere represented as so great a person. And 'tis not likely that such a compellation would be given to any mere man or created angel.

And his being called the great prince that "standeth for the children of thy people," is a further evidence that 'tis the Messiah that is meant. Who can this great prince be that stands for the children of God's people, but the person that is elsewhere spoken of[^002-note22] as he that shall rule over men [II Sam. 23:3]; that most mighty one that shall gird his sword on his thigh, and ride forth because of truth, meekness and righteousness [Ps. 45:3-4]; and that shall "judge the poor of the people, and save the children of the needy, and break in pieces the oppressor," and "deliver the needy when the[y] crieth, the poor also, and him that hath no helper," and "redeem their soul from deceit and violence" [Ps. 72:4, 12]; who is their Immanuel, and the Lord their righteousness, that shall "stand for an ensign of the people" [Is. 11:10]; and one that is lifted up as a standard against the enemy when he comes in like a flood [Is. 59:19]; and is the king of God's people that shall come to them, having salvation [Zech. 9:9]; is the Redeemer that shall come to Zion [Is. 59:20]; is the defense of God's people like an "hiding place from the wind, a covert from the tempest, as rivers of water in a dry place, and as the shadow of a great rock in a weary land [Is. 32:2]; that greatly loves God's people and unites himself to them, and interests himself much in their concerns, so he betroths them unto him and rejoices over them, as the bridegroom over the bride [Is. 62:5]; and is the angel of the covenant that God's people delight in [Mal. 3:1]; and the angel of his presence that in his love and in his pity redeems them [Is. 63:9]; who of old appeared to Joshua with a sword drawn in his hand, as the captain of the Lord's host [Josh. 5:13]; who stands as the shepherd of God's people, to feed them and to defend them, and stands as their intercessor, to make "intercession for the transgressors" [Is. 53:12]. 'Tis impossible that a compellation should agree better to the character of any person than this compellation, "*The great prince that standeth for the children of thy people*," does the character of the Messiah, as exhibited in the prophecies of the Old Testament. So ch. 10, at the last verse, he is called their prince. Now there is nothing in any part of the Scripture that looks as if any one particular angel of the created angels did, above all others, interest himself in the affairs of God's Israel, and was appointed over 'em, and had the care of them committed to him as their prince. And besides, this--viz. that Michael was their prince, that stood as it were alone for their defense against their enemies--is there mentioned as a thing much noted in the Scripture or in other writings of the prophets, as it can be understood of nothing else. "But I will show thee that which is noted in the scripture of truth: and there is none that holdeth with me in these things, but Michael your prince." There is nothing in the following words that this can be applied to, and writings of proceeding prophets do indeed speak abundantly of this; but they say nothing of any created angels that is their prince, that is set peculiarly over them and stands peculiarly for them.

And whereas Michael, ch. 10:13, is called the first of the chief princes, this is to represent him as the head of all principality, and is agreeable to Ps. 89:4, "I will make him my firstborn, higher than the kings of the earth." This name, according to the Hebrew idiom, signifies much the same thing as "prince of princes," but only expressed more strongly: for in that language, the prince of princes signifies as much as a Prince in the superlative degree, or the chief of all princes, as the Holy of Holies signifies the most holy of all. But here the expression is stronger; 'tis not only the chief of princes, but the chief of the chief of princes. Therefore, there is much reason to think that the person here spoken, is the same with that spoken of, ch. 8:25, "he shall also stand up against the Prince of princes"; by which undoubtedly a divine person, and not any mere creature, is intended.

Here Michael, this great prince that stands for the people of God, is represented as delivering them in that [that] is, in a peculiar and eminent manner, the time of the trouble of Israel above all others, which is agreeable to what is represented of the Messiah, Jer. 30:3, etc., "For, lo, the days come, saith the Lord, that I will bring again the captivity of my people Israel and Judah"; vv. 5-9, "For thus saith the Lord; We have heard a voice of trembling, of fear, and not of peace. Ask ye now, and see whether a man doth travail with child? wherefore do I see every man with his hands on his loins, as a woman in travail, and all faces are turned into paleness? Alas! for that day is great, so that none is like it; it is even the time of Jacob's trouble; but he shall be saved out of it. . . . in that day, saith the Lord of hosts, I will break his yoke from off thy neck, . . . But they shall serve the Lord their God, and David their king, whom I will raise up unto them."

Here the resurrection of the dead is spoken of as attending Michael's appearing, which has often been observed to be frequently spoken of as what will attend the coming of the Messiah. And particularly, as here [v. 1], Michael is said to stand up, and the dead to rise; so Job says [19:25], "I know that my Redeemer liveth, and that he shall stand at the latter day over the earth" (as the words would have been more properly rendered): "and though after my skin," etc.

V. 3, "they that be wise shall shine as the brightness of the firmament." This is agreeable to Is. 60, at the beginning, "Arise, shine; for thy light is come, . . . kings shall come to thy light, and princes to the brightness of thy rising";[^002-note23] and v. 15, "I will make thee an eternal excellency, a joy of many generations."
