Of this king it is said, in the preceding verse, that he should be to his people "a place of broad rivers and streams," and is undoubtedly the same king in the two first verses of the preceding chapter [Is. 32:1-2], "Behold, a king shall reign in righteousness, . . . And a man shall be a rivers of water in a dry place." See also Zeph. 3:14-15 to the end, and Dan. 2:44 and Dan. 7:14, 27.

Here in this 33rd of Isaiah, vv. 21-22,[^004-note33] the church in the times of the Messiah is introduced, saying, "the Lord shall be unto us a place of broad rivers and streams," and "the Lord is our judge, the Lord is our lawgiver, the Lord is our king; he will save us," as though Jehovah was the only judge, the only lawgiver and savior, and refreshing river of water that she gloried in. And yet, in the preceding part of this same discourse, the Messiah is spoken of as the great king that should reign over her, and judge that should execute judgment for [her], and the great Savior that should be her defense, and shadow, and great rock, and refreshing "rivers of water in a dry place," in the preceding chapter 32, vv. 1-2, 16-18; and often in the prophecy, and almost everywhere, is spoken of as the great king, lawgiver, judge and savior, light and defense and refreshment of his church. This strongly argues that the Messiah is God.[^004-note34]

Again, it is evident that the Messiah was to be a divine person, in that, in the 41st and 42nd and 43rd chapters of Isaiah, we have a great prophecy of the Messiah introduced by setting forth how that there was none found amongst men sufficient to counsel and help Zion, and to declare and accomplish salvation for her, ch. 41, latter end; and upon this it is declared, what glorious salvation God's chosen servant should accomplish; and in the process of this prophecy of God's chosen servant, ch. 43:10-12, God's people and this his chosen servant, this great Savior himself, are brought in as witnesses to confirm that there is no savior but God himself, none besides him that could declare and accomplish salvation. "Ye are my witnesses, saith the Lord, and my servant whom I have chosen: that ye may know and believe me, and understand that I am he: before me there was no God formed, neither shall there be after me. I, even I, am the Lord; and beside me there is no savior. I have declared, and saved, and have showed, when there was no strange god among you: therefore ye are my witnesses, saith the Lord, that I am God." Compare this last verse with the introduction of the prophecy, ch. 41:26 to the end. See also, 8th verse of the foregoing chapter: there God, speaking of his chosen servant, says, in the foregoing words [Is. 42:6-7], "I will give thee for a covenant of the people, for a light of the Gentiles; to open the blind eyes," and then adds, "I am Jehovah: that is my name: and my glory I will not give unto another."

See also what has been said in the preceding discourse on "The Prophecies of the Messiah," on Is. 62:11 to 63:6, §33; and Is. 40:9-11, §34; and Zeph. 3, §35; and Ps. 50, and 96, and 97, and 98, and 99, and 100, and 113, and Is. 2 at the beginning, and Mic. 4, §36.

Again, another thing that makes it evident that the Messiah is God, is what God says to the Messiah, Ps. 45:2-7. "Thou art fairer than the children of men: . . . Gird thy sword upon thy thigh, O most mighty, in thy glory and in thy majesty. . . . Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever: a scepter of righteousness is the scepter of thy kingdom. Thou lovest righteousness, and hatest wickedness: therefore God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows." See the evidence there is that the whole of this is spoken to and of the Messiah, in the preceding discourse of "The Prophecies of the Messiah," §46.

(See §106, *Corol*.) Again, it is an evidence that the Messiah is God, that we are directed to trust in him, and they are said to be blessed that trust in him. In Is. 28:15-17, the Messiah is represented as a sure foundation of trust, in opposition to all false refuges that shall be swept away with storms and floods. "We have made lies our refuge, and under falsehood have we hid ourselves: therefore thus saith the Lord God, Behold, I lay in Zion for a foundation a stone, a tried stone, a precious corner stone, a sure foundation: he that believeth shall not make haste. Judgment also will I lay to the line, and righteousness to the plummet: and the hail shall sweep away the refuge of lies, and the waters shall overflow the hiding place"; and Ps. 2:12, "Kiss the Son, lest he be angry, and ye perish from the way, when his wrath is kindled but a little. Blessed are they that put their trust in him." Whereas they that trust in man are pronounced cursed. Jer. 17:5-7, "Thus saith the Lord; Cursed be the man that trusteth in man, and maketh flesh his arm, and whose heart departeth from the Lord. For he shall be like the heath in the desert, and shall not see when good cometh; but shall inhabit the parched places of the wilderness, in a salt land and not inhabited. Blessed is the man that trusteth in the Lord, and whose hope the Lord is." Men, even the most potent of them, and though they be princes, are represented everywhere in the Old Testament as utterly unfit to be trusted in. Is. 2:22, "Cease ye from man, whose breath is in his nostrils: for wherein is he to be accounted of?" That which is nothing to be accounted of, is far from being a precious cornerstone. Ps. 118:8-9, "It is better to trust in the Lord than to put confidence in man. It is better to trust in the Lord than to put confidence in princes." In the 62nd Psalm, the Psalmist much insists on it, that God alone is his refuge and defense, and rock of his salvation, and the object of his trust, and all are much urged to trust in him alone; and then it follows, in the 9th verse, "Surely men of low degree are vanity, and men of high degree are a lie: to be laid in the balance, they are altogether lighter than vanity." If all men are nothing but vanity and [a] lie when depended upon as a refuge, as is here expressed, then surely such refuges are some of those refuges of lies and falsehood spoken of in the 29th of Isaiah, to which the Messiah is set in opposition as a tried stone, a sure foundation. Mere man in these and many other places, is represented unfit to be trusted in because he is weak, frail and unable to help or save. Ps. 146:3-4, "Put not your trust in princes, nor in the son of man, in whom there is no help." But the Messiah is represented as one mighty and able to help. Ps. 89:19, "Then thou spakest in vision to the holy one, and saidst, I have laid help on one that is mighty." So he is spoken of as "mighty to save." Is. 63:1, "Who is this that cometh from Edom, with dyed garments from Bozrah?this that is glorious in his apparel, travelling in the greatness of his strength? I that speak in righteousness, mighty to save."

Again, the Messiah is spoken of as God, and as the living God, in opposition to mortal men. Job 19:24-26, "For I know that my redeemer liveth, and that he shall stand at the latter day upon the earth: and though after my skin worms destroy this body, yet in my flesh shall I see God: whom I shall see for myself, and mine eyes shall behold, and not another; though my reins be consumed within me."

That the Messiah is a divine person appears by Mic. 5:2, "But thou, Bethlehem Ephratah, though thou be little among the thousands of Judah, yet out of thee shall he come forth unto me that is to be ruler in Israel; whose goings forth have been from of old, of everlasting." The words evidently an express a mystery or paradox: for first it is said of this ruler in Israel that he should "*come forth*" out of Bethlehem Ephratah; and then it is immediately added, "whose *comings forth* have been of old, from everlasting": for the words translated "*come forth*" and "*goings forth*" are in their root the same. So that what the Holy Ghost expresses here, is as much as this: "The Messiah hereafter shall proceed from me in Bethlehem Ephratah, who indeed proceeds from me from eternity." See note on the verse.[^004-note35]

That the Messiah is no mere man is evident, in that he is so often prophesied of as immortal or ever-living. 'Tis very often spoken in Scripture as the property of mere man, that he is mortal. Gen. 3:19, "dust thou art, and unto dust thou shalt return"; Job 4:18-19, 21, "Behold, he put no trust in his servants; and his angels he charged with folly: how much less in [them] that dwell in houses of clay, whose foundation is in the dust, which are crushed before the moth? . . . Doth not their excellency which is in them go away?"; Job 7:1-2, etc., "Is there not an appointed time to man on the earth? are not his days like the days of an hireling?," etc.; Job 8:9, "our days upon earth are a shadow"; Job 14[:1-2, 10, 20], "Man that is born of a woman is of few days, and full of trouble. He cometh forth like a flower, and is cut down: he fleeth also as a shadow, and continueth not," etc., "man dieth, and wasteth away: yea, man giveth up the ghost, and where is he? . . . Thou changest his countenance, and sendest him away"; Ps. 89:48, "What man is he that liveth, and shall not see death? shall he deliver his soul from the hand of the grave?"; Ps. 90, "Thou turnest man to destruction: . . . they are like grass which groweth up. In the morning it flourisheth, and groweth up; in the evening it is cut down, and withered. . . . we spend our years as a tale that is told. The days of our years are threescore years and ten," etc.; Ps. 103:14, etc., "he remembreth that we are as dust. As for man, his days are as grass: as the flower of the field, so he flourisheth. For the wind passeth over it, and it is gone"; Ps. 144:4, "Man is like to vanity: his days are as a shadow that passeth away"; Ps. 146:3-4, "Put not your trust in princes, nor in the son of man, in whom there is no help. His breath goeth forth, he returneth to his earth; in that very day his thoughts perish"; Eccles. 3:20, "all are of the dust, and all turn to dust again"; Eccles. 12:7, "Then shall the dust return to the earth as it was"; Is. 40:6-8, "All flesh is grass, and all the goodliness thereof as the flower of the field: the grass withereth, the flower fadeth"; Is. 51:12, "that thou shouldst be afraid of a man that shall die, and of the son of man which shall be made as grass"; and innumerable other places. Yea, mortality is spoken of as a great note of distinction between God and the Son of God, and mere man. Ps. 82:6-7, "I have said, Ye are gods; and all of you children of the most High. But ye shall die like men." But yet the Messiah is often spoken of as living forever. Job 19:24-26, "my redeemer liveth, . . . though worms destroy this body," etc.; Ps. 72:5, "They shall fear thee as long as the sun and moon endure, throughout all generations"; v. 7, "In his days shall the righteous flourish; and abundance of peace so long as the moon endureth"; v. 15, "he shall live"; v. 17, "His name shall endure for ever"; Is. 9:6-7, "his name shall be called . . . The everlasting Father, . . . Of the increase of his government and peace there shall be no end, upon the throne of David, and upon his kingdom, to order it, and to establish it with judgment and with justice, from henceforth even for ever"; Ezek. 37:25, "my servant David shall be their prince for ever"; Ps. 110:4, "The Lord hath sworn, and will not repent, Thou art a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek."

Again, that the Messiah is God may be argued from Dan. 12:1, "And at that time shall Michael stand up, the great prince which standeth for the children of thy people." That this Michael is the Messiah has been already shown, "Prophecies of Messiah," §92. There is great reason to think this great prince and archangel, or first of the chief princes, ch. 10:13, or the head of the angels, must be the same with him that is elsewhere called the angel of the covenant and the angel of God's presence, that in his love and pity redeems Israel; and that captain of the Lord's host that appeared to Joshua with a sword drawn in his hand; and the angel to whom God committed the care of the congregation in the wilderness, that we know was Jehovah. Again, this appears from Dan. 7:13, "I saw in the night visions, and, behold, one like the Son of man came with the clouds of heaven." The clouds of heaven are a note of his divinity, and therefore 'tis mentioned as wonderful that one like the Son of man should come with clouds of heaven.

Again, it may be argued that the Messiah is Jehovah from Mic. 5:4 compared with Ex. 23:20-21, "Behold, I send an Angel before thee, to keep thee in the way, and to bring thee into the place which I have prepared. Beware of him, and obey his voice, provoke him not; . . . for my name is in him." In Mic. 5:4, the Messiah is spoken of in a remarkable agreement with this in Exodus: "And he shall stand and feed in the strength of Jehovah, and in the majesty of the name of the Lord Jehovah his God; and they shall abide: for now shall he be great unto the ends of the earth." So that the Messiah, or the Angel, spoken of, Ex. 23, has the name Jehovah, and his strength and majesty. This also confirms the foregoing argument from Dan 12:1, for there is all reason to suppose that the great prince, that there is said to stand for the children of the people of the Prophet, is the same ruler in Israel of whom it is said, Mic. 5, that "he [be][^004-note36] great unto the ends of the earth," that "shall stand and feed Israel in the strength of the Lord, and in the majesty of the name of the Lord his God."

I have already shown that the prince spoken of, Ezekiel, ch. 44, and in the following chapters, is the Messiah; and have shown that this prince must be a divine person, §74.[^004-note37]

Again, the deity of the Messiah may be further argued from the Messiah's being said, in Is. 28:16, to be "a stone, a tried stone, a precious corner stone, a sure foundation," when compared with Deut. 32:4, "He is the Rock"; II Sam. 22:32, "For who is God, save the Lord? and who is a rock, save our God?"; Ps. 62:2, "He only is my rock and my salvation; he is my defense; I shall not be greatly moved"; and v. 6, "He only is my rock and my salvation."

'Tis an evidence that the Messiah is true God, in that he is set in opposition to molten images in the manner that he is. Is. 41:23-24, 26-29, and 42:1, etc.,

Show the things that are to come hereafter, that we may know that ye are gods: . . . Behold, ye are of nothing, . . . Who hath declared from the beginning, that we may know? and beforetime, that we may say, He is righteous? yea, there is none that showeth, yea, there is none that declareth, yea, there is none that heareth your words. The first shall say to Zion, Behold them, behold them: and I will give to Jerusalem one that bringeth good tidings. For I beheld, and there was no man; even amongst them, and there was no counselor, that, when I asked of them, could answer a word. Behold, they are all vanity; their works are nothing: their molten images are wind and confusion. Behold my servant, whom I uphold; mine elect, in whom my soul delighteth; I have put my spirit upon him: he shall bring forth judgment to the Gentiles;

and v. 8, "I am the Lord: that is my name: and my glory will I not give to another, and my praise to graven images."

Here the Messiah is represented as being sent of God, to do that which he had challenged the false gods of the heathen to do, and which, it is implied, if they could do it, would [be] a good evidence that they are God, that their cause is good and they righteous in their pretenses: and that is foretelling things to come as of their own knowledge. "Produce your cause, bring forth your strong reasons, . . . Show the things that are to come hereafter, that we may know that ye are gods. . . . Who hath declared from the beginning, that we may know? and beforetime, that we may say, He is righteous? . . . I beheld, and there was none; even amongst them, and there was no counselor, that, when I asked of them, could answer a word." But in the 27th verse, and 42nd chapter, at the beginning, 'tis[^004-note38] represented that the Messiah should be such a counselor, and do those things that those false gods could not do.

Again, the Messiah is represented as the object of prayer and praise. Ps. 20:9, "Save, Lord: let the king hear us when we call"; Ps. 72:15, "prayer also shall be made for him" (or "through him"; see "Prophecies," §40)[^004-note39] "continually; and daily shall he be praised," or "blessed," as it is in the original, the word in the original being the same that is commonly used to signify an act of worship to a deity.

Again, that the Messiah is God is evident from Zech. 3:[8-]9, "for, behold, I will bring forth my servant the Branch. For behold the stone that I have laid before Joshua; upon one stone shall be seven eyes" (this stone I have shown is the Messiah): [^004-note40] "I will engrave the graving thereof, saith the Lord of hosts, and I will remove the iniquity of that land in one day," compared with ch. 4:10, "For who hath despised the day of small things? for they shall rejoice, and shall see the plummet in the hand of Zerubbabel with those seven; they are the eyes of the Lord, which run to and fro through the whole world." Here the seven eyes that are represented as being graved by God on the headstone of the temple, are[^004-note41] so united to it by God as to be in the substance of the stone, or to be the eyes of the stone: but those same eyes are said to be the eyes of the Lord.

Again, that the Messiah is God is manifest from that, that he is called the man that is God's fellow, Zech. 13:7. Thus it is, and thus only, that that can be verified, Zech. 12:8, "and the house of David shall be as God, even as the angel of the Lord before them." God will not thus give his glory to worms of the dust, when his design is to show that all flesh is grass, or to bring it to pass that the Lord alone should be trusted in that day.

That the Messiah is God may be strongly argued from what God's people are represented saying in the times of the Messiah. Is. 26:13-14, "O Lord, other lords besides thee have had dominion over us: but by thee only will we make mention of thy name. They are dead, they shall not live; they are deceased, they shall not rise: therefore hast thou visited and destroyed them, and made all their memory to perish." Here, all other lords besides Jehovah are most expressly and fully excluded, and yet one of the names given the Messiah in the prophecies of the Old Testament is *Lord*, the word in the original being the same as in this place. Ps. 110:1, "The Lord said to my *Lord*"; and Mal. 3:1, "the *Lord*, whom ye seek, shall suddenly come into his temple." And the church is expressly commanded to worship him as her Lord, Ps. 45:11, "for he is thy *Lord*; and worship thou him." Again, that which is here particularly mentioned as an evidence that those other lords they had acknowledged and submitted to were vain, and not to be worshipped, is that they died like men, and remain under the power of death, and are not risen again; which is a thing wherein the Messiah is particularly distinguished from them, as I shall show afterwards out of the prophecies of the Old Testament.[^004-note42] Now, therefore, can it be supposed that the church in the times of the Messiah, who is expressly commanded to worship the Messiah as her Lord, and does do so, would make this profession to God, that she had worshipped other lords heretofore but for the future would own no other Lord but him, unless the Messiah, whom she owned and worshipped as her Lord, was the same with God? and that she would give this as a reason, why all other persons on earth that were not God, were not to be acknowledged and served as Lord, that they die and rise not again: when this would not be true of one merely human lord, and that very Lord that she worshipped, even the Messiah, on the supposition that he is a mere human lord, and not God himself.

To these things agrees the wish of the church for the days of the Messiah, Is. 64:1, "Oh that thou wouldst rent the heavens, that thou wouldst come down." In this wish, the church has respect to God's coming down and being present on earth in a manner that he never has been before: for it is supposed that God's immediate presence had as it were been confined to the heavens, as though the heavens, by impervious pavement, had shut God in and confined him to the region above. And therefore, if God should come to have his tabernacle with men, and to dwell with them, as he would do in the days of the Messiah, a new and extraordinary thing would come to pass, as much as if the strong pavement of [the] heavens should be rent; and he that had been from the beginning the glorious inhabitant of heaven, should come to dwell immediately and properly with men and among men, not by extraordinary symbols and manifestations of his presence and wonderous works, as had been from time to time, but in a more immediate manner, and on the supposition that God becomes man, and the Messiah is God. This well explains the matters, though there probably are other ways also wherein God was to be extraordinarily present on earth in the days of the kingdom of the Messiah, that the Holy Spirit may also have respect to in this place.

See §16, *Corol*.

15.[^004-note43] It was foretold that the Messiah should be a person that was in a peculiar manner beloved of God. This is implied in, or may fairly be inferred from, almost all the prophecies, but is more especially and directly expressed in Is. 42:1, where the Messiah is called "mine elect, in whom my soul delighteth." Is. 49:6-7, "It is a light thing that thou shouldst be my servant to raise up the tribes of Jacob, . . . Thus saith the Lord, the Redeemer of Israel, and his Holy One, to him whom man depiseth, . . . Kings shall see and arise, princes also shall worship, because of the Lord that is faithful, and the Holy One of Israel, and he shall choose thee." See also Ps. 2, and 45, and 110 and 89.

16.[^004-note44] It was the doctrine of the ancient prophets, that the Messiah was the Son of God. There was some divine person that was known in the church of Israel by the name of the Son of God, as appears by Prov. 30:4, "what is his name, and what is his son's name, if thou canst tell?" This divine person that they called the Son of God, seems to have been the same divine person that had been wont from time to time to appear in a glorious visible form, in the form of a man or in a visible shape, beautiful and illustrious, as he did to Jacob when he wrestled with him, and as he did to Moses and the seventy elders, to Joshua, and to Manoah, Isaiah, Ezekiel and others. The angels that appeared in a glorious form, sometimes are called "sons of God," Job 38:7. But this divine person that is called *the Angel* of the Lord by way of eminency, is also in like manner by way of eminency called the Son of God. The same was called the angel of God's presence, and the angel of the covenant. This person that thus often appeared in a glorious visible form of a man, was so known in the church of Israel by the name of the son of God, that it was famed among the heathen round about; so that they had a tradition of the Son of God appearing in a glorious form of a man, as they had of many other things about God from the Israelites. And therefore, when Nebuchadnezzar saw a person with Shadrach, Meshach and Abednego in the burning fiery furnace, in such a visible form, he at once supposed it to be this person that he had heard of, that used thus to appear in favor of his people on some extraordinary occasions; and therefore he says, "the form of the fourth is like the son of God" [Dan. 3:25].

I have already showed that the Messiah is the same person with him that is called the angel of the Lord, and the angel of God's presence, and the angel of the covenant; and therefore, by what is said above, is the Son of God. And thus God is represented calling the Messiah his Son, in a very eminent and peculiar manner, Ps. 2:7, "thou art my Son; this day have I begotten thee"; v. 12, "Kiss the Son, lest he be angry." The princes of Israel of the house of David, God's chosen, beloved and anointed, have this compellation given them, Ezek. 21:10, "it contemneth the rod of my son, as every tree." But in a more eminent mannner it is given to him who is the great promised Branch of the house of David, the great *David* or beloved, and elect and anointed, so often spoken of by the prophets. Ps. 89:26-27, "He shall cry unto me, Thou art my father, my God, and the rock of my salvation. Also I will make him my firstborn, higher than the kings of the earth." And the Jews all along, even in Christ's time, understood these things thus. They understood that the Messiah was to be, in an eminent and peculiar manner, the Son of God, as appears plainly by the history of the evangelists in many passages, as particularly Matt. 26:63, "And the high priest answered and said unto him, I adjure thee by the living God, that thou tell us whether thou be the Christ" (or the Messiah), "the Son of God"; Matt. 14:33, "Of a truth thou art the Son of God"; John 6:69, "we believe and are sure that thou art that Christ," (or Messiah), "the Son of the living God"; John 11:27, "Yea, Lord: I believe that thou art the Christ, the Son of God, which should come into the world"; Matt. 16:16, "Simon Peter answered and said, Thou art Christ, the Son of the living God"; John 1:34, "I saw, and bare record that this is the Son of God"; see v. 49; John 20:31, "But these things are written that ye may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God." See also Matt. 14:33 and Acts 8:37. By all which it appears that "the Messiah" and "the Son of God," were commonly used among the Jews as expressions equipollent, or amounting to the same thing. (See the next section, *Corol*.; see "Scripture," no. 501a.) [^004-note45]

*Corol*. This is a confirmation of what was before observed, §14, that it indeed was the doctrine of the prophets, that the Messiah was a divine person: for 'tis evident that he that was in the Old Testament called the Son of God, was a divine person, or one that had a divine nature; as man begets a son in his own image or nature, so the Son of God is in His image and nature. The princes of Israel of old, because they were types of the Messiah, the proper kings of Israel were called sons of God figuratively; and just so far, they are figuratively called gods. Ps. 82:6, "I said, Ye are gods; and all of you children of the most High." So the Messiah, the true king of Israel, the proper Son of God, is properly God. And I have before shown,[^004-note46] the person that is called the angel of the Lord, the angel of God's presence and the angel of the covenant, who is the same with him, that was called the Son of God, was truly God, or a person that had the divine nature. And thus the Jews in Christ's time understood: they understood the compellation, the *Son of God*, to carry in it something divine, and to signify a person that had the divine nature; and therefore accused Christ with blasphemy in calling himself the Son of God, as thereby making himself God, and equal with God. John 5:17-18, "But Jesus answered them, My Father worketh hitherto, and I work. Therefore the Jews sought the more to kill him, because he not only had broken the sabbath, but said also that God was his Father, making himself equal with God"; and John 10:33, etc., "For a good work we stone thee not; but for blasphemy; and because thou, being a man, makest thyself God. Jesus answered them, . . . Say ye of him, whom the Father hath sanctified, and sent into the world, Thou blasphemest; because I said, I am the Son of God?" In the same account, the high priest accused him with blasphemy. Matt. 26:63-65, "I adjure thee by the living God, that thou tell us whether thou be the Christ, the Son of God. Jesus saith unto him, Thou hast said: . . . Then the high priest rent his clothes, saying, He hath spoken blasphemy; what further need have we of witnesses? behold, now ye have heard his blasphemy." See §20,[^004-note47] *Corol*.

17.[^004-note48] It was foretold that in the days of the Messiah there should be a very extraordinary exercise and display of divine mercy and grace. Ps. 108:4, "For thy mercy is great above the heavens: and thy truth reacheth unto the clouds"; Ps. 48:9, "We have thought of thy lovingkindness, O God, in the midst of thy temple"; Ps. 145:7, "They shall abundantly utter the memory of thy great goodness, and shall sing of thy righteousness"; Is. 63:7, "I will make mention of the lovingkindness of the Lord, [. . .] according to all that the Lord hath bestowed on us, and the great goodness towards the house of Israel, which he hath bestowed on them according to his mercies, and according to the multitude of his lovingkindnesses"; Jer. 33:6, "Behold, I will bring it health and cure, and I will cure them, and will reveal unto them the abundance of truth and peace"; and v. 9, "And it shall be to me a name of joy, a praise and an honor before all the nations of the earth, which shall hear of all the good that I do unto them: and they shall fear and tremble for all the goodness and for all the prosperity that I procure unto it"; Hos. 3:5, "Afterward shall the children of Israel return, and seek the Lord their God, and David their king; and shall fear the Lord and his goodness in the latter days"; Zech. 4:7, "and he shall bring forth the headstone thereof with shoutings, crying, Grace, grace unto it"; and ch. 9:17, "For how great is his goodness, and how great is his beauty!"

18.[^004-note49] It was foretold that in the days of the Messiah, that God would accomplish some very eminently great work of goodness for his people, that was called *that good thing that God had promised them*. Jer. 33:14, "Behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that I will perform that good thing which I have promised unto the house of Israel and to the house of Judah"; Ps. 85:10-12, "Mercy and truth are met together; . . . Truth shall spring out of the earth; and righteousness shall look down from heaven. Yea, the Lord shall give that that is good."

And this great work of divine goodness thus foretold, was some great salvation that God should accomplish that, by way of eminency, was called THE SALVATION OF GOD, as being eminently so, and so much beyond all other salvations that they were not worthy to be compared with it. Gen. 49:18, "I have waited for thy salvation, O Lord"; Ps. 14:7, "O that the salvation of Israel were come out of Zion! when the Lord bringeth back the captivity of his people, Jacob shall rejoice, and Israel shall be glad." We have the same words again in Ps. 53:6; II Sam. 23:5, "this is all my salvation, and all my desire"; Ps. 85:9-11, "Surely his salvation is nigh them that fear him; that glory may dwell in our land. Mercy and truth are met together; righteousness and peace have kissed each other. Truth shall spring out of the earth; and righteousness shall look down from heaven. Yea, the Lord shall give that which is good"; Ps. 98:2-3, "The Lord hath made known his salvation: and his righteousness hath he openly showed in the sight of the heathen. He hath remembered his mercy and his truth toward the house of Israel: all the ends of the earth have seen the salvation of our God"; Is. 25:8-9, "He will swallow up death in victory; . . . And it shall be said in that day, Lo, this is our God; we have waited for him, and he will save us: this is the Lord; we have waited for him, we will be glad and rejoice in his salvation"; Is. 46:13, "I bring near my righteousness; it shall not be far off, and my salvation shall not tarry: I will place salvation in Zion for Israel my glory"; Is. 51:5-6, "My righteousness is near; my salvation is gone forth, and mine arms shall judge the people; the isles shall wait upon me, and on mine arm shall they trust. . . . the earth shall wax old as a garment, . . . but my salvation shall be for ever, and my righteousness shall not be abolished"; v. 8, "For the moth shall eat them like a garment, and the worm[^004-note50] shall eat them like wool: but my righteousness shall be for ever, and my salvation from generation to generation"; Is. 56:1, "Thus saith the Lord, Keep ye judgment, and do justice: for my salvation is near to come, and my righteousness to be revealed"; Is. 45:17, "Israel shall be saved of the Lord with an everlasting salvation"; Ps. 50:23, "to him that ordereth his conversation aright will I show the salvation of God."

God's working this salvation for his people, is expressed by his giving them peace, in Hag. 2:9, "in this place will I give peace"; and revealing to them the abundance of truth and peace, Jer.[^004-note51] 33:6, "I will bring it health and cure, and I will heal them, and reveal to them the abundance of truth and peace."

It was foretold that this SALVATION should be so great, that the redemption out of Egypt should not be worthy [to] be compared with it. Is. 43:16-19, "Thus saith the Lord, which maketh a way in the sea, and a path in the mighty waters; which bringeth forth the chariot and horse, and the army and the power; they shall lie down together, they shall not rise: they are extinct, they are quenched as tow. Remember ye not the former things, neither consider the things of old. Behold, I will do a new thing: now it shall spring forth; shall ye not know it? I will even make a way in the wilderness, and rivers in the desert." Is. 64:1-4, "Oh that thou would rent the heavens, that thou wouldst come down," etc.; v. 3, "When thou didst terrible things which we looked not for, thou comest down, the mountains flowed down at thy presence. For since the beginning of the world men have not heard, nor perceived by the ear, neither hath the eye seen, O God, besides thee, what he hath prepared for him that worketh for him"; Jer. 16:14-15, "Therefore, behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that it shall be no more said, The Lord liveth, which brought up the children of Israel out of the land of Egypt; but, The Lord liveth, which brought up the children of Israel out of the land of the north." Again we have the same words, Jer 23:7-8. Agreeable to this, is that in Is. 11:15-16, "And the Lord shall utterly destroy the tongue of the Egyptian sea" (by a much greater work than only once occasionally dividing it); "and with his mighty wind shall he shake his hand over the river, and shall smite it in the seven streams, and make men go over dryshod. And there shall be an highway for the remnant of his people, which shall be left from Assyria; like as it was to Israel in the day that he came up out of the land of Egypt." See note on Jer. 31:32.[^004-note52]

It was foretold that God should bring to pass this great SALVATION and peace for his people by the Messiah. Zech. 9:9, "Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion; shout, O daughter of Jerusalem: behold, thy King cometh unto thee: he is just, and having salvation"; Is. 52:7, "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that bringeth good tidings, that publisheth *peace*; that bringeth good tidings of *good*, that publisheth *salvation*; that saith unto Zion, Thy God reigneth!"; vv. 9-13, etc., "Break forth into joy, sing together, ye waste places of Jerusalem: for the Lord hath comforted his people, he hath *redeemed* Jerusalem. The Lord hath made bare his holy arm in the eyes of all the nations; all the ends of the earth shall see the *salvation of our God*. Depart, depart ye, . . . For ye shall not go out with haste, nor go by flight: for the Lord will go before you; and the God of Israel will be your rereward. Behold, my servant shall deal prudently," etc.; Jer. 23:5-8, "Behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that I will raise up unto David a righteous Branch, and a King shall reign and prosper, . . . In his days shall Judah be saved, . . . and this is the name whereby he shall be called, The Lord Our Righteousness. Therefore, behold, the days come, saith the Lord, that they shall no more say, The Lord liveth, which brought up out of the land of Egypt," etc.; Is. 61, at the beginning, "the Lord hath anointed me to [. . .] proclaim liberty to captives, and the opening of the prison to them that are bound"; v. 10, "he hath clothed me with the garments of salvation, he hath covered me with the robe of righteousness"; ch. 62:1, "For Zion's sake I will not hold my peace, and for Jerusalem's sake I will not rest, till the righteousness thereof go forth as brightness, and the salvation thereof as a lamp that burneth"; v. 11, "Say ye to the daughter of Zion, Behold, thy salvation cometh; behold, his reward is with him, and his work before him," with the beginning of the next chapter, in the next verse but one, "Who is this that cometh from Edom," etc., "I that speak in righteousness, mighty to save"; v. 4, "the year of my redeemed is come"; Hag. 2:7, [9,] "the desire of all nations shall come: and I will fill this house with glory, . . . and in this place will I give peace, saith the Lord"; Is. 59:20, "And the Redeemer shall come to Zion, and unto them that turn from transgression in Jacob, saith the Lord." The same is plainly implied in Isaiah, ch. 42 and ch. 49, and innumerable other places.

And thus the Jews about the time that Jesus appeared, understood the prophecies of the Old Testament, that in the days of the Messiah there was to be that great salvation that was called, by way of eminency, God's salvation, and that this salvation was to be by the Messiah, as appears by what Zacharias says, Luke 1:68, etc. "Blessed be the Lord God of Israel; who hath visited and *redeemed*his people, and hath raised up an horn of *salvation* for us in the house of his servant David; as he spake by the mouth of his holy prophets, which have been since the world began; that we should be saved from our enemies, and from the hand of them that hate us; to perform the mercy promised to our fathers, and to remember his holy covenant; the oath which he sware to our father Abraham"; vv. 77-78,[^004-note53] "to give knowledge of salvation to his people by the remission of their sins, through the tender mercy of our God." And also by what Simeon says; see Luke 2:26, "it was revealed to him, that he should not see death[^004-note54] till he had seen the Lord's Christ." Vv. 29-30, "Now lettest thou thy servant depart in peace for mine eyes have seen thy salvation." And also what is said of Anna the prophetess: "And she coming in that instant gave thanks likewise unto the Lord, and spake of him to all them that looked for redemption in Jerusalem" [^004-note55]

19.[^004-note56] It was foretold by the ancient prophets that God, in the Messiah's days, should bring to pass some very strange, mysterious and wonderful thing, an unparallelled work of wisdom, power and mercy that was altogether new, before unseen and unheard of, and that none could ever have conceived of; and therefore should greatly surprise and fill with admiration and astonishment those that should be informed of it, and come to the understanding of it. Num. 23:23, "According to this time it shall be said of Jacob and of Israel, What hath God wrought!"; Mic. 7:15, "According to the days of thy coming out of the land of Egypt will I show unto him marvelous things." That work of God toward his people, when he delivered them out of Egypt, is spoken of as[^004-note57] exceeding and astonishingly wonderful, without parallel. Ex. 34:10, "before all thy people I will do marvels, such as have not been done in all the earth, nor in any nation"; and Deut. 4:32, "For ask now of the days that are past, which were before thee, since the day that God created man upon the earth, and ask from one side of heaven unto the other, whether there hath been any such thing as this great thing is, or hath been heard like it?" But that is spoken as but a first resemblance of what God would do for his people in the days of the Messiah, and as nothing to it, in no measure so wonderful. Is. 64, at the beginning, "Oh that thou would rent the heavens, that thou wouldst come down, that the mountains might flow down at thy presence, . . . When thou didst terrible things which we looked not for, thou camest down, the mountains flowed down at thy presence. For since the beginning of the world men have not heard, nor perceived by the ear, neither hath the eye seen, O God, besides thee, what he hath prepared for him that waiteth for him"; Is. 43:16-19, "Thus saith the Lord, which maketh a way in the sea, and a path in the mighty waters; which bringeth forth the chariot and horse," etc., "Remember ye not the former things, neither consider things of old. Behold, I will do a new thing," etc.

The unparalleled wonderfulness of this great thing that God would do, is also expressed in II Sam. 7:19, "And this was yet a small thing in thy sight, O Lord; but thou hast spoken also of thy servant's house a great while to come. And is this the manner of man, O Lord God?"; Is. 52:15 with 53:1, "So shall he sprinkle many nations; the kings shall shut their mouths at him: for that which had not been told them shall they see; and that which they had not heard shall they consider"; Jer. 33:3, "Call to me, and I will answer, and show thee great and mighty things, which thou knowest not," taken with v. 6, "I [. . .] will reveal unto them the abundance of truth and peace," and vv. 14-15, "I will perform that good thing which I have promised . . . I will cause the Branch of righteousness to grow up unto David," etc.; Ps. 118:23, "This is the Lord's doing; it is marvelous in our eyes"; Is. 29:14, "Therefore, behold, I will proceed to do a marvelous work amongst this people, even a marvelous work and a wonder: for the wisdom of their wise men shall perish, the understanding of their prudent men shall be hid" (compare this with Is. 52:15 and with 53:1); Ps. 22:31, "They shall come, and declare his righteousness unto a people that shall be born, that he hath *done this*"; Ps. 72:18, "Blessed be the Lord, the God of Israel, who only doth wondrous things," and 86:10, "For thou art great, and dost wondrous things," and 89:5, "And the heavens shall declare thy wonders"; Is. 25:1, "O Lord, thou art my God; I will exalt thee, I will praise thy name; for thou hast done wonderful things"; Joel 2:26, "And ye shall [. . .] praise the name of the Lord your God, that hath dealt wondrously with you"; Ps. 145:5, "I will speak of the glorious honors of thy majesty, and of thy wondrous works."

And because God would work a new kind of work for his people, with new and before unseen and unheard of displays of God's glory and grace, therefore the church is called upon in the days of the Messiah to sing a new song of praise to God. Is. 42:9-10, "*new things* do I declare: before they spring forth I tell you of them. Sing unto the Lord a *new song*, and his praise from the end of the earth"; Ps. 98:1-2, "O sing unto the Lord a *new song*; for he hath done *marvelous things*: his right hand, and his holy arm, hath gotten him the victory. The Lord hath made known his salvation: his righteousness hath he openly showed in the sight of the heathen"; Ps. 96:1, etc., "O sing unto the Lord a new song: sing unto the Lord, all the earth"; Ps. 149:1, "Praise ye the Lord. Sing unto the Lord a new song."[^004-note58]

20.[^004-note59] It was foretold that the Messiah should be a very wonderful, mysterious person. Is. 9:6, "his name shall be called Wonderful," or "Secret."

*Corol*. This confirms what was observed in §16,[^004-note60] viz. that the Messiah is the Son of God, of whom it is said, Prov. 30:4, "what is his name, and what is his son's name, if thou canst tell," intimating that his Son's name is secret, or wonderful, as is said of the Messiah, Is. 9:6. This also confirms that the Son of God and the Messiah are the same with the angel of the Lord that appeared to Manoah, who told him that his name was Secret, or Wonderful: for the word in the original is the very same as in Is. 9:6. And this confirms that the Messiah is a person that is God, because 'tis very plain that he that was of old, by way of eminency, called the angel of the Lord, that appeared to Manoah, etc., was Jehovah.

See the next.

21.[^004-note61] See the last. Particularly it was signified by the ancient prophecies that the Messiah, this Son of God, should be a very wonderful person with regard to his generation, or the manner of his being begotten; and particularly, that there should be a great mystery in his generation in this respect, viz. that he should be born to God in time in Bethlehem Ephratah, and yet that he proceeds from God from all eternity, and that he has no beginning and is the parent that gives beginning to all things, and is yet born and becomes a child himself. And 'tis also intimated in ancient prophecies that what should in a peculiar manner render his generation [wonderful], should be his humiliation, or that one of such generation or descent as he should suffer such things. In Is. 53:8, it is signified that he should be a person of a most unsearchably wonderful and mysterious generation—"*and who shall declare his generation?*"—and in the words immediately following, 'tis intimated that his humiliation is what peculiarly renders his generation wonderful: "for he was cut off out of the land of the living: for the transgression of my people was he smitten." And in Mic. 5:2, 'tis signified that his generation was most wonderful on that account, that he was to be generated and born to God in Bethlehem Ephratah, and yet proceeds from God from of old, even from everlasting. "But thou, Bethlehem Ephratah, though thou be little among the thousands of Judah, yet out of thee shall he come forth unto me that is to be ruler in Israel; whose goings forth" (or "whose comings forth," for the word is the same as before) "have been from of old, from everlasting." This is evidently spoken as a great paradox. So also 'tis spoken of as a thing very wonderful, Is. 9[:6], that the Messiah should be born, and so have a beginning and be a young child, and yet that he has no beginning, and is the parent that gives beginning to all things: "For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given: . . . and his name shall be called Wonderfull, . . . The everlasting Father."

22.[^004-note62] It was signified in the ancient prophecies that the Messiah should be a very wonderful person on that account, that he should be both God and man. So 'tis spoken of, Is. 9, as a great wonder that God should accomplish for his people, and so a fit sign of an unexpected and otherwise incredible deliverance God should accomplish for his people, that not only that a virgin should concieve and bear a son, but that she should bear such a son, viz. one whose name should be called *Immanuel*, i. e. God with us; which is accomplished in God's becoming one of us, God dwelling among mankind as one of mankind, and an inhabitant of the earth. The same thing is spoken of as a great wonder, Is. 9:6, where it is prophesied that the Messiah should be a child born and a son given, and so little and feeble and of late beginning; and yet the MIGHTY GOD, not little and feeble; and the EVERLASTING FATHER, being the parent of all things, instead of [a] child born, and one that had been from everlasting, instead of being lately born: and therefore in the same [verse] is said to be Wonderful. So in Is. 4:2, the Messiah is called both "the branch of the Lord" and "fruit of the earth." So the same paradox is couched in [the] compellation given to the Messiah in Zech. 13:7, "the man that is my fellow." The same wonder is represented in vision to Daniel [7:13], in which he saw "*one like the Son of man come with clouds of heaven*," which were of old the established symbols of the divine nature, majesty and glory. So it is spoken of as a wonderful thing that Job was assured of, though so mysterious (Job 19:25-27), that God should at the latter day become visible to bodily eyes—"*in my flesh shall I see God:*[. . .]*and these eyes shall behold him*": putting it out of all dispute, by this double expression of his meaning, that he means he shall see him with his bodily eyes. And therefore, agreeable to this, he in this place calls him his near kinsman: "I know," says he, "that my *redeemer* liveth"; in the original, *GOÆ;`LI*, "my near kinsman liveth." The word is the same as in Ruth 3:9, "Thou art a near kinsman." Agreeable to this that Job says concerning God's becoming visible to the eye, is that in Is. 52:7-8, "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that bringeth good tidings, that publisheth peace; that bringeth good tidings of good things, [. . .] that saith unto Zion, Thy God reigneth! Thy watchmen shall lift up the voice; with the voice together shall they sing: for they shall *see eye to eye*, when the Lord shall bring again Zion"; i. e. "Thou shalt see that King that reigns, who is Zion's God, eye to eye." Which may serve to show us the full force of such expressions as those, Is. 40:9, "O Zion, that bringest good tidings, get thee into the high mountain; O Jerusalem, that bringest good tidings, lift up thy voice with strength; lift up, be not afraid; say unto the cities of Judah, *Behold your God*." Those passages that are so much like one another, may serve to explain one the other. And the last mentioned place confirms that by the watchmen's seeing eye to eye, is meant their seeing or beholding Zion's God, spoken of in the preceding verse. Agreeable to this is that in Is. 25:9, "And it shall be said in that day, Lo, this is our God; we have waited for him, and he will save us: this is the Lord; we have waited for him, we will be glad and rejoice in his salvation." Indeed, it can't be supposed that the incarnation of the Messiah, is the only thing that there is here respect to. But if we put all those places that have been mentioned together, it seems reasonable to suppose that there is some respect to that.

Dan. 7:13, "I saw in the night visions, and behold one like the Son of man came with clouds of heaven." His coming with clouds of heaven was a note of his divinity; it is therefore mentioned as a wonderful sight which Daniel saw in his vision, that one like the Son of man should appear coming with clouds of heaven.[^004-note63]

23.[^004-note64] There are a great many representations in the Prophets of wonderful things of an outward and temporal nature to be brought to pass in the Messiah's days, and of many externally glorious circumstances of the people of God in the time of his kingdom that, it is evident, either from the nature of the representations them[selves], or [as] may be made plain by the Old Testament, are not literally but figuratively and mystically to be understood.

To put this matter beyond doubt, I would take notice of the following things:

1. Nothing is more plain, than that it was the manner of old to utter prophecies in very enigmatical and dark speeches, making use of bold figures and allegories; and therefore, predictions uttered by inspiration from God were called parables. So Balaam's prophesies were called parables [Num. 22]. And a view of the prophecies themselves, that were uttered from time to time from the beginning of the world, do plainly show it. Agreeable hereto, the prophets used typical representations of future events in actions, as well as words.

2. There are many things foretold by the prophets, concerning the times of the Messiah's kingdom, that in their literal sense are impossible, and others that, if not simply impossible, yet it would be in itself very absurd to understand them literally. So it is utterly impossible that all nations should be gathered together to the mountain of the house of the [^004-note65]

It would be absurd to take that text literally, [Is. 55:]12, "the mountains and hills shall break forth before you into singing, and all the trees of the field shall clap their hand." And we have no more reason literally to understand the words that follow in the next verse, "Instead of the thorn shall come up the fir tree, and instead of the briar shall some up the myrtle tree"; and those many other parallel texts of God's planting "in the wilderness the cedar, the shittah tree, and the myrtle, and the oil tree," and setting "in the desert the fir tree, and pine tree, and box tree together," and the wilderness budding and blossoming as the rose, and causing waters to bring forth in the wilderness, etc., as in Is. 41:18-20, and ch. 35:1-7, and 43:19-20, and 32:15 and 29:17.[^004-note66]

And as we are to understand those places of waters in the desert, the wilderness blossoming as the rose, and the desert bringing forth such beautiful, pleasant trees; so undoubtedly are we to understand those things in the 47th chapter of Ezekiel, of that river of living waters issuing out of the sanctuary and going down into the desert, and from thence into the sea and the waters of the sea, being made fresh and sweet, and the very many pleasant, fruitful trees that should grow on either bank of the river, bringing forth new fruit every month with unfading leaves and unfailing fruit, whose fruit "shall be for meat, and leaves for medicine," vv. 7 and 12.[^004-note67]

There can be no such thing as a literal fulfillment of that, Is. 45:8, "Drop down, ye heavens, from above, and let the skies pour down righteousness: let the earth open, and let them bring forth salvation, and let righteousness spring up together"; and that, Ps. 72[:3], "The mountains shall bring peace unto the people, and the little hills, by righteousness." So it would be most absurd to understand that in a literal sense, Is. 41:15[-16], "Behold, I will make thee a new sharp threshing instrument having teeth: thou shalt thresh the mountains, and beat them small, and shall make the hills as chaff. Thou shalt fan them, and the wind shall carry them away, and the whirlwind shall scatter them." And innumerable other passages might be mentioned, where it is equally plain at first sight that it would be ridiculous to understand the prophecies according to the letter of them.[^004-note68]

3. Upon a supposition that the representations of outward, glorious things in the days of the Messiah are to be understood literally, the prophecies are exceeding inconsistent one with another.

As particularly, those prophecies that foretell all nations going up to the temple at Jerusalem and bringing their offerings to the altar of God there, as the children of Israel used to do, as particularly those, Is. 2:2-3, Mic. 4:1-2, and Is. 56:6-8 and Is. 60:6-13, Zech. 14:16-19. I say, those things are inconsistent with that in Is. 19:19, 21, "In that day there shall be an altar to the Lord in the midst of the land of Egypt, and a pillar at the border thereof to the Lord. . . . And the Lord shall be known to Egypt, and the Egyptians shall do sacrifice and oblation," etc.; and that, Mal. 1:11, "For from the rising of the sun even to the going down of the same, my name shall be great among the Gentiles; and in every place incense shall be offered unto my name, and a pure offering: for my name shall be great among the heathen, saith the Lord God"; Dan. 9:24, "Seventy weeks are determined . . . to finish the transgression, and to make an end of sins, and to make reconciliation for iniquity, and to bring in everlasting righteousness, and to seal up the vision and prophecy"; v. 27, "he shall cause the sacrifice and oblation to cease."

This that is said of all nations going up to the mountain of the house of the Lord, and being gathered in a great congregation there, as the children of Israel were wont to be, is also inconsistent with Ezekiel's description of the measures of the court of the temple, Ezek. 42:14 to the end; which, though very large, yet was in no wise large enough to contain such a congregation, nor if it were a hundred times as large. And what is inconsistent with both those things--viz. with all nations being gathered to the temple, and with the whole description of Ezekiel's temple, and all that is said about it, literally understood--is that elsewhere it is represented that there should be no temple or altar literally so, in the times of the Messiah's kingdom. Is. 66:1-2, "Thus saith the Lord, The heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool: where is the house that ye build unto me? and where is the place of my rest? For all those things hath mine hand made, and all those things have been, saith the Lord: but to this man will I look, even to him that is poor and of a contrite spirit, and trembles at my word." This is inconsistent with what is so often represented in the prophecies of God's reigning in Jerusalem and Mt. Zion, and in the temple, in the times of the Messiah. For this prophecy represents the throne of God as not in any earthly temple or city, but in heaven, at that time; and so his throne is represented as being in heaven, in Dan. 7:9-10, 13-14. Again, Jer. 3:16-17 is altogether inconsistent with there being any temple in the days of the Messiah's kingdom: "And it shall come to pass, when ye be multiplied and increased in the land, in those days, saith the Lord, they shall no more say, The ark of the covenant of the Lord: neither shall it come into mind: neither shall they remember it; neither shall they visit it; neither shall that be done any more. At that time they shall call Jerusalem the throne of the Lord; and all the nations shall be gathered together unto it, to the name of the Lord, to Jerusalem." This is inconsistent with the nations being gathered together to the temple, to offer sacrifices before the throne of God in the mountain of the temple. If Jerusalem itself be God's throne, instead of the ark and mercy seat above [or] upon[^004-note69] it in the temple, where is any room for the temple to contain and be the place of God's throne? Or shall we suppose that, instead of the house containing the throne of God, the throne of God contains the house of God? This overthrows the literal sense of all that is said in the prophecies of the times of the Messiah about the temple of the Lord, the mountain of the house of the Lord and offering sacrifices upon the altar of the Lord, and God's placing his tabernacle amongst his people, the business of the priests and Levites in the temple, the glory of the Lord in the temple.

What is said, Ezek. 45 and 46, of the ordinances of the Passover, that grand memorial of God's bringing the children of Israel up out of Egypt, if literally understood, is inconsistent with Jer. 16:14-15 and ch. 23:7-8, which imply that those memorials of that deliverance should cease. And so what is prophesied, Ezek. 46, of the sacrifices for the new moons, is inconsistent with Is. 60:20, "thy moon shall not withdraw it[self]"; i. e. it shall continue to shine upon thee without ceasing. But this is inconsistent with there being any new moons, for the moon does remarkably withdraw itself as it changes, and ceases to shine at all for many days together. And what is said in the same chapter of the sacrifices that were to be offered to God every morning, is inconsistent with what is said, Is. 60:20, "thy sun shall no more go down"; and if it no more goes down, then neither shall it any more arise, and so there will be no mornings. 'Tis also represented, in Zech. 14:6-7, that there shall be no more processions of day and night, and interchanges of light and darkness, but that it should be all one continued day, being light at the time that the evening or night used to be.

Again, these prophecies of there being no successions of day and night, if we understand all literally, are directly contradicted by Jer. 33:20-21, where is an implicit, strong declaration that there should be, in the days of the Messiah, day and night in their season. "Thus saith the Lord; If you can break my covenant of the day, and my covenant of the night, that there should not be day and night in their season; then may also my covenant be broken with David my servant," etc. Here is a plain prophecy of the Messiah, as appears by vv. 14-15. Putting these things together, therefore, the words do especially imply that that covenant of the day and night, should not be broken at the time of the fulfillment of this prophecy. Those prophecies of a continual day, are also inconsistent with the prophecies that speak of day and night in the days of the Messiah, as Is. 4:5, and 27:3, and 60:11 and 62:6.

Again, in Is. 60:19, it is prophesied that the sun should no more be continued for a light by day, nor the moon by night. But in Jer. 31:35-36, 'tis foretold that that ordinance of God, by which he appointed the sun to be a light by day and the moon and the stars for light by night, should continue still: "Thus saith the Lord, which giveth the sun for a light by day, and the ordinances of the moon and stars for a light by night, . . . If those ordinances depart from before me, saith the Lord, then the seed of Israel also shall cease from being a nation before me for ever."

In Is. 30:26, it is represented as though the light of the sun and moon should be vastly increased in the time of the prosperity and glory of the Messiah's kingdom. "Moreover the light of the moon shall be as the light of the sun, and the light of the sun shall be sevenfold, as the light of seven days, in the day that the Lord bindeth up the breach of his people, and healeth the stroke of their wound." And in other places, 'tis represented as though the sun and moon at that time should be darkened, and their light greatly diminished. Joel 2:31, "The sun shall be turned into darkness, and the moon into blood, before the great and terrible day of the Lord come"; and ch. 3:15-16, "The sun and the moon shall be darkened, and the stars shall withdraw their shining. The Lord shall shall roar out of Zion," etc.; v. 18, "And it shall come to pass in that day, that the mountains shall drop down new wine," etc.; Is. 24:23, "Then the moon shall be confounded, and the sun ashamed, when the Lord of hosts shall reign in mount Zion, [. . .] before his ancients gloriously."

In Is. 11:6-9 and 65:25, 'tis represented that lions and other beasts of prey should lose their rapacious nature, and that they should become harmless; and as such, they should remain in the land of Israel, and feed and lie down with their cattle, and be on God's holy mountain amongst God's people there, their little children being freely conversant with them. But in Is. 35:8-9, it seems to be supposed that their rapacious nature shall be continued, and that therefore they shall be kept at a distance and not suffered to come nigh God's people, or be in their way, so that they travel safely. "And an highway shall be there, and a way, and it shall be called The way of holiness;. . . No lion shall be there, nor any ravenous beasts shall go up thereon." And in Ezek. 34:25, "'tis represented that they shall ALL be destroyed out of the land, and not suffered to come amongst the cattle of God's people: "and I will cause the evil beasts to cease out of the land: and they shall dwell safely in the wilderness, and sleep in the woods."

There are many passages in the Prophets that seem to represent as though the mountains would all be removed and gone in the days of the Messiah, as Is. 2:14, "And the day of the Lord shall be upon all the high mountains, and upon all the hills that are lifted up"; Is. 40:4, "Every valley shall be exalted, and every mountain and hill shall be made low: and the crooked shall be made straight, and the rough places plain"; Is. 42:15-16, "I will make waste mountains and hills, and dry up all their herbs; and I will make the rivers islands, and I will dry up the pools. . . . I will make darkness light before them, and crooked things straight"; Is. 49:11, "I will make all my mountains a way"; Ezek. 38:20, "the mountains shall be thrown down, and the steep places shall fall." In Is. 41:15-16, 'tis represented that the mountains shall be threshed, and beat small, and made as chaff, and that the wind should carry them away. In Is. 64:1-2, 'tis represented that the mountains should be melted down before God. And yet there are many other prophecies that do imply that the mountains shall yet remain. Ps. 72:3, "The mountains shall bring peace unto the people, and the little hills, by righteousness"; and v. 16, "There shall be an handful of corn in the earth upon the top of the mountains"; Is. 44:23, "break forth into singing, ye mountains"; so Is. 49:13; ch. 55:12, "the mountains shall break forth before you into singing"; Is. 42:11, "Let the wilderness and the cities thereof lift up their voice, the villages that Kedar doth inhabit: let the inhabitants of the rock sing, let 'em shout from the top of the mountain," which implies that the mountains in the wilderness and foreign countries should yet remain, and not only the mountains of Israel; Joel 3:18, "The mountains shall drop new wine and the hills shall flow with milk"; Amos 9:13, "The mountains shall drop sweet wine"; and many other places. In Ezek. 37:27, 'tis foretold that God['s] people, in their future glorious day in the Messiah's times, should have God's tabernacle in the midst of 'em; but this is inconsistent with the prophecies in general that represent the manner of God's presence among his people as the most glorious: but his dwelling in the tabernacle was far inferior to his dwelling in the temple--see II Sam. 7--and besides, other prophecies speak of God's temple at that time.

In Is. 19,[^004-note70] 'tis represented that in the days of the Messiah, Egypt was to remain and be in blessed circumstances, being owned as God's people, so that God should say, "Blessed be Egypt my people"; and that Israel should be the third with Egypt and Assyria, rejoicing with them in the same privileges, there being an altar in Egypt, and should offer acceptable sacrifices to God; and God should send the people of Egypt a glorious savior. But in Joel 3:19-20, 'tis said that at that time, Egypt should be a desolation, while Judah and Jerusalem shall dwell in perpetual safety and prosperity. So in the same 19th of Isaiah, 'tis represented that Assyria should be admitted to the same priviledes with the land of Israel in the days of the Messiah. And yet in Mic. 5, 'tis represented that God's people at that time should lay waste the land of Assyria: see v. 2, "But thou, Bethlehem Ephratah, . . . out of thee shall come forth unto me he that is to be ruler in Israel"; v. 6, "And they shall waste the land of Assyria with the sword." So 'tis foretold that Babylon[^004-note71] should be perpetual desolations, and never should be built again. Is. 13:19-22, "And Babylon, the glory of kingdoms, the beauty of the Chaldees' excellency, shall be as when God overthrew Sodom and Gomorrah. It shall never be inhabited, neither shall be dwelt in from generation to generation: neither shall the Arabian pitch tent there; neither shall the shepherds make their folds there. But wild beasts of the desert shall be there; and their house shall be full of doleful creatures; and owls shall dwell there, and satyrs shall dance there. And the wild beasts of the island shall cry in their desolate houses, and dragons in their pleasant palaces: and her time is near to come, and her days shall not be prolonged." But yet 'tis foretold that Babylon should, in the days of the Messiah, be united to God's people. Ps. 87:4, "I will make mention of Rahab and Babylon to them that know me," etc.[^004-note72] In Ezek. 36:35, 'tis said of the cities of Israel, "the waste and desolate and ruined cities shall become fenced, and inhabited"; and yet in ch. 38:11, 'tis represented that the people should dwell "without walls, having neither bars nor gates." So also Zech. 2:4, "Jerusalem shall be inhabited as towns without walls."

The promises in most places are made to Israel, but if we should understand this of Israel according to the flesh, this would be inconsistent with other prophecies that speak of the Jews as being rejected and destroyed, and God's people's being called by another name, as Is. 65:1-2, "I am sought of them that asked not for me; I am found of them that sought me not: I said, Behold me, behold me, to a nation that was not called by my name. I have spread out my hands all the day unto a rebellious people"; vv. 6-7, "I will recompense, even recompense into your bosom, your iniquities, and the iniquities of your fathers together"; vv. 12-13, 15, "Therefore will I number you to the sword, and ye shall all bow down to the slaughter: because when I called, ye did not answer; . . . Therefore thus saith the Lord, Behold, my servants shall eat, but ye shall be hungry: . . . And ye shall leave your name for a curse to my chosen: for the Lord God shall slay thee, and call his servants by another name." But this may be shown more fully afterwards. This overthrows the literal sense of all that is said about rebuilding the temporal posterity, their sacrifices, festivals, etc.

Upon presupposition that the prophecies are all to be understood literally, there are some particular prophecies overthrow almost all the rest. Particularly it is foretold that this world, before the glory of the Messiah's kingdom, should be utterly destroyed. Is. 24:19-20, "The earth is utterly broken down, the earth is clean dissolved, the earth is moved exceedingly. The earth shall reel to and fro like a drunkard, and shall be removed like a cottage; and the transgression thereof shall be heavy upon it; and it shall fall, and not rise again"; Is. 34:5, "And all the host of heaven shall be dissolved, and the heavens shall be rolled together as a scroll: and all their host shall fall down, as the leaf falleth off from the vine, and as a falling fig from the fig tree." And it is often foretold that there should be new heavens and a new earth, Is. 66:17 and 51:16 and 66:22. This, if literally understood, overthrows all that is said about bringing God's people to the land of Canaan, bringing them from Egypt, Assyria and the isles; and rebuilding Jerusalem, and God's reigning on Mt. Sion; and such and such countries being brought into the kingdom of the Messiah; and the kingdom of the Messiah's being extended from the River Euphrates to the ends of the earth, etc., etc.

4. That supposition, that the representations of outward glorious things in the days of the Messiah are to be understood literally, don't only render different prophecies inconsistent one with another; but it renders several of those particular prophecies, wherein these representations are contained, inconsistent with themselves. As in the 34th of Isaiah, 9th and 10th verses, it is foretold concerning Idumea, that "the streams thereof shall be turned into pitch, and the dust thereof into brimstone," and that "the land thereof shall become burning pitch," and that "it shall not be quenched night nor day," and "the smoke thereof shall go up for ever"; that "from generation to generation it should lie waste," and "none shall pass through it for ever and ever." But yet, in the very next words, in an utter inconsistence with the land's being thus in a state of perpetual conflagration, it is added, v. 11, "But the cormorant and the bittern shall possess it; the owl also and the raven shall dwell in it"; and v. 13, etc., "And thorns shall come up in her palaces, nettles and brambles in the fortress thereof: and it shall be an habitation of dragons, and a court for owls. The wild beasts of the desert shall also meet with the wild beasts of the island, and the satyr shall cry to his fellow; and the shriek-owl shall rest there, and find for herself a place of rest. There shall the great owl make her nest, and lay, and hatch, and gather under her shadow: there shall the vultures also be gathered, every one with her mate. Seek ye out of the book of the Lord, and read: no one of these shall fail, none shall want her mate: for my mouth hath commanded, and his spirit hath gathered them. And he hath cast the lot for them, and his hand hath divided it unto them by line: they shall possess it for ever, from generation to generation shall they dwell therein."

Again, in Is. 60:20, it is said that the "sun shall no more go down" upon Jerusalem." And yet, in the same chapter, v. 11, 'tis implied that there be a succession of day and night: "Therefore thy gates shall be open continually; they shall not be shut day nor night."

And those prophecies of the creation of new heavens and a new earth, Is. 65:17 and 66:22, are, if literally understood, inconsistent with the literal sense of other things in those very chapters, as this, ch. 65:9-10, "And I will bring forth a seed out of Jacob, and out of Judah an inheritor of my mountains: and mine elect shall inherit it, and my servants shall dwell there. And Sharon shall be a field of flocks, and the valley of Achor a place for the herds to lie down in"; and that, ch. 66:19, "And I will set a sign among them, and will send those that escape of them to the nations to Tarshish, Pul, and Lud, that draw the bow, to Tubal, and Javan, to the isles afar off, that have not heard my fame." There are all countries and places of the old earth, which are herein represented as yet remaining.

5. It seems to be intimated by God himself, that when he had exhibited things by the prophets in their visions and prophecies, he had made use of figurative and typical representations. Hos. 12:10, "I have also spoken by the prophets, and I have multiplied visions, and used similitudes, by the ministry of the prophets."

6. The prophets, in many instances when they make representations of glorious external things, do apply these representations to spiritual things, and explain 'em of them; as that remarkable prophecy in Is. 65:17, of God's creating new heavens and a new earth, that "the former may not be remembered, nor come into mind," is explained in a spiritual sense, in the words immediately following, to signify the destruction of the people of God: "But be ye glad and rejoice for ever in that which I create: for, behold, I create Jerusalem a rejoicing, and her people a joy." In Is. 2:13-14, it is foretold that "the day of the Lord" should be "upon all the cedars of Lebanon, that are high and lifted up, and upon all the oaks of Bashan, and upon all the high mountains, and upon all the hills that are lifted up." But this in the 17th verse following, is explained in a spiritual sense, for abasing the pride of men: "And the loftiness of man shall be bowed down, and the haughtiness of men shall be made low: and the Lord alone shall be exalted in that day." And whereas the prophecies often speak of a glorious throne, on which God shall appear and reign in the days of the Messiah, the prophet Jeremiah, ch. 3:17, declares what that throne is, and shows it to be spiritual, and not anything material like the golden throne over the ark, viz. the church of God: "they shall no more say, the ark of the covenant of the Lord: [. . .] At that time they shall call Jerusalem the throne of the Lord." And whereas the prophecies often speak of the great glory of the temple in the days of the Messiah,the prophet Haggai represents that glory, not to an external glory, as that which is by silver and gold, but a spiritual glory, viz. the glorious manifestations of God's presence and grace, in that place, Hag. 2:7-9, "the desire of all nations shall come: and I will fill this house with glory, saith the Lord of hosts. The silver is mine, and the gold is mine, saith the Lord of hosts. The glory of this latter house shall be greater than of the former, saith the Lord of hosts: and in this place will I give peace, saith the Lord of hosts." And so other things that appertain to the external glory of God's people that is represented in the Prophets, are explained of things spiritual, by the prophets themselves. Their strong fortresses, walls, and bulwarks and gates, are explained to be spiritual, Is. 26:1, "We have a strong city; salvation will God appoint for walls and bulwarks"; Is. 60:18, "thou shalt call thy walls Salvation, and thy gates Praise"; Zech. 2:4-5, "Jerusalem shall be inhabited as towns without walls . . . For I, saith the Lord, will be unto her for a wall of fire round about, and will be the glory in the midst of her"; and Is. 33:6, "And wisdom and knowledge shall be the stability of thy times, and strength of salvation." And so their riches, so often spoken of, is explained to be spiritual there, in the next words: "the fear of the Lord is his treasure." So their bountiful and glorious clothing is explained to be spiritual, even their holiness and righteousness. Is. 52:1, "put on thy beautiful garments, O Jerusalem, the holy city: for henceforth there shall no more come unto thee the uncircumcised and the unclean"; and Zech. 3:4, "Take away the filthy garments from him. And unto him he said, Behold, I have caused thine iniquity to pass from thee, and I will clothe thee with change of raiment." And their glorious ornaments, and beautiful crown and diadem, is explained to be spiritual, Is. 28:5, "In that day shall the Lord of hosts be for a crown of glory, and for a diadem of beauty, to the residue of this people." In Is. 4:6, it is foretold, that in the days of the Messiah, God's people should have refuge or hiding places, and a shadow from the heat and a covert from storm and from wind. But in ch. 32:2, this matter is explained, and 'tis shown to be a spiritual hiding place from the wind and storm, and a shadow from the heat, that God's people shall then have, viz. the Messiah himself: "And a man shall be an hiding place from the wind, and a covert from the tempest; . . . and the shadow of a great rock in a weary land." We often read in the Prophets of rivers of water that God will give in the parched wilderness, or dry and thirsty land. But in the same Is. 32:2, it is shown what those rivers of water in a dry land are; and 'tis explained of that which is spiritual: "a man shall be [. . .] rivers of water in a dry place." And again, ch. 33:21, "the glorious Jehovah will be unto us a place of broad rivers and streams": this further shows how we are to understand that river of living water that goes down from Mt. Zion into the desert, spoken of in the 37th chapter of Ezekiel. And as we are to understand that part of the description, which Ezekiel gives us, of the state of things in the temple and land of Israel, and of the world under the Messiah, so doubtless we are understand the whole description contained in the nine last chapters of Ezekiel's prophecy.

And whereas the glory of the days of the Messiah is frequently set forth by a great increase of light--that the light of the moon shall be as the light of the sun, and the light of the sun sevenfold, and that there shall be no interruption of darkness by successions of day and night, but a continual light without the sun's going down--but there are several passages in the Prophet that show that those things are to be understood in a spiritual sense, and that this sun and this light are spiritual. Is. 60:19, "The sun shall no more be thy light by day; neither for brightness shall the moon give light unto thee: but the Lord shall be unto thee an everlasting light, and thy God thy glory"; and Mal. 4:2, "But unto you that fear my name shall the Sun of righteousness," etc. The anointing of the Messiah is explained of a spiritual anointing; see forward, §32.
