For thus saith the Lord; David shall never want a man to sit upon the throne of the house of Israel; neither shall the priests the Levites want a man before me to offer burnt offerings, and to kindle meat offerings, and to do sacrifice continually"; Ps. 16:4, "their drink offerings of blood will I not offer, nor take up their names into my lips"; Ezek. 45:17, "And it shall be the prince's part to give burnt offerings, and meat offerings, and drink offerings, in the feasts, and in the new moons, and in the sabbaths, in all solemnities of the house of Israel," together with the context, and also ch. 46:1-7.

76.[^005-note23] It was foretold that the sacrifice that this great high priest should offer up to God should be himself, which he should offer up to God in his death, so that he should die as a sacrifice to make atonement for the sins of men. Is. 53:10, "thou shalt make his soul an offering for sin."

77. It was foretold that the Messiah should not suffer for any fault of his own, but that in his sufferings and death he should bear the punishment of our sins, that we might escape and be delivered from that punishment. Dan. 9:26, "after threescore and two weeks shall Messiah be cut off, but not for himself"; Is. 53:4-5, "Surely he hath borne our griefs, and carried our sorrows: yet we did esteem him stricken, smitten of God, and afflicted. But he was wounded for our transgressions, he was bruised for our iniquities: the chastisement of our peace was upon him; and with his stripes we are healed"; v. 9, "he had done no violence, neither was deceit found in his mouth"; in v. 11 he is called God's "righteous servant," and 'tis said, he "shall justify many, for he shall bear their iniquities"; v. 12, "he was numbered with the transgressors; and he bare the sin of many"; Ps. 69:4, "They that hate me without a cause are more than the hairs of mine head: they that would destroy me, being mine enemies wrongfully, are mighty: then I restored that which I took not away." This well agrees with Christ's making satisfaction by his sufferings for that injury which he had not done.

"It is a common thing among the Jews to call the Messiah, *Isch Copher*, i. e. The Appeaser." "See the Chaldee Paraphrase on Cant. 1:14. R. Judas in *Chasidim*, and R. Simeon in *Bereschith Rabba*, say, that the Messiah should bear our sins." See Grotius, *de Verit.*, Bk. 5, §15.[^005-note24]

78.[^005-note25] It was foretold that all the debt that sinners owe to Gods' justice should be exacted of the Messiah, and that he shall bear the whole punishment of the sin that he suffered for. Is. 53:6, "All we like sheep are gone astray; we have turned every one to his own way; and the Lord hath laid on him the iniquity of us all." This is spoken of here, as showing how great the burden was that was laid upon him; but there would be no argument in it, unless the whole of the debt was exacted of him, and not only some part. Ps. 69:4-5, "then I restored that which I took not away. O God, thou knowest my foolishness; my sins are not hid from thee" (see notes on the verse).[^005-note26]

79.[^005-note27] It was foretold that the Messiah should make a proper atonement for sin by his sacrifice, such as was never made before, and which never any of the ancient sacrifices of beasts could do. Thus making reconciliation for iniquity is foretold as a new thing, Dan. 9:24, "Seventy weeks are determined upon thy people and upon thy holy city, to finish the transgression, and to make an end of sin, and to make reconciliation for iniquity," implying that making reconciliation for iniquity was a thing future, and not what ever yet had been; Zech. 13:1, "In that day there shall be a fountain opened to the house of David and the inhabitants of Jerusalem for sin and for uncleanness." Compare these with Is. 40:16, "And Lebanon is not sufficient to burn, nor the beasts thereof for a burnt offering"; and Mic. 6:6-7, "shall I come before him with burnt offerings, with calves of a year old? will the Lord be pleased with thousands of rams, and ten thousands of rivers of oil? shall I give my firstborn for my transgression, the fruit of my body for the sin of my soul?"; and Ps. 40:6, "Sacrifice and offering thou didst not desire; mine ears hast thou opened: burnt offering and sin offering hast thou not required"; and Ps 50:8, etc., "I will not reprove thee for thy sacrifices or thy burnt offerings, to have been continually before me. I will take no bullock out of thine house, nor he goats out of thy folds. For every beast of the forest is mine, and the cattle on a thousand hills. I know all the fowls of the mountains: and the wild beasts of the field are mine. If I were hungry, I would not tell thee: for the world is mine, and the fullness thereof. Will I eat the flesh of bulls, or drink the blood of goats?"; Ps. 51:16, "thou desiredst not sacrifices; else would I give it: thou delightest not in burnt offering."

80. It was foretold that the sacrifice of the Messiah should make full satisfaction to God's justice, such as should make all other sacrifice after that needless. Dan. 9:24, "Seventy weeks are determined upon thy people and upon thy holy city, to finish the transgression, and to make an end of sins, and to make reconciliation for iniquity, and to bring in everlasting righteousness," with v. 27, "in the midst of the week" (or "in the half of the week") "he shall make the sacrifice and oblation to cease." 'Tis evident that in v. 24, "to finish the transgression, and make an end of sin, and to make reconciliation for iniquity, and to bring in an everlasting righteousness," are various expressions to signify the same glorious and wonderful work that shall be done, and event that shall come to pass. The word in the original translated, "make reconciliation for iniquity," is the same that is used in the Law for making atonement by sacrifice, so that the words imply that the Messiah should offer such an atonement for sin as should make an end of, or consume, the transgression (as the word is), and seal up sin (as in the margin);[^005-note28] i. e. quite complete the business of reconciliation, so that there should be no further occasion for going about to make reconciliation, or offer any further atonement for, or bring sin any more to remembrance as a thing to be atoned for. So that by these expressions it seems as if sacrifices for sin thenceforward must cease, and be made to cease by the Messiah; as v. 27, 'tis expressly said they should [cease] at that very time spoken of in this verse, viz. in the last half of [the] week of the seventy weeks. Putting these things together, we can't understand those prophecies otherwise, than that the Messiah should offer up such a sacrifice to atone for sin as should render all other sacrifices and oblations for sin needless, and should put an end to them.

The same thing is evidently implied in that, Zech. 3:8-9, "for, behold, I will bring forth my servant the Branch. For behold the stone that I have laid before Joshua; upon one stone shall be seven eyes: behold, I will engrave the graving thereof, saith the Lord of hosts, *and I will remove the iniquity of that land in one day.*" A thus removing the iniquity of God's people is here spoken of as a new and extraordinary thing, some great thing whereby that shall be brought to pass that is very different from what used to be: as much as to say, "Removing their iniquity shall not be a thing continually a-doing by long and continued series of sacrifices, by which there is continually remembrance made of sins anew, signifying that a complete atonement was not yet [to] be made; but the business shall then be finished, when the BRANCH is brought forth, in one day, and shall not remain still to be done, but the work of reconciliation and atoning for sin shall be, from that day forward, forever completed."

It was foretold that in the days of the Messiah there should be a wonderful reconciliation of mercy and truth, justice and peace, so that they shall meet together and entirely agree in the same salvation of God's people. Ps. 85:9-10,[^005-note29] "Surely his salvation is nigh them that fear him; that glory may dwell in our land. Mercy and truth are met together; righteousness and peace have kissed each other."

81.[^005-note30] That the incarnation of the Son of God, and the Divinity's being united to the human nature of the Messiah, was in order to his making atonement for sin, is agreeable to Zech. 3:9, "For behold the stone that I have laid before Joshua; upon one stone shall be seven eyes: behold, I will engrave the graving thereof, saith the Lord of hosts, and I will remove the iniquity of that land in one day." I have before shown from the Old Testament, that the Divinity was to be united to humanity in the person of the Messiah.[^005-note31] This verse seems to contain a prophecy of it: for those seven eyes, as they are interpreted in the next chapter, v. 10, "are the eyes of the Lord," or the divine Wisdom, or Logos. These are, by an immediate and wonderful work of God, inherent in a stone, so as to become the eyes of a stone, a thing in itself very far from sight, as the human nature is in itself weak and blind, and far from divinity. But the words that immediately follow[^005-note32] in the end of the verse, seem to represent the end and design of God in this, is wholly and perfectly to remove the iniquity of his people.

82. It appears by the prophecies of the Old Testament, that the redemption and salvation of God's people, so often foretold by the prophets to be in the time of the Messiah, should be by the blood of the Messiah. By what has been said, it appears that the blood of the sacrifice that should be offered for God's people in the days of the Messiah, should be the Messiah's own blood. But it is foretold that it should be by the blood of that sacrifice that the redemption of God's people should be. Zech. 9:9-11, "Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion; shout, O daughter of Jerusalem: behold, thy King cometh unto thee: . . . and he shall speak peace to the heathen: . . . As for thee also, by the blood of thy covenant I have sent forth thy prisoners out of the pit wherein there is no water"; Is. 53:5, "the chastisement of our peace was upon him; and by his stripes we are healed."

It was foretold that the Messiah's sucess in his great work, which is this work of salvation, be given as a consequence and reward of his doing the will of God in his sufferings. Is. 53:10-11, "when thou shalt make his soul an offering for sin, he shall see his seed, . . . the pleasure of the Lord shall prosper in his hands. He shall see of the travail of his soul, and be satisfied: . . . he shall justify many," etc.

83.[^005-note33] It was foretold that God should be well pleased and delighted with the perfect righteousness of the Messiah, as his servant and subject to his law, as that which magnifies the law and makes it honorable. Is. 42:19-21, "Who is blind, but my servant? or deaf, as my messenger that I sent? who is blind as he that is perfect, and blind as the Lord's servant? Seeing many things, but thou observest not; opening the ears, but he heareth not. The Lord is well pleased for his righteousness' sake; he will magnify the law, and make it honorable."

84.[^005-note34] It was foretold that the Messiah should justify others, or make others righteous by his righteousness that he should perform as God's servant, as one subject to his authority and commands; and especially the righteousness he wrought out in laying down his life. Is. 53:11-12, "by his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many" (or "make many righteous," *JatzdÎk TzaidÎk*): "for he shall bear their iniquities. Therefore will I divide him a portion with the great, and he shall divide the spoil with the strong; because he hath poured out his soul unto death." By the Messiah's "knowledge" seems to be only another word for righteousness, as oftentimes it is in the Old Testament; and as his seeing and hearing is put for his obedience, Is. 42:19-21. Jer. 23:6, "this is the name by which he shall be called, The Lord Our Righteousness."

85.[^005-note35] That Christ should thus, by his sufferings at Jerusalem, or in the land of Canaan, procure salvation and blessedness for mankind, is agreeable to Is. 25:6-8, "And in this mountain shall the Lord of hosts make unto all people a feast of fat things," etc., "And he shall destroy in the mountain the face of the covering," etc., "He will swallow up death in victory."

86.[^005-note36] That the wisdom of God should be marvelously displayed in and through the Messiah, and in the way of salvation by him, seems pretty plainly to be one thing signified in that, Zech. 3:9, "behold the stone that I have laid before Joshua; upon one shall be seven eyes: behold, I will engrave the graving thereof, [. . .] and I will remove the iniquity of that land in one day." See ch. 4, v. 10, "For who hath despised the day of small things? for they shall rejoice, and shall see the plummet in the hand of Zerubbabel with those seven; they are the eyes of the Lord, which run to and fro through the whole earth."

87.[^005-note37] It was foretold that forgiveness of sin should be one great benefit by which God should wonderfully display his grace through the Messiah. Dan. 9:24, "Seventy weeks are determined upon thy people and upon thy holy city, to finish the transgression, and to make an end of sins, and to make reconciliation for iniquity, and to bring in everlasting righteousness"; Zech. 3:4, "Take away the filthy garments from him. And unto him he said, Behold, I have caused thine iniquity to pass from thee, and I will clothe thee with change of raiment"; vv. 8-9, "I will bring forth my servant the Branch. . . . upon one stone shall be seven eyes: [. . .] and I will remove the iniquity of that land in one day"; Zech. 13:1, "In that day there shall be a fountain opened to the house of David and to the inhabitants of Jerusalem for sin and for uncleanness"; Ps.[^005-note38] 65:2-3, "O thou that hearest prayer, unto thee shall all flesh come. Iniquities prevail against me: as for our transgressions, thou shalt purge them away"; Joel 3:21, "For I will cleanse their blood that I have not cleansed: for the Lord dwelleth in Zion"; Jer. 31:34, "for they shall all know me, from the least of them unto the greatest of them, saith the Lord: for I will forgive their iniquity, and I will remember their sin no more"; Jer. 33:8, "And I will cleanse them from all their iniquity, whereby they have sinned against me; and I will pardon all their iniquities, whereby they have sinned, and whereby they have transgressed against me"; v. 15, "and I will cause the Branch of righteousness," etc.; Ps. 85:2, "Thou hast forgiven the iniquity of thy people, thou hast covered all their sin," with v. 10, "Mercy and truth are met together," etc.; Is. 43:25, "I, even I, am he that blotteth out thy transgressions for mine own sake, and will not remember thy sins"; Is. 44:22-23, "I have blotted out, as a thick cloud, thy transgressions, and, as a cloud, thy sins: return unto me; for I have redeemed thee. Sing, O ye heavens; for the Lord hath done it," etc.; Mic. 7:18-19, "Who is a God like unto thee, that pardoneth iniquity, and passeth by the transgression of the remnant of his heritage? he retaineth not his anger for ever, because he delighteth in mercy. He will turn us again, he will have compassion on us; he will subdue our iniquities; and thou wilt cast all their sins into the depths of the sea."[^005-note39]

[88.][^005-note40] It is agreeable to ancient prophecy that the Messiah should obtain forgiveness of such sins as no sacrifice was appointed for in the Law, and had not promises of forgiveness in that Law, viz. contumary and willful, obstinate rebellion. This is implied in that, Ps. 68:18, "Thou hast ascended on high, thou hast led captivity captive: thou hast received gifts for men; yea, for the REBELLIOUS also, that the Lord God might dwell among them." The word *sorerim*, translated "rebellious," signifies contumacious, stubborn, willfully disobedient, as is evident by the common use of it in the Old Testament. See Deut. 21:18, 20; Ps. 66:7 and 78:8, Prov. 7:11, Is. 1:23 and 65:2, Jer. 6:28, Hos. 9:15, Zech. 7:11. But for such wickedness as this, no sacrifice was appointed in the Law of Moses, and no encouragement given in that Law that ever it should be forgiven; and that Law gave no hope (excepting as the gospel was hid in that Law, or gospel grace obscurely revealed or hinted therein) that ever such sin should be forgiven. See Num. 15:30-31 and Deut. 29:18-21.

89. Whereas the prophecies do very often speak of great honor [and] glory that the Messiah should be possessed of, and also speak of a state of great meanness, abasement and suffering, even to the ignominious death: the prophecies do signify that his low, suffering state should be first, and his exaltation and hlory afterwards. Ezek. 17:22-24, "Thus saith the Lord God; I will also take of the highest branch of the high cedar, and will set it; *I will crop off* from the top of his young twigs a tender one, *and I will plant it upon an high mountain and eminent*. In the mountain of the height of Israel will I plant it; and it shall bring forth boughs, and bear fruit, and be a goodly cedar: and under it shall dwell all fowl of every wing; in the shadow of the branches thereof shall they dwell. And all the trees of the field shall know that I the Lord have brought down the high tree, *have exalted the low tree*, have dried up the green tree, and *made the dry tree to flourish*"; Ps. 118:22, "The stone which the builders refused is become the head of the corner," with the context. So the latter part of the 53rd chapter of Isaiah, speaks of the Messiah's exaltation as following his humiliation and sufferings, and 52nd chapter, 13th verse, "my servant [. . .] shall be exalted and extolled and be very high"; [vv.] 14-15, "As many were astonied at thee; his visage was so marred more than any man, and his form more than the sons of men: so shall he sprinkle many nations; the kings shall shut their mouths at him"; Ps. 69:29, "But I am poor and sorrowful: let thy salvation, O God, set me up on high."

90.[^005-note41] By the prophecies of the Old Testament, the Messiah's success in his great undertaking of saving of his people and the conversion of his followers, and the enlargement of his kingdom and the prosperity, honor, joy and triumph of his people, should chiefly follow the Messiah's humiliation and sufferings. This appears by the 22nd, and 69th, and 102nd and 118th Psalms; and Is. 52:14-15,[^005-note42] "As many were astonied," etc., "So shall he sprinkle many nations"; Is. 53:10-12, "when thou shalt make his soul an offering for sin, he shall see his seed, he shall prolong his days, and the pleasure of the Lord shall prosper in his hand. He shall see of the travail of his soul, and be satisfied: by his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many; for he shall bear their iniquities. Therefore will I divide him a portion with the great, and he shall divide the spoil with the strong; because he hath poured out his soul unto death"; and in the beginning of the next chapter [Is. 54:1], "Sing, O barren, thou that didst not bear."

91.[^005-note43] The prophecies represent, agreeable to the doctrine of the New Testament concerning Jesus Christ, that both the Messiah's own personal glory and exaltation, and also the conversion and salvation, and joy and glory of his people, should not only follow his humiliation and sufferings, but be dependent thereon, and given as the reward thereof. So the matter is represented in Is. 52:13-15, and Is. 53:10-12 and 54:1-2, etc.

92.[^005-note44] By the prophecies of the Old Testament, the deliverance, joy and prosperity of the Messiah's people should depend on his deliverance and exaltation, as being their head; so that in his deliverance they shall be delivered, and in his exaltation they shall be exalted, and that they shall reign with him. In the 22nd Psalm, from the 19th [verse] to the end, 'tis represented that in the Messiah's deliverance and exaltation, his people should be delivered and exalted, as in their head. So in Ps. 69:20 to the end, and Ps. 102:10 to the end, and Ps. 118:17 to the end; and Is. 53:12 with 54:1-2, etc.; and Ps. 60:5, "That thy beloved may be delivered; save with thy right hand, and hear me"; and Ps. 108:6, the same words; Is. 26:19, "Thy dead men shall live, together with my dead body shall they arise. Awake and sing, ye that dwell in the dust."

That the Messiah's people should be exalted in the Messiah's exaltation, and should reign with him, is foretold, Zech. 6:10 to to the end:

Take of them of the captivity, even of Heldai, of Tobijah, and of Jedaiah, which are come from Babylon, and come thou the same day, and go into the house of Josiah the son of Zephaniah; then take silver and gold, and make crowns, and set them upon the head of Joshua" (or "Jesus") "the son of Josedech, the high priest; and speak unto him, saying, Thus speaketh the Lord of hosts, saying, Behold the man whose name is The BRANCH; and he shall grow up out of his place, and he shall build the temple of the Lord: and he shall bear the glory, and he shall sit and rule upon his throne: and he shall be a priest upon his throne; and the counsel of peace shall be between them both. And the crowns shall be to Helem, and Tobijah, and to Hen the son of Zephaniah, for a memorial in the temple of the Lord. And they that are far off shall come and build in the temple of the Lord.

And that God's people shall reign in great glory with the Messiah, is also implied in Dan. 7:13-14, "I saw in the night visions, and, behold, one like the Son of man came with the clouds of heaven, and came to the Ancient of days, and they brought him near before him. And there was given him dominion, and glory, and a kingdom that all people, nations, and languages, should serve him: his dominion is an everlasting dominion, which shall not pass away, and his kingdom that which shall not be destroyed"; taken with the explanation of this vision, v. 18, "But the saints of the most High shall take the kingdom, and possess the kingdom for ever, even for ever and ever"; and v. 22, "Until the Ancient of days came, and the judgment was given to the saints of the most High; and the time came that the saints possessed the kingdom"; and v. 27, "And the kingdom and dominion, and the greatness of the kingdom into the whole heaven, shall be given to the people of the saints of the most High, whose kingdom is an everlasting kingdom, and all dominions shall serve and obey him"; Ps. 45:6-7, "Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever: the scepter of thy kingdom is a right scepter. Thou lovest righteousness, and hatest wickedness: therefore God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows"; with v. 9, "upon thy right hand did stand the queen in gold of Ophir"; and vv. 15-16, "With gladness and rejoicing shall they be brought: they shall enter into the king's palace. Instead of thy fathers shall be thy children, whom thou mayst make princes in all the earth."

How agreeable are these things in ancient prophecies with such passages of the New Testament, as these:[^005-note45] "I appoint unto you a kingdom, even as my Father hath appointed unto me" [^005-note46]

93.[^005-note47] It was foretold that the Messiah should rise from the dead to a glorious and happy life, and to die no more, and that the eternal life he should receive should be a reward for his doing the will of God in his sufferings; and it was also foretold that he should rise so soon, that it should be before there was time for his body to corrupt, and particularly that he should rise the third day. The things that have been observed in the 87th and 89th sections preceding, do imply that the Messiah should rise from the dead, and that he should rise to a more glorious and happy life, and that this life and happiness are given in reward for his sufferings. Ps. 16:9-11, "Therefore my heart is glad, and my glory rejoiceth: my flesh also shall rest in hope. For thou wilt not leave my soul in hell; neither wilt thou suffer thine Holy One to see corruption. Thou wilt show me the path of life: in thy presence is fullness of joy; at thy right hand are pleasures for evermore." Here 'tis foretold that the Messiah should rise before his flesh should see corruption (which at that temperate season of the year, in which was the Passover, is within three days--Lazarus, about that season, lay four days in the grave, and therefore of him his sister said, "by this time he stinketh" [John 11:39]); and that he should rise to a most happy life, in that after his death God would show him the path of life which led to his presence, where is fullness of joy, and where are great pleasures. And 'tis implied that he should rise to eternal life in that expression, where "are pleasures for evermore."

Is. 26:19, "Thy dead men shall live, together with my dead body shall they arise"; Ps. 22:15, "thou hast brought me into the dust of death," with v. 20, "Deliver my soul from the sword," and [v.] 24, "he hath not despised nor abhorred the affliction of the afflicted; neither hath he hid his face from him; but when he cried unto him, he heard"; Ps. 102:3, "my days are consumed like smoke," and v. 11, "My days are like a shadow that declineth; I am withered like grass"; v. 23, "He weakened my strength in the way; he shortened my days." These things imply the death of the Messiah; and from what follows may well be inferred that he should rise again and live forever: "I said, O my God, take me not away in the midst of my days: thy years are throughout all generations. . . . the earth and the heavens . . . shall perish, but thou shalt endure: . . . thou art the same, and thy years have no end. The children of thy servant shall continue, and their seed shall be established before thee" [Ps. 102:24-28].

In Is. 53:10, "when thou shalt make his soul an offering for sin, he shall see his seed, he shall prolong his days": here, in the same sentence wherein there is mention made of his making his soul an offering for sin (which means his dying, as appears by vv. 7-9, 12), it is declared that when this shall be done, or after this, he shall prolong his days. This implies that his life shall be restored, and when restored, shall be remarkably continued and prolonged. And if we take this with the two following verses, it evidently appears that this new, long life, is spoken of as what should be given as a reward for his doing the will of God in his sufferings.

And whereas it is very plainly and abundantly foretold that the Messiah should die--as has been already shown[^005-note48]--take these prophecies together with those that foretell the Messiah's living or reigning forever, and his rising again from the dead is implied. That the Messiah should live and reign forever, is often foretold. Ps. 45:2, "God hath blessed thee for ever"; v. 6, "Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever"; Ps. 21:4, "He asked life of thee, and thou gavest it him, even length of days for ever and ever," and v. 6, "For thou hast made him most blessed for ever: thou hast made him exceeding glad with thy countenance"; Ps. 61:6-8, "Thou wilt prolong the king's life: and his years as many generations. He shall abide before God for ever: O prepare mercy and truth, which may preserve him. So will I sing praise unto thy name for ever"; Ps. 110:4, "The Lord hath sworn, and will not repent, Thou art a priest for ever"; Ps. 72:5, "They shall fear thee as long as the sun and moon endure, throughout all generations"; v. 7, "In his days shall the righteous flourish; and abundance of peace so long as the moon endureth"; v. 15, "And he shall live, and to him shall be given of the gold of Sheba: prayer also shall be made for him continually; and daily shall he be praised"; Is. 9, v. 6, "his name shall be called [. . .] everlasting Father"; v. 7, "Of the increase of his government and peace there shall no end, upon the throne of David, and upon his kingdom, to order it and to establish it with judgment and with justice from henceforth even for ever"; Ezek. 37:25, "my servant David shall be their prince for ever"; Dan. 7:13-14, "I saw in the night visions, one like the Son of man . . . his dominion is an everlasting dominion, which shall not pass away, and his kingdom that which shall not be destroyed"; and also vv. 18 and 27. Job speaks of him as having life that is immortal, whose life therein differs from his that should yield to death and corruption. Job. 19:24-26, "I know that my redeemer liveth, [. . .] and though after my skin worms destroy this body," etc.; Jer. 33:15, "In those days, and at that time, I will cause the Branch of righteousness to grow up unto David," with v. 17, "For thus saith the Lord; David shall never want a man to sit upon the throne of the house of Israel," with vv. 20-21, "If you can break my covenant of the day, and my covenant of the night," etc., "then may also my covenant be broken with David my servant, that he should not have a son to reign on his throne."

'Tis particularly foretold that the Messiah should rise the third day. Hos. 6:2, "After two days he will revive us: in the third day he will raise us up," taken with Is. 26:19, "Thy dead men shall live, together with my dead body shall they arise."

94.[^005-note49] By the ancient prophecies, the Messiah, after his rising from the dead, was to ascend into heaven, and in his exaltation (which I have shown the prophecies represent as following his humiliation and death)[^005-note50] he was to have his throne in heaven.

In the three last verses of the 16th Psalm, 'tis foretold that when the Messiah shall rise from the dead, God will show him the path to that blessed abode, which is the place of his own glorious presence, and where he should be at God's right hand: "my flesh shalt rest in hope. For thou wilt not leave my soul in hell; neither wilt thou suffer thine Holy One to see corruption. Thou wilt show me the path of life: in thy presence is fullness of joy; and at thy right hand are pleasures for evermore." But the scriptures of the Old Testament do represent heaven as especially God's dwelling place, the place of his throne and glorious presence. This is very often called God's dwelling place. 1 Kgs. 8:30, "when they shall pray towards this place" (i. e. towards the temple), "then hear thou in heaven thy dwelling place"; so vv. 39, 43 and 49. Here 'tis observable that Solomon, at the dedication of the temple, often distinguishes between the temple and God's dwelling place. Heaven is called God's temple, and his throne is said to be in heaven. Ps. 11:4, "The Lord is in his holy temple, the Lord's throne is in heaven." This is called God's "holy habitation," Deut 26:15, so Jer. 25:30; and "the place of his habitation," and "his holy dwelling place," II Chron. 30:27, Ps. 33:13-14. 'Tis called his sanctuary, Ps. 102:19, "for he hath looked down from the height of his sanctuary." 'Tis said that "the Lord hath prepared his throne in the heavens," Ps. 103:19. Heaven is called God's tabernacle, Job. 36:29, "who can understand [. . .] the noise of his tabernacle?" Yea, 'tis said to be God's throne, Is. 66:1, "The heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool"; Job 26:9, "He holdeth back the place of his throne, and spreadeth his cloud upon it." Heaven is represented as God's dwelling place in opposition to the earth, which is spoken of as the proper dwelling place of men. Ps. 115:16, "The heaven, even the heavens, are the Lords: but the earth hath he given to the children of men"; Eccles. 5:2,[^005-note51] "God is in heaven, and thou in the earth." And therefore, when God comes to dwell on earth, it is always spoken of as coming down from heaven; so he is represented as coming down on Mt. Sinai and into the temple. And his dwelling anywhere on earth is spoken of as his great condescension, stooping to dwell below his proper abode to dwell in man's abode. So Solomon speaks of it as a great wonder, that God will dwell on earth in his temple. And that God should place his tabernacle with the children of Israel, is spoken of as a wonderful act of condescension, Ezek. 37:27. And God in coming to dwell on earth, is spoken of as renting the heavens and bowing the heavens, Is. 64 at the beginning.[^005-note52] Heaven is called the high and holy place where God dwells, Is. 57:15, "thus saith the high and lofty One who inhabiteth eternity, whose name is Holy; I dwell in the high and holy place." And heaven is represented as the place of God's glorious presence. Is. 63[:15], "Look down from heaven, and behold from the habitation of thy holiness and of thy glory." 'Tis one of God's titles, "He that dwelleth in the heavens," Ps. 123:1; and part of his proper glory, that he dwelleth on high, Ps. 113:5, "Who is like unto the Lord our God, who dwelleth on high." Heaven is called God's place, Hos. 5:15, "I will go and return unto my place." When Enoch and Elijah were taken to God's blissful and glorious presence, they were taken into heaven; the way to heaven was the path of life to them, by which they escaped death.

As in the 16th Psalm, 'tis foretold that God would show the Messiah the way to his blissful and glorious presence, and his right hand, so Ps. 110[:1], it is said, God would bid him sit on his right hand; and in Zech. 6:13, 'tis represented that he should sit on God's throne. Now the Old Testament knows of but two things that are called the throne of God: the one is heaven, or his throne in heaven; and the other is the mercy seat over the ark in the holy of holies, in the temple and tabernacle. The former the Old Testament represents as the proper temple and tabernacle of God, and his original dwelling place, and the proper place of his throne. Heaven is called God's sanctuary, Ps. 33:13-14 and 102:19; and his tabernacle, Job 36:29; and his temple, Ps. 11:4; and here God's throne is said to be, Ps. 11:4, Ps. 103:19, Job 26:9; yea, heaven is said to be his throne, Is. 66:1. And the latter the Old Testament represents as called God's throne less properly, and more properly called his footstool. Lam. 2:1, "and remembered not his footstool in the day of his anger"; I Chron. 28:2, "As for me, I had it in my heart to build an house of rest for the ark of the covenant of the Lord, and for the footstool of our God, and had made ready for the building"; Ps. 132:7, "We will go into his tabernacles: we will worship at his footstool"; Is. 60:13, "to beautify the place of my sanctuary; and to make the place of my feet glorious." The inferior manner in which God dwelt in the Jewish sanctuary was expressed by that, that he placed his name there; and these are represented as only the outworks or gates of heaven, Gen. 28:17. Yea, though God is represented as dwelling in holy places on earth, yet he was so far from dwelling in them as he does in heaven, that when he appeared in them from time to time, he is represented as then coming from heaven to them, as though heaven were his fixed abode, and Mt. Sinai and the tabernacle and temple, places into which he would occasionally turn aside and appear. Thus God is said to have descended in a cloud and appear[ed] to Moses, when he passed by him and proclaimed his name, though he had before that from time to time appeared there in the mount of God, and though Moses at that time had long been conversing with God in the mount. The temple is so much less properly the place of God's abode than heaven, that the Wise Man, that built the temple, speaks of men's worshipping God in the temple: he at the same [time] advises those that worship him there to be sensible that God is in heaven, and they on the earth; they are on earth in the temple, but he would have them conceive of God as not where they are, but in heaven, Eccles. 5:1-2. So when God withdrew from the land of Israel to heaven, it is spoken of, Hos. 5:15, as God's going and returning to his place, as though the land of Israel were not properly his place.

Now when the prophecies speak of the Messiah's sitting down on God's throne at God's right hand, 'tis much most reasonable to understand this of God's higher throne, and that which the Old Testament represents as properly God's throne and dwelling place, and not that which it speaks of as more properly his footstool, for these reasons:

1. Because this is spoken of in these prophecies as the Messiah's glorious honor and exaltation. Although I have shown before that the prophecies do abundantly represent the Messiah as God, or a divine person:[^005-note53] but for God, in the person of the Son, to appear seated on that which the Old Testament represents as more properly God's footstool, would hardly be represented as so glorious an exaltation of him that was the Jehovah. God's being enthroned in heaven, is spoken of in the Old Testament as one thing wherein appears God's infinite highness and exaltation as the great king. Eccles. 5[:2], "God is in heaven, and thou on the earth"; Is. 57[:15], "thus saith the high and lofty One that inhabiteth eternity, [. . .] I dwell in the high and holy place"; Is. 33:5, "God is exalted; for he dwelleth on high"; Ps. 113:5, "Who is like unto the Lord our God, for he dwelleth on high." Though indeed God humbles himself to behold the things that are in earth,[^005-note54] yet 'tis a great manifestation of the high honor and glory of God in the sight of men, short-sighted worms, that he has his throne in heaven; and therefore this is spoken of as a thing wherein God will make the glorious honor and dignity of the Son of God to appear. But God's dwelling on the earth in the temple at Jerusalem, is spoken of as a thing wherein God indeed puts great honor on men, and shows his great mercy, but a thing wherein he most marvelously manifests his condescension. "Will God indeed dwell on the earth?" says Solomon [I Kgs. 8:27]; and in so doing, He is spoken of as bowing the heavens, and renting the heavens, etc. [Is. 64:1]. Therefore, 'tis not to be supposed that the great honor and glory in which Jehovah should appear in the person of the Messiah, should consist in his dwelling in a temple made by men's hands in Jerusalem, especially considering,

2. That the prophecies do everywhere represent as though God would glorify himself, and would in all respects appear in his majesty, honor, and infinite dignity and excellency, in the days of the Messiah, much more than he ever had done; that the glory of the Lord should be revealed, and all flesh should see it together; that men should behold it and marvel, God's enemies be astonished and tremble, and through fear submit themselves; and God's people should admire and praise him, and boast and say, This is our God; and that now God will be great and exalted, and take to himself his great power, etc. And one way in which the prophecies do represent God as being so greatly glorified in the days of the Messiah, when his name shall be excellent in all the earth, is that he will set his glory above the heavens. Compare Ps. 148:13 and Ps. 8:1. And the way that the prophecies speak of God's thus manifesting his glory, is in the person of the Messiah, who I have showed is that divine person that is spoken of as Jehovah reigning over his people, appearing in glory to defend them, etc. Now, therefore, can any imagine that the glorious enthronement of the Messiah spoken of, in God's throne at his right hand, wherein his great honor and glory shall be so highly manifested, God saying to him, "Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever," etc., is only Jehovah's being seated on that which in the Old Testament is represented not properly [as] the throne of God, but rather his footstool?

3. The Messiah's dominion is often represented in the prophecies as universal, extending over all nations of the world, and heaven is spoken of as the proper place of the throne of him who don't only rule over one nation, but all nations; and it is foretold that God in the days of the Messiah would appear as dwelling in heaven, because he would then appear ruling over all nations. Ps. 103:19, "The Lord hath prepared his throne in the heavens; and his kingdom ruleth over all," with v. 22, "Bless the Lord, all his works in all places of his dominion"; and Ps. 47:5-8; and Ps. 113:4-5, which I have shown is a prophecy of the times of the Messiah:[^005-note55] "The Lord is high above all nations, and his glory above the heavens. Who is like unto our Lord who dwelleth on high." And God's thus dwelling on high, is[^005-note56] spoken of as one thing wherein his exaltation shall appear in the days of the Messiah (here see note on Ps. 113 in "Harmony of the Old and New Testament," p. 167);[^005-note57] Is. 33:5, "The Lord is exalted; for he dwelleth on high: he hath filled Zion with judgment and righteousness."

4. 'Tis unreasonable to understand this throne of God, in which the Messiah should sit at God's right hand, to be the mercy seat in the temple at Jerusalem, because what has been already observed, §24, of what the prophecies do everywhere represent of the nature and extent of the Messiah's kingdom, do show that the temple at Jerusalem, and its worship and ordinances, should not be continued for God's people at that time; and how impossible it is that God's people of all nations should come to worship there after the manner of the Jews; and how inconsistent such a manner of worship is with many prophecies.

5. What puts the matter beyond all dispute, and do fully determine that this throne of God on which the Messiah shall sit and reign at God's right hand, is that which the Old Testament represents as God's proper throne in his true temple and proper dwelling place, and not that which the same scriptures represent as more improperly is so called, is this: that the prophecies do particularly declare that in the days of the Messiah, the temple made with men's hands shall no longer [be] God's dwelling place or rest, and the golden throne over the ark should no longer be accounted his throne, but that heaven then be should be looked on as his temple and throne. Is. 66:1, "Thus saith the Lord, The heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool: where is the house that ye build unto me? and where is the place of my rest?" And in Jer. 3:16-17, 'tis very plainly signified that, in the Messiah's days, there shall be no such throne of God as that in the temple over the ark; intimating that it should be so was not a constitution suited to the multitude of God's people, and largeness of his church at that time. "And it shall come to pass, when ye are multiplied and increased in the land, in those days, saith the Lord, they shall say no more, The ark of the covenant of the Lord: neither shall it come into mind; neither shall they remember it; neither shall they visit it; neither shall that be done any more. At that time they shall call Jerusalem the *throne* of the Lord," etc.

Again, the ascension of the Messiah into heaven is foretold, Ps. 68:18, "Thou hast ascended on high, thou hast led captivity captive: thou hast received gifts for men; yea, for the rebellious also, that the Lord God might dwell among them." That the Messiah is here spoken of, and that his ascension into heaven is here spoken of, see particularly proved, in the discourse of "The Prophecies of the Messiah," §58.[^005-note58]

The ascension of the Messiah is also foretold, Gen. 49:9, "from the prey, my son, art thou gone up"; and Ps. 47:5, "God is gone up with a shout, the Lord with the sound of a trumpet." Concerning both which places, see also the forementioned §58 of "Prophecies of the Messiah."

Again, the ascension of the Messiah from the earth into heaven at his exaltation, and in order to receive his glory and kingdom, [is foretold]. Dan. 7:13-14, "I saw in the night visions, and behold, one like the Son of man came with the clouds of heaven, and came to the Ancient of days, and they brought him near before him. And there was given [him] dominion, and glory, and a kingdom, that all people, nations, and languages should serve him," etc. His being here spoken of as the Son of man, implies that he is of this lower world, a native of the earth; but here he is manifested as coming to God in the clouds of heaven, and therefore this implies his ascending up from the earth to God in heaven. God, or the Ancient of days, in the 9th verse is represented as sitting on the throne of his glory, which throne is said to be "like the fiery flame," having thousand thousands ministering to him. This glorious throne, the scripture of the Old Testament everywhere tells us, is heaven. And the prophecies do particularly represent the place of God's throne as being in heaven in the Messiah's days, as in Is. 66:1 and Ps. 113:4-5, Is. 33:5, Ps. 148:13 and Is. 57:15. Ps. 47:5-8, "God is gone up with a shout, the Lord with the sound of a trumpet. Sing praises to God, sing praises unto our King, sing praises. For God is the King of all the earth: sing ye praises with understanding. God reigneth over the heathen: God sitteth on the throne of his holiness." But here [Dan. 7:13], this Son of man is represented as being brought from the earth, from amongst the sons of men, and being "brought near before him" (which is the same, in other words, with that in the 16th Psalm, his being brought into God's presence and to his right hand). The Messiah's ascending hither to that glorious throne of the Ancient of days, with clouds of heaven, implies that that throne was in heaven.

In the 118th Psalm, 19th verse, the Messiah is represented as having the gates of righteousness opened to him after his deliverance from his sufferings, that he may go in thereat; where there is doubtless an allusion to the gates of the temple. But it was before observed that the temple of God in the days of the Messiah is not any temple built by men, but heaven.[^005-note59]

In the 24th Psalm, it is foretold that the principal gates and chief doors of God's everlasting temple shall be opened to the Messiah when he comes from victory over his enemies, to reign on his throne as the king of glory; and that being perfectly pure and holy, he shall ascend into the hill of the Lord and stand in his holy place. But heaven is elsewhere represented as the hill or mountain of the Lord, and the mountain of his sanctuary, or holy place. Ps. 102:19, "For he hath looked down from the height of his sanctuary; from heaven did the Lord behold the earth." And this seems to be called "the mount of the congregation." Is. 14:13-14, "For thou hast said in thine heart, I will ascend into heaven, I will exalt my throne above the stars of God: I will also sit upon the mount of the congregation, in the sides of the north: I will ascend above the heights of the clouds; I will be like the most High." And from what has been before observed, it is apparent that there was to be no other mount or hill of God in the days of the Messiah. See the evidence more particularly that the Messiah is spoken of in this Psalm, and that this is a prophecy of his ascension into heaven, in the preceding discourse, §85 (see Bp. Kidder's *Demonstration*, Pt. 3, p. 51a).[^005-note60]

That honor which the king of Babylon, the city of the enemies of Zion, is represented in that 14th of Isaiah, [vv.] 13-14, just mentioned, as ambitious of--viz. of ascending into heaven and exalting his throne "above the stars of God," to sit in "the mount of the congregation," to "ascend above the heights of the clouds," and to be "like the most High"--it was the will of God should really be given to the Messiah, his own Son, the king of Zion, the city of God's people, that is a divine person and the man of God's right hand, God's firstborn, higher than the kings of the earth, to whom belonged divine honor, and to whom it was plainly promised that he should sit on God's throne at his right hand.

In Ezek. 17:22-24, 'tis promised that the Messiah after his humiliation, wherein he should be as a low and dry tree, and after his death, wherein he should be as a tender twig cropped off, God would plant him "upon an high mountain and eminent," even "the mountain of the height of Israel." This can be no other than the height of God's sanctuary, spoken of, Ps. 102:19, and the mountain of the congregation, spoken of, Is. 14:13; which, we are told, are heaven. As to Mt. Zion in the city of David, or the mountain of the temple, they neither of them were so superlatively high, as the double and treble expression, "*high* mountain and *eminent*," "the mountain of the height of Israel," signifies, according to the Hebrew idiom.

To the like purpose with the last mentioned text in Ezekiel and Ps. 24, is that in the 2nd Psalm, 6th verse, "Yet have I set my king on my holy hill of Zion"; which is represented as being done after the violent opposition he met with from men.

The supposition that the Messiah should ascend into heaven, is agreeable to what is said in Deuteronomy [34:10] of his being a prophet "like unto Moses," who ascended up into Mount Sinai to God on the behalf of the people, to transact affairs there with God on their[^005-note61] behalf; and there as it were dwelt with God, saw his glory, was admitted to God into his presence, where was great joy and glory, and conversed with him "face to face."

The ancient Jews, who gave the name of "the kingdom of heaven" to the kingdom of the Messiah, seemed to have understood that when God should appear to reign in this kingdom, he should have his throne in heaven, and that the Messiah should ascend into heaven and also have his throne there with God: and from thence to have given this kingdom the name of the kingdom of heaven, because it would be a kingdom wherein the heavens should rule, and the throne of the kingdom be in the heavens. They seem to have taken it from the 7th chapter of Daniel, where the throne of the Ancient of days, when he sets up his kingdom after the four monarchies, is represented as being in heaven, and the Messiah as coming there to him, to be with him, and reign with him there over all nations.[^005-note62]

Discussing Christ's covenant with God the Father to suffer and die, Charnock writes: "The ancient *Jews* had some prospect of this Covenant; one of their Writers saith, *God treated with the Messiah; Righteous Messiah, those who are hid with thee, are such whose sins in time shall bring thee into grief, they ears shall hear reproaches, thy tongue shall cleave to the roof of thy mouth, Thou shalt be wearied with sorrow. The Messiah answered, Lord of the World, I joyfully take them upon me, and charge my self with their torments; but upon this condition, that thou shalt quicken the dead in my dayes*: God, saith the *Rabbi*, *granted him this, and from that time the Messiah charged himself with all kinds of torments*; as it is written, *Isa. 53. He was afflicted*: So that the Death of Christ was not by a fortuitous rancounter of things, nor meerly by the violence of the *Jewish*rage, nor from any inability in his Father, or himself, to hinder so strange an event, but it was the issue of a previous agreement flowing from infinite Love, managed by incomparable Wisdom, disposing things to so great an end." Charnock cites "*R. Hadars*: Chan. in Gen. 1. 1. cited by *Mornai*, contra *Ieuss*, ch. 6, p. 163, &c."

[I Cor. 6:2-3]; "in heaven their angels do always behold the face of my Father which is in heaven" [Matt. 18:10]; "Are they not all ministering spirits, sent forth to minister to them that shall be the heirs of salvation?" [Heb. 1:14]; with many others of like import.

In the time of God's setting up his kingdom on earth, reigning in the greatest displays of his glory and mercy, as king over all nations (vv. 3-4), and in the most excellent state of things on earth, in mercy to the miserable, afflicted and poor in spirit, and in making the Gentiles to become his church, and (v. 9) and exalting his church to its highest honor on earth: I say, in such a state of things does it especially become God to have his throne in heaven, yea, above all heaven, and not in any temple on earth, and so set his throne in heaven by an ascension or a new exaltation thither." For note on vv. 5-6, see WJE 24:528-29, and on v. 6, Poole, *Synopsis Criticorum*, *3*, col. 954.

Kidder is considering an objection against Christ's words in Mark 10:19. Responding to the man who asked what he needed to do to inherit eternal life, Christ only cited some precepts of godly behavior. But this, Kidder rejoins, does not mean Christ did not mean the other precepts should be forgotten. On p. 51, Kidder adds: "That Jesus is not blameable upon this account, because he may be justified from the *old Testament* in this method. Lord, *who shall abide in thy tabernacle? And who shall dwell in thy holy hill?* (Psal. xv. 1.) This is a question very like to that of the man in the gospel. *David Kimchi* tells us, that by the *tabernacle* and the *holy hill* heaven is meant, the state of recompence and bliss to righteous souls." Kidder cites "R. D. Kimchi *in* Ps. xv. mon. *Nizke Mammon*. c. 5."

type="miscellany">

## "The Fulfillment of the Prophecies of the Messiah" Miscellanies no. 1068.3 Misc. 1068.3 (ed.)

Jonathan Edwards95.[^006-note1] It was foretold that the devil should be remarkably conquered, confounded and punished by the Messiah. Gen. 3:15, "And I will put enmity between thee and the woman, and between thy seed and her seed; it shall bruise thy head, and thou shalt bruise his heel"; Is. 27:1, "In that day the Lord with his sore and great and strong sword shall punish leviathan the piercing serpent, even leviathan that crooked serpent; and he shall slay the dragon that is in the sea"; Zech. 3:1-2, etc., "And he showed me Joshua the high priest standing before the angel of the Lord, and Satan standing at his right hand to resist him. And the Lord said unto Satan, The Lord rebuke thee, O Satan, even the Lord that hath chosen Jerusalem rebuke thee," taken together with what follows in that chapter, which shows how Satan is to be confounded in his attempts, viz. by setting a fair mitre on Joshua's or Jesus' head, and bringing forth God's servant the Branch, etc.; Is. 65:25, "The wolf and the lamb shall feed together, and the lion shall eat straw like the bullock: and dust shall be the serpent's meat. They shall not hurt nor destroy in all my holy mountain," compared with Is. 11:1, 9.

96.[^006-note2] It is foretold that the inhabitants of heaven should behold admire and praise the wonders of God's works of mercy and redemption by the Messiah. Ps. 89:1-5, "I will sing of the mercies of the Lord . . . For I have said, Mercy shall be built up for ever: . . . I have made a covenant with my chosen, I have sworn unto David my servant. Thy seed will I establish for ever, and build up thy throne to all generations. And the heavens shall praise thy wonders, O Lord: thy faithfulness also in the congregation of the saints."

97.[^006-note3] It was foretold that the Messiah should be a mediator to stand between an infinitely holy, great and dreadful God, and weak, feeble, sinful men, to screen them from the fire of God's wrath and to be a medium of their intercourse with a Being so much above them, and [of] such majesty, purity and justice that they were not fit to have access to and communication with immediately. For the children of Israel's expressing their sense of their need of such a mediator, and medium of their access to God, and free and friendly intercourse with him, is mentioned as the very occasion of the great promise given of the Messiah at Mt. Sinai. Deut. 18:15-18, "The Lord thy God will raise thee up a Prophet from the midst of thee, of thy brethren, like unto me; unto him ye shall hearken; according to all that thou desiredst of the Lord thy God in Horeb in the day of the assembly, saying, Let me not hear again the voice of the Lord my God, neither let me see this great fire any more, that I die not. And the Lord said unto me, They have well spoken that which they have spoken" (i. e. "They have said it with good reason: they indeed stand in need of a mediator; they are not fit for immediate access"). "I will raise 'em up a Prophet from among their brethren, like unto thee, and I will put my words in his mouth; and he shall speak unto them all that I command him." 'Tis promised that he should be from the midst of them, of their brethren, intimating that he shall be such an one as they need, in a nature not so much above them, not clothed with such terrible majesty, but one that they might have free access to without fear.

98.[^006-note4] As the apostle Paul in his epistle to the Hebrews represents that Jesus Christ in this differs from the high priests of old, that whereas they used to enter into the holiest of all but once a year to appear before the throne of God, Jesus Christ was a high priest that entered into the holy of holies to sit down on the throne of God on God's right hand, there to remain continually as a royal priest; so in exact agreement with this is that prophecy in Zech. 6:13, "Even he shall build the temple of the Lord; and he shall bear the glory, and shall sit and rule upon his throne" (i. e. upon God's throne); "and he shall be a priest upon his throne."

99.[^006-note5] As the prophecies represent the Messiah as a priest to offer sacrifice for his people, so they represent him as an intercessor for them. Is. 53:12, "he bare the sin of many, and made intercession for the transgressors"; Zech. 1:12, "Then the angel of the Lord answered and said, O Lord of hosts, how long wilt thou not have mercy on Jerusalem and on the cities of Judah, against which thou hast had indignation this threescore and ten years?" This that is here called the angel of the Lord, seems to be the same that is elsewhere so called in the Old Testament, which I have showed is the same person with the Messiah, in §13 of this discourse.[^006-note6]

100.[^006-note7] It was foretold that the enlargement and advancement of the kingdom of the Messiah, and the increase of the number of his subjects, and his success in the actual salvation of his people, and their prosperity and joy and triumph over their enemies, should be chiefly after the Messiah's ascension into heaven, and dependent upon it. Ezek. 17:22-23, "I will also take of the highest branch of the high cedar, and will set it; I will crop off from the top of his young twigs a tender one, and will plant it in an high mountain and eminent: in the mountain of the height of Israel will I plant it; and it shall bring forth boughs, and bear fruit, and be a goodly cedar: and under it shall dwell all fowl of every wing; in the shadow of the branches thereof shall they dwell"; Ps. 2:6, etc., "I have set my king on my holy hill of Zion. . . . Ask of me, and I will give thee the heathen," etc.; Ps. 110[:1-6], "Sit thou on my right hand, until I make thine enemies thy footstool. The Lord shall send the rod of thy strength out of Zion: rule thou in the midst of thine enemies. Thy people shall be willing in the day of thy power, in the beauty of holiness from the womb of the morning: thou hast the dew of thy youth. . . . The Lord at thy right hand shall strike through kings . . . He shall judge among the heathen, . . . he shall wound the heads over many countries"; Ps. 68, throughout, observing the connection of the rest of the Psalm with vv. 4 and 18 and 33; and the whole Ps. 47, observing the connection of the rest of the Psalm with v. 5; Ps. 108:5 to the end, "Be thou exalted, O God, above the heavens: and thy glory above all the earth; that thy beloved may be delivered: . . . God hath spoken in his holiness; I will rejoice, I will divide Shechem"; Ps. 7:7-8, "So shall the congregation of the people compass thee about: for their sakes therefore return thou on high. The Lord shall judge the people," etc.; and Ps. 8:1-2, "O Lord, our Lord, how excellent is thy name in all the earth! who hast set thy glory above the heavens. Out of the mouth of babes and sucklings thou hast ordained strength," etc.; which places in the 7th and 8th Psalms, so agreeable to what we have in the 47th and 68th and 108th Psalms, there is all reason to suppose have respect to the same thing; Zech. 6:13-15, "he shall sit and rule upon his throne; and he shall be a priest upon his throne: . . . And the crowns shall be to Helem, and to Tobijah, and to Jedaiah, and to Hen . . . And they that are far off shall come and build in the temple of the Lord"; Dan. 7:13-14, "I saw in the night visions, and behold one like the Son of man came with the clouds of heaven, and came to the Ancient of days, and they brought him near before him. And there was given him dominion, and glory, and a kingdom, that all people, nations, and languages, should serve him"; Ps. 118:18 to the end, "Open to me the gates of righteousness: I will go in to them, and praise the Lord: the gate of the Lord, into which the righteous shall enter. . . . This is the day which the Lord hath made; we will be glad and rejoice in it. Save now, I beseech thee, O Lord: I beseech thee, send now prosperity."

101.[^006-note8] It was foretold that in the times of the Messiah's kingdom, not the being of any particular nation would qualify persons to be received as some of the people of God, and give them a title to the privileges and blessings of the Messiah's kingdom; but that those that were righteous and holy only should be accounted the Messiah's people, or admitted to the privileges of such. Ps. 50:5, "Gather my saints together; those that have made a covenant with me by sacrifice"; v. 7, "Hear, O my people, and I will speak; O Israel, and I will testify against thee: I am God, even thy God"; v. 16, "But unto the wicked God saith, What hast thou to do to declare my statutes, or that thou shouldst take my covenant in thy mouth?"; v. 23, "Whoso offereth praise glorifieth God: and to him that ordereth his conversation aright will I show the salvation of God"; Ps. 24:3-6, "Who shall ascend into the hill of the Lord? and who shall stand in his holy place? He that hath clean hands, and a pure heart; . . . He shall receive the blessing from the Lord, and righteousness from the God of his salvation. This is the generation of them that seek him, that seek thy face, O Jacob"; Is. 26:2, "Open ye the gates, that the righteous nation that keepeth the truth may enter in"; Is. 33:14-17, "The sinners in Zion" (i.e the sinners in the nation of Israel that pretended to be all God's people) "are afraid; fearfulness hath surprized the hypocrites. Who among us shall dwell with the devouring fire? who among us shall dwell with everlasting burnings?" (signifying that God will entirely reject them though they dwell in Zion). "He that walketh righteously, and speaketh uprightly; he that despiseth the gain of oppressions, and shaketh his hands from holding of bribes, that stoppeth his ears from hearing of blood, and shutteth his eyes from seeing evil. He shall dwell on high: his place of defense shall be the munitions of rocks" (he shall continue to dwell on Mt. Zion, that was an high and strong place defended with munitions of rocks): "bread shall be given him; his water shall be sure. Thine eyes shall see the king in his beauty: they shall behold the land that is very far off"; v. 20, "Look upon Zion, the city of our solemnities: thine eyes shall see Jerusalem a quiet habitation," etc.; v. 22, "the Lord is our judge, the Lord is our lawgiver, the Lord is our king; he will save us"; Mal. 3:16-18, "Then they that feared the Lord spake often one to another: . . . And they shall be mine [. . .] in the day that I make up my jewels; . . . Then shall ye return, and discern between the righteous and the wicked, between him that serveth God and him that serveth him not"; ch. 4:1-3, "For, behold, the day cometh that shall burn as an oven; and all the proud, yea, all that do wickedly, shall be as stubble: . . . But unto you that fear my name shall the Sun of righteousness arise with with healing in his wings; . . . And ye shall tread down the wicked"; Is. 52:1, "put on thy beautiful garments, O Jerusalem, for henceforth there shall no more come into thee the uncircumcised and the unclean"; Ezek. 44:6-7, "O ye house of Israel, let it suffice you of all your abominations. In that ye have brought into my sanctuary strangers, uncircumcised in heart, and uncircumcised in flesh, to be in my sanctuary, to pollute it"; with v. 9, "Thus saith the Lord; No stranger, uncircumcised in heart, nor uncircumcised in flesh, shall enter into my sanctuary, of any stranger that is among the children of Israel"; Ps. 45:4, "ride prosperously because of truth, meekness and righteousness"; Joel 3:17, "So shall ye know that I am the Lord your God dwelling in Zion, my holy mountain: then shall Jerusalem be holy, and there shall no stranger pass through her any more"; Zech. 14:21, "in that day there shall be no more the Canaanite in the house of the Lord of hosts"; Ps. 69:32, "The humble shall see this, and be glad: and your heart shall live that seek God"; with vv. 35-36, "For God will save Zion, and will build the cities of Judah: that they may dwell there, and have it in possession. The seed also of his servants shall inherit it: and they that love his name shall dwell therein"; Ps. 22:22-23, "I will declare thy name unto my brethren: in the midst of the congregation will I praise thee. Ye that fear the Lord, praise him; all ye seed of Jacob, glorify him; and fear him, all ye seed of Israel"; with vv. 25-26, "My praise shall be of thee in the great congregation: I will pay my vows before them that fear him. The meek shall eat and be satisfied: they shall praise the Lord that seek him"; v. 30, "A seed shall serve him; it shall be accounted to the Lord for a generation"; Is. 65:8-11, "Thus saith the Lord, As the new wine is found in the cluster, and one saith, Destroy it not; for a blessing is in it: so will I do for my servants' sakes, that I may not destroy them all. And I will bring forth a seed out of Jacob, and out of Judah an inheritor of my mountains: and mine elect shall inherit it, and my servants shall dwell there. And Sharon shall be a fold of flocks, and the valley of Achor a place for the herds to lie down in, for my people that have sought me. But ye are they that forsake the Lord"; vv. 13-15, "Therefore thus saith the Lord God, Behold, my servants shall eat, but ye shall be hungry" (speaking of the wicked among the Jews, as appears by the preceding part of the chapter): "behold, my servants shall drink, but ye shall [be] thirsty: behold, my servants shall rejoice, but ye shall be ashamed: behold, my servants shall sing for joy of heart, but ye shall cry for sorrow of heart, and shall howl for vexation of spirit. And ye shall have your name for a curse unto my chosen"; vv. 18-19, "for, behold, I create Jerusalem a rejoicing, and her people a joy. And I will rejoice in Jerusalem, and joy in my people"; vv. 22-23, "for as the days of a tree are the days of my people, mine elect shall long enjoy the work of their hands. . . . they shall be called the seed[^006-note9] of the blessed of the Lord, and their offspring with them"; ch. 66:5, "Hear ye the word of the Lord, ye that tremble at his word; Your brethren that hated you, and cast you out for my name's sake, said, Let the Lord be glorified: but he shall appear to your joy, and they shall be ashamed"; v. 14, "and the hand of the Lord shall be known towards his servants, and his indignation towards his enemies"; Dan. 7:18, "But the saints of the most High shall take the kingdom"; v. 22, "and the time came that the saints possessed the kingdom"; v. 27, "And the kingdom," etc., "shall be given to the people of the saints of the most High"; Is. 8:15-16, "And many among them shall stumble, and fall, and be broken, and snared, and taken. Bind up the testimony, and seal the law among my disciples"; Is. 35:8, "And an highway shall be there, and a way, and it shall be called, The way of holiness; the unclean shall not pass over it"; with v. 10, "And the ransomed of the Lord shall return, and come to Zion," etc.; Is. 4:3-4, "And it shall come to pass, that he that is left in Zion, and he that remaineth in Jerusalem, shall be called holy, even every one that is written among the living in Jerusalem: when the Lord shall have washed away the filth of the daughters of Zion, and shall have purged the blood of Jerusalem from the midst thereof by the spirit of judgment, and by the spirit of burning"; Ezek. 20:38, "And I will purge out from among you the rebels, and them that transgress against me: I will bring them forth out of the country where they sojourn, and they shall not enter into the land of Israel: and ye shall know that I am the Lord"; Ps. 85:8-9, "I will hear what God the Lord will speak: for he will speak peace unto his people, and to his saints: but let them not turn again to folly. Surely his salvation is nigh them that fear him; that glory may dwell in our land"; Ps. 118:20, "This gate of the Lord, into which the righteous shall enter"; Is. 57:13, "When thou criest, let thy companies deliver thee; but the wind shall carry them all away; but he that putteth his trust in me shall possess the land, and shall inherit my holy mountain." 'Tis probable that some respect is had to this state of things in the days of the Messiah, when the righteous only should be reckoned of God's Israel, in Ps. 37:9-11, "For evildoers shall be cut off: but those that wait upon the Lord, shall inherit the earth. For yet a little while, and the wicked shall not be: yea, thou shalt diligently consider his place, and it shall not be. But the meek shall inherit the earth: and delight themselves in the abundance of peace"; and v. 29, "The righteous shall inherit the land, and dwell therein for ever"; Is. 59:20, "And the Redeemer shall come to Zion, and unto them that turn from transgression in Jacob."

102.[^006-note10] As it was often foretold that in the times of the Messiah's kingdom, those only that should be holy and righteous of the nation of Israel should be accounted the people of God, and admitted to the privileges of the Messiah's kingdom; so it was foretold that all that were righteous, of whatever nation they were, should be accepted as God's people and admitted to those privileges. Is. 56:6-8, "Also the sons of the stranger, that join themselves to the Lord, to serve him, and to love the name of the Lord, to be his servants, every one that keepeth the sabbath from polluting it, and taketh hold of my covenant; even them will I bring to my holy mountain, and make them joyful in my house of prayer: their[^006-note11] burnt offerings and their sacrifices shall be accepted on mine altar; for my house shall be called an house of prayer for all people. The Lord which gathereth the outcasts of Israel saith, Yet will I gather others to him, besides those that are gathered unto him"; Ps. 24:1-6, "The earth is the Lord's, and the fullness thereof; the world, and they that dwell therein. For he hath founded it . . . Who shall ascend into the hill of the Lord? . . . He that hath clean hands, and a pure heart; . . . He shall receive the blessing from the Lord, and righteousness from the God of his salvation. This is the generation of them that seek him"; Ps. 145:9-12, "The Lord is good to all: and his tender mercies are over all his works. All thy works shall praise thee, O Lord; and thy *saints* shall bless thee. They shall speak of the glory of thy kingdom, and talk of thy power; to make known to the *sons of men* his mighty acts, and the glorious majesty of his kingdom"; and vv. 18-20, "The Lord is nigh unto all them that call upon him, to all that call upon him in truth. He will fulfill the desire of them that fear him: he will also hear their cry, and will save them. The Lord preserveth all them that love him: but all the wicked will he destroy."

See this more abundantly manifested hereafter in future sections concerning the calling of the Gentiles, and their being admitted to the same privileges with the Jews.[^006-note12]

103. They represent faith, or a spiritual coming to the Messiah, and looking to him and trusting in him, and in God through him, as that which will give persons an interest in him and the benefits of his kingdom. Is. 28:15-17, "Because ye have said, . . . we have made lies our refuge, and under falsehood have we hid ourselves: therefore thus saith the Lord God, Behold, I lay in Zion for a foundation a stone, a tried stone, a precious corner stone, a sure foundation: he that believeth shall not make haste. Judgment also will I lay to the line, . . . and the hail shall sweep away the refuge of lies, and the waters shall overflow the hiding place"; Is. 27:4-5, "who would set the briers and thorns against me in battle? I would go through them, I would burn them together. Or let him take hold of my strength, that he may make peace with me; and he shall make peace with me"; Jer. 3:19, "But I said, How shall I put thee among the children, and give thee a pleasant land, a goodly heritage of the host of nations? And I said, Thou shalt call me, My father; and shalt not turn away from me"; with vv. 22-23, "Return, ye backsliding children, and I will heal your backslidings. Behold, we come unto thee; for thou art the Lord our God. Truly in vain is salvation hoped for from the hills, and from the multitude of mountains: truly in the Lord our God is the salvation of Israel"; Is. 45:22, "Look to me, and be ye saved, all the ends of the earth," and vv. 24-25, "Surely, shall one say, in the Lord have I righteousness and strength: even to him shall men come; . . . In the Lord shall all the seed of Israel be justified, and shall glory"; Is. 55:1-3, "Ho, every one that thirsteth, come ye to the waters, and he that hath no money; come ye, buy, and eat; yea, come, buy wine and milk without money and without price. . . . hearken diligently unto me, and eat ye that which is good, and let your soul delight itself in fatness. Incline your ear, and come to me: hear, and your soul shall live ;and I will make an everlasting covenant with you, even the sure mercies of David"; Is. 40:31, "But they that wait upon the Lord shall renew their strength; they shall mount up with wings as eagles; they shall run, and not be weary; they shall walk, and not faint"; Is. 56:4-5, "For thus saith the Lord unto the eunuchs that . . . take hold of my covenant; even to them will I give in mine house . . . a place and a name"; and vv. 6-7, "Also the sons of the stranger, that join themselves to the Lord, . . . and take hold of my covenant; even them will I bring to my holy mountain"; Is. 57:13, "When thou criest, let thy companies deliver thee; but the wind shall carry them all away; vanity shall take them: but he that putteth his trust in me shall possess the land, and inherit my holy mountain"; Is. 60:9, "Surely the isles shall wait for me, and the ships of Tarshish first"; Is. 51:5, "My righteousness is near; my salvation is gone forth, and mine arms shall judge the people; The isles shall wait upon me, and on mine arm shall they trust"; Zech. 9:9-12, "behold, thy King cometh unto thee: he is just, and having salvation; . . . his dominion shall be from sea to sea, . . . As for thee also, by the blood of thy covenant I have sent thy prisoners out of the pit wherein there is no water. *Turn ye to the strong hold, ye prisoners of hope*"; Is. 26:1-4, "In that day shall this song be sung; We have a strong city; salvation will God appoint for walls and for bulwarks. Open ye the gates, that the righteous nation which keepeth the truth may enter in. Thou wilt keep him in perfect peace, whose mind is stayed on thee: because he trusteth in thee. Trust in the Lord for ever: for in the Lord Jehovah is the rock of ages" (as it is in the Hebrew);[^006-note13] Hos. 14:1-3, "O Israel, return unto the Lord thy God; for thou hast fallen by thine iniquity. Take with you words, and turn to the Lord: say unto him, Take away all iniquity, and receive us graciously: . . . Asshur shall not save us; we will not ride on horses: neither will we say any more to the work of our hands, Ye are our gods: for in thee the fatherless findeth mercy."

104.[^006-note14] The prophecies do represent repentance as the special condition of remission of sins in the days of the Messiah's kingdom. Jer. 3:12-13, "Go and proclaim these words towards the north, and say, Return thou backsliding Israel, saith the Lord; and I will not cause mine anger to fall upon you: for I am merciful, saith the Lord, and I will not keep anger for ever. Only acknowledge thine iniquity, that thou hast transgressed against the Lord thy God, and hast scattered thy ways to the strangers under every green tree, and ye have not obeyed any voice, saith the Lord"; vv. 21-22, "A voice was heard upon the high places, weeping and supplication of the children of Israel: for they have perverted their way, and have forgotten the Lord their God. Return, ye backsliding children, and I will heal your backslidings"; vv. 24-25, "For shame hath devoured the labor of our fathers from our youth: their flocks and their herds, their sons and their daughters. We lie down [in] our shame, and confusion covereth us: for we have sinned against the Lord our God, we and our fathers, from our youth even to this day, and have not obeyed the voice of the Lord our God"; Jer. 31:9, "They shall come with weeping, and with supplications will I lead them"; [vv.] 18-20, "I have surely heard Ephraim bemoaning himself thus; Thou hast chastized me, and I was chastized, as a bullock unaccustomed to the yoke: turn thou me, and I shall be turned; for thou art the Lord my God. Surely after that I was turned, I repented; and after that I was instructed, I smote upon my thigh: I was ashamed, yea, even confounded, because I did bear the reproach of my youth. Is Ephraim my dear son? is he a pleasant child? for since I spake against him, I do earnestly remember him still: therefore my bowels are troubled for him; I will surely have mercy upon him, saith the Lord"; Is. 55:7, "Let the wicked forsake his way, and the unrighteous man his thoughts: and let [him] return unto the Lord, and he will have mercy upon him; and to our God, for he will abundantly pardon," with the context; Is. 59:20, "And the Redeemer shall come to Zion, and to them that turn from transgression in Jacob, saith the Lord"; Ezek. 16:61-63, "Then thou shalt remember thy ways, and be ashamed, when thou shalt receive thy sisters, . . . And I will establish my covenant with thee; and thou shalt know that I am the Lord: that thou mayst remember, and be confounded, and never open thy mouth any more because of thy shame, when I am pacified toward thee for all that thou hast done, saith the Lord God"; Ezek. 20:43-44,[^006-note15] "And there shall ye remember your ways, and all your doings, wherein ye have been defiled; and shall loath yourselves in your own sight for all your evils that ye have committed. And ye shall know that I am the Lord, when I have wrought with you for my name's sake, not according to your wicked ways, nor according to your corrupt doings, saith the Lord God"; Ezek. 36:31-33, "Then shall ye remember your evil ways, and your doings that were not good, and shall loath yourselves in your own sight for your iniquities and for your abominations. Not for yourselves do I this, saith the Lord God, be it known unto you: be ashamed and confounded for your own ways, O house of Israel.
